# Chapter Summaries

## Chapter 0141 — The Day After

**The seventh of the eighth tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, day fifty.** Nobody drowned and nobody was hurt and a carter said to a second carter that it was a good seventh, and that was the whole of what was said about it for the better part of two days. **Halse Tarrant, fifty-eight, thirty-one years on the wall and one of the hands that laid it, goes out along the top of it at the turn and walks it, and says the whole of it in the trade's mouth and it is all true:** the harm is the gravel and not the hole, the wall is not holding the sea up but two feet of gravel sideways against an ebb, a breach is a hole in a gravel bank with a wall in front of it, **the guild was right and he wants that said by him and in the open and not once in the next year by anybody else**, and take that wall out and you have got no north end in three winters. **And then the part nobody wants: a breach in the foot of a wall is not a morning's work with a cart.** He has a lead line the guild put there nineteen years ago and a boat of his own and he can sound to a foot inside of forty yards — three of the four things, and he has had three of them since about the sixth year. **The fourth is a day, and the day is not his, and that is not even the thing stopping him:** the hole is in nine foot of water and there is nothing with wheels on within forty yards of it, and on a fourth, a sixth or a seventh the wall stands up out of the sand and you can walk a flat boat over the top of it — which is not the answer, because the ebb on those three days comes out of that deep sideways, and if you are on the seaward face when it starts you are on the wrong side of the wall from the only water there is to get off on, and the hole holds about eleven times in twenty and the nine is a man in nine foot. **So it wants somebody who has done it, there is exactly one person on this coast who has done it, she is not in this lane, and nobody has been to ask her, because a man who keeps a wall does not go and ask a person for a boat.**

**And Ilyan's first ask on this coast, out loud, in the open, at the head of the slip, in front of about nine people, in about four seconds, and it is not a speech:** *Would you like to be the one standing there.* **Sorrel Gannock, thirty-six, puts the warp down and says yes, and says she has been waiting about two days for somebody to ask her and is not going to pretend she has not.** Then she tells him what it costs, and the finding of the chapter is in it: **a name at the bottom of a thing is not a wage and does not pay for oil; what it does is make a person able to point at her and say *that one, she was the one*, and she would rather have it on the day she chose it than have it put there by a man who needed a boat.** And she says yes **to the asking and not to being sent for**, and tells him the next thing will have to be a thing she is asked and not a thing she is given, and she does not know what it is yet.

**Pell Cawley, fifty, thirty-one years at that hard, hears the whole of it and says nothing, because it is not his wall and not his boat.** A man of thirty stands at the near end in the dark at the ninth bell having asked somebody a question out loud for the first time in fifty days, and been told yes, and he has two more to ask and no idea what they will be.

## Chapter 0142 — Three Places

**The first of the ninth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, day fifty-one.** Three places in one day: a yard at the north end, a shed two miles along the coast, a room a mile and a flight of stairs up the hill. **Not one of the three can see the other two, and that is the shape of it and he had known the shape of it for a week and had not been anywhere.**

**Sorrel Gannock says what she is holding and it cannot be written down.** Twice yesterday and once this morning, and no number. She can tell you where that deep is, not in words: everybody who has ever tried has written down where it *was*, which is a different thing and no use to anybody in a boat. **You cannot have it except by being in a boat with her on a day when the sea is over the crown, and you cannot have it on a good day, because on a good day it is nine foot of green water over the whole of it.** And it can only be put down in a yard, standing up, with two men on a net forty feet off hearing it, because there is no room on this coast a person like her can stand in, and **she is not going to be produced.** She tells him to come back for the next thing and not in a hurry, and says the other two have never been asked for anything and that he is the first person to ask either of them, and that is not nothing and is not a reason to hurry.

**Hollis Thrale, sixty-one, deaf on the right — the ear is his father's and his father's father's, and the kiln at the end wall was put up by a grandfather who could only work one-handed — has a pane on his own wall, three years old, that the guild has never seen.** He says the third and the eighth columns out loud in four minutes, in his own mouth, and would not have them read off the wall by the only other person in that shed who can read them, because a man who has had a number in his head for two years ought to be able to say it standing up. **The third gives the outer end of the wall as a place, two hundred and sixty-one yards from the post at the inner end, and after the figure a height, which is the top of the inner end where the wall is nine courses and about six foot. The eighth gives the toe: gravel standing to the second course along the whole of it and to the fifth at the inner end. The guild's third says two hundred and sixty-four; the guild's eighth says the fourth course for the whole length and nothing whatever about the inner end.** He has had both figures for two years and **cannot get them into a room, and the reason is not money and not spite: the only instrument anybody on this coast will act on is on the north wall of a room a mile up the hill and has not been out of that room in nineteen years, and the person who has it is right about the sea.**

**Bettany Rook, fifty-one, at a table with her hood up and a clerk of twenty-two beside her.** She had sent for nobody; nothing on that coast would have got a message up that hill except a cart and a man with a reason. She says she can tell which two places he has been from the dust on his coat. Then the whole of it, standing up: two panes in oak frames on the north wall, the older nineteen years and eight months old and the only legible copy of the charter on four hundred miles, made by a woman called Mare who is dead and whose name is inside the lid of the batch book two miles along the coast; the newer about three months old and three months of a woman of twenty-four who is paid nothing. **A record of a moving thing is a working instrument and instruments do not travel, and the guild said that to a man in a lane in four lines last week and every part of it was right.** She has a table of tides she can read out to a man on a bar day in ninety seconds and has never been wrong about that water. **And the reason she is not going to go and get the figure about the wall: the way of getting a number out of a person on this coast is a boy up a lane, and she has watched what that does to a boy about four times in nineteen years and has never once done it herself and is not starting on a sixth evening with a stranger watching her not do it.** She says the whole of what she is holding is worth exactly nothing and tells him so, so that he does not go and find out.

**And the chapter's finding is structural and is not a document:** three people can each say a true thing and the three true things cannot be in one place, and the reason they cannot is a custom about four hundred years old and a room with a lock on it and a year of somebody's back. **And a fourth road went up past the north end to a hut four miles above it where a woman of eighty-one is knitting with a boy of eight on a board, and he worked out on the seventh where he was going and he is not going up that one, and no person on this coast knows he left it off, and he is not going to tell anybody either.**

## Chapter 0143 — The Refusal

**The second of the ninth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, day fifty-two. THE BATCH'S SPINE.** In the lane at the head of the flat at the third bell, with the chain up, a cart of deals waiting and about nine people within twenty feet, **Hal Bevan, fifty-four, nineteen years on that chain, says the thing out loud.** He has never in nineteen years stood in that lane and asked the flat anything. **A claim has got somebody standing behind it; you cannot have a claim with nobody's name at the end of it, and that is not him being careful, that is what a claim *is*.** Not the guild, which will not and is right. Not a man in a good coat from a city four hundred miles behind, who has no standing on this road and is right about that. Not the man who keeps the wall, because he keeps it and is owed by it and has been waiting nineteen years for somebody to ask him for a day and nobody did. **The people who go over it. So he is asking the flat, in the open, and wants an answer in the open, and has never done this in nineteen years and it is not going to feel like anything.**

**Nobody objected. And a blank is not a no and a blank is not a yes** — there is a clerk two hundred miles up this coast reading a line with a wage on it and nobody's name on it, and there are two things he can do and neither is any use to anybody, and this quarter he chose the one that writes nothing.

**And Corrie Tallow, forty-eight, four years at that stall, forty yards off under her awning because a sentence like that carries about four times further than a man thinks, says no.** And is right. **A party is not a silence.** A party is a person who has said something out loud that they are then standing behind, and if you have not said it you have not got it. **Nowhere on four hundred miles of this coast does one person answer for another person about a crossing; that is about four hundred years old and it is in nobody's book, everybody in that lane has it filed as a rule about who signs what, and it is not a rule about signing — it is the shape of a person not being used by somebody else's pen, and it is why a crossing on this coast counts a person and not a cart.** You cannot get that out of a silence, or out of nine people, or out of a body, **and if the flat stands behind a claim about that wall then what stands behind it is a body, and there is not one thing on this coast a body can stand behind.**

**And then the part that is about the wall and not about her: if the flat is not a party, the claim has to be carried by people who were not on the flat, and there are three of them, and the only things on this coast a claim can go into are glass and paper** — the glass is a year and a fire and eleven months of a woman of twenty-four who is paid nothing and has not been asked, and the paper is a locked room a mile up this hill where two panes have hung in oak frames for nineteen years and have not been out of that room once in nineteen years. **And the answer being no does not stop the water, and she wants that said in the same breath, because she has watched a thing get refused once on this coast and is not going to be the second thing that gets refused and then gets pointed at as though the refusing had done something.**

**THE COST, AND IT IS NOT THE PERSON WHO REFUSED.** A refusal that costs nobody anything is a performance. It has cost **a day** — a man's own day, a cart, a pair of beasts, an afternoon at a landing that somebody else was going to lose — **and it has not cost it yet.** The only way anybody finds out anything about that wall is a lead line, a boat, a man who can sound and a day; three of those are in a shed and in a boat and in a man of fifty-eight, and the fourth is not for sale. **Cleve Gannock gave one on the fifth of the eighth tide, four days ago; it cost him a cart, a pair of beasts and his own afternoon, he was two hours late and told the man there it was the beasts, nobody thanked him, and he said *you are not going to ask me* before anybody had asked him anything.** He gave it once. He is not going to give it twice and he is right. **Nobody has asked him for a second one, nobody is going to, and that is the cost: that wall is not going to be sounded again by anybody this season, and that is the whole of what the no has bought, and it bought it for free — and she would like somebody to write down that it was free, because in about six weeks one of them is going to be standing in that lane telling somebody it was the worst thing anybody ever did here.**

**Jory Vance, twenty-two, four years at that counter, makes the day-book entry standing up at the chain in the ordinary way. There is no name at the end of any line of it and there is never going to be one, and the chapter says why:** a name at the end of that line would be a person standing behind it, and a person who has said nothing out loud is not standing behind anything, and a clerk in a building two hundred miles up this coast would read a name there and enter it and go on down the page, and in about a year somebody would go looking for the minute that made this and would not be able to find the person.

**And the chapter closes on him working out, standing in the cold, that the thing that has just been refused was the only door on this coast ever built for a thing that was not the water, and that it was built by nobody and shut by a woman of forty-eight who sells shingle and gets four shillings and twopence a day and is owed nothing, and that it was correct.** He has five pages in a penny exercise book and no sixth. **Sera Quill's one sheet is in the back of it with two questions on it, one of them about consent, and it gave him the name of the thing a long way before the thing came up this coast, and a name is not the thing, and it had not helped.** And a claim about eleven yards of the middle of a wall went forward up this coast with nobody's name at the end of any line of it, and it is going to be carried by three people who were not on that flat, and not one of them has been asked.

## Chapter 0144 — Eleven Months

**The third of the ninth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, day fifty-three. Thornglass.** Two miles up the coast road at the seventh bell, an hour and a quarter, nobody told he had come and nobody met him on the road. Under the batch book on the bench is a sheet of the guild's own season paper with a clerk's date on it and nothing under the date, and it will be under that book at the turn. The second fire was let in at the second bell and a woman of twenty-four has been standing at it since the bell, because once a second fire is let in there is nobody who can walk away from it.

**Hollis Thrale answers before the question is asked, and asks for it to be asked out loud in the shed anyway, because he is the only person on this coast who has ever been asked a question in this shed and he would like to know what it is like the second time.** *Would you like to be the one standing there.* **He says no, hears it come out of his own mouth before he has a thing behind it, takes about four seconds, and then says yes** — and that he has wanted to be asked for about two years, has told nobody including the woman at the fire, and has not said a wrong thing in that shed in about nine years.

**And then the finding he made two years ago, standing where Ilyan is standing, and he may do as he likes with it: a record is a thing a person has to stand behind. That is the whole of what a pane is. It is not the glass, not the guild, not the frame. It is the man who stood beside the arch on one morning with the weather he got, and the four hundred people who stand at that fire after him, and the guild has been borrowing his standing for three years and does not know it.** Which means the question just asked is the only question anybody on this coast has ever asked about a record, nobody has ever asked it of the man who makes them, and a man of sixty-one who has been the man at the fire for forty-one years is not a person anybody thinks to ask. **You are three days late. You are not too late. Three days is nothing. Nineteen years would have been something.** And then: that is not an excuse, it is a description.

**And the part that stops it: the cost of a record on this coast is a person for eleven months, and that is the whole of the price and there is no discount.** A person, a fire and a year, and the year has to be paid for before anybody knows whether there is going to be anything at the end of it, and the person is whoever is standing at the second fire, and she is twenty-four and paid nothing and the third-year apprentice of a man who cannot hear on his right. **Nobody has asked her. I have not asked her. You are not going to ask her, and I am telling you that now so that you cannot** — because he has walked two miles to be careful and he will get here again and it will be easier the second time, and that is exactly how it happens. **Asking her is not a question. It is an instruction with a question on the end of it**, and that is his sentence and it is about nine years old and he has never said it out loud in that shed before today. She would say yes. She would say yes because she is twenty-four and has not answered a letter and does not know yet that she is allowed to say no to a thing like that, and he would have her yes on his conscience and on Ilyan's, and he is not putting it on a girl's back to find out.

**So: no third pane — not because a third pane is wrong, but because it is another eleven months of the same person and you cannot settle a disagreement between two photographs by making a third one.** And the guild's charter is nineteen years and eight months old and it is a pane, and a pane cannot be crossed out, and that one is never coming out of that room, and there is one key and the key is on a belt on a person. **The order for a fifth pane is in a drawer in that room with the date on the outside of it, and the season's money is not in the arch, and that is a year, and a year ends on a letting day the tide chooses, and Danno Ray has been standing on shingle all season telling anybody who will stand still that a rule which cannot be changed for a year is not a rule but a season, and he is right, and neither of them has got a use for being right about it, and nobody on this coast has ever picked it up.**

He reads the order sheet out loud while putting a bar in the fire, and the System renders it: **a pane, a date, no figure, and the season's money is not in the arch — and what is not decided is whether anybody on this coast will be told about the arch before the money is in it, or after.** Then the two columns again, unasked, in about a minute and a half, and then the question he has been waiting two years for: **what would those two have to be for that wall to be in the right place.** A wall does not move; what moves is the hole. Two hundred and sixty-one against two hundred and sixty-four is three yards in nineteen years and eight months, and something has taken the gravel off the outer end, and he will not put a figure on a wall about water. And the eighth column is the other half: **if the toe stands to the fourth course there is two feet eight of gravel packed in front of the whole of that face and the wall is doing exactly what it was put there to do; if the toe stands to the second course the gravel has dropped a foot and four for the whole of two hundred and sixty yards and dropped further where the wall is highest, and what the ebb is then doing is coming round the bottom of a wall instead of running off the end of one, and there is a length of that wall with nothing under it.** And the man on the coast who has an answer in a book is two miles down that road and has not been told, and has not been told for two years, and that is on him and not on the other man, and he has not got a way to put it right that is not a coach.

**And in a room two hundred miles up this coast a version of all of it is on a shelf, put there in a season of money about nineteen years and eight months ago, correct in every line, and it is not going to be told anything this week, and the man reading it has a quarter in front of him with a line carrying a wage and no person on it, and he chose the one that writes nothing, the way he chose it last quarter, and will choose it next quarter.** At the ninth bell the man who walked two miles goes back down the road on a leg that has not been his own since the fourth of the third tide, and about nine people on that coast have now seen something happen to him on a road or in a boat and not one of them has said a word.

## Chapter 0145 — On the Wall

**The fourth of the ninth tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, day fifty-four. THE CHAPTER THE OUTLINE NAMES: the guild officer, with her life in the scene, and she is the one who has to be persuaded and she is persuaded by an argument about her own knowledge.**

**The stone at the head of the flat is not bare, because a fourth is one of the two days it is there, and about fourteen people within earshot work out inside a minute that this is the second day in a week the stone has been standing there with nobody on it, and not one of them says a word, because this coast does not discuss a door it has decided to walk past.** **The harbourmaster does not know the seventh and does not know this one either.** She comes down the hill at the fourth bell with a board under her arm and her hood up, as she has for six years, and **she does not go round the far stones — she goes up the lane four hundred yards to the north end instead, because the wall is out of the water on a fourth, a sixth and a seventh, and a harbourmaster who has not looked at a wall on one of those three days in seven in nineteen years has not been looking at a wall.** The leg goes on wet shingle in about four seconds, stays gone for two hundred yards of standing still, comes back a minute later worse, and about nine people see it and none of them says a word.

**She will not go out on the wall, and says why: in nineteen years she has never had her boots on it, and she is not going to be the first woman on it on a fourth with the sea standing over the crown, and he may write that down as cowardice, and it is not cowardice, it is nineteen years of being the person people come to and not the person who goes out.** Then she makes him ask. **She says no, and says no fast, and says why she said it so fast, and it is not because she does not want it.** Eight days ago on a shingle in front of about forty people she said no to the stone and then said the rest of it out loud, and she has not said it to anybody since and has not been asked it since, **and he has come back down that hill to ask her the same question in a different place, and what he has done is make her answer of eight days ago the more expensive.** Because she said: *I would rather be a coast than a woman*, and meant it, and has never said it to anybody before him and did not enjoy saying it and has not regretted it and has not gone back and wondered whether she should have said it differently. Either he did not hear her, which nobody is going to believe, or he heard her and came back anyway — **and she would like to know what he has come back with, and she would rather have it in the open at the foot of this wall than in a room with a door shut, because a room is where this coast puts things that are about people, and in nineteen years nobody in that building has ever asked her to stand in a particular place, and the one time she has been asked it was on a shingle and out loud, and the difference between those two things is the whole of what she has been trying to tell him for eight days.**

**He says the wrong thing out loud first, and then uses her own words back to her and says plainly that he is quoting a woman and not a record, that there is no record in this and he is not going to bring one down to her and has not asked her for one twice and will not ask a third time — and that is the whole of what is different about it.** He quotes her back: *do not write it that I stood on that stone for six years out of duty*, and the rest of it, that standing in somebody's habit is not the same thing as agreeing to a duty, and that if she had agreed to it she would have had to have been asked first, and nobody asked her. **And he says the true half of the thing he came to say: not that she agreed to it, but that he has come to ask her. And he has been on this coast fifty-four days and nobody on it has asked him for one thing.**

**And she refuses to let him believe it worked.** *I do not like this. I want that said before anything else, because I have worked out what you have done and I do not like it, and you are going to go away from this wall believing that a woman of fifty-one on a bar day has been persuaded. **I have not been persuaded of anything.** I have been asked a question I have never been asked, and I have said no to it, and I have given you the reason for the no that I have never given anybody, and that is not being persuaded. **You have quoted me to myself in the open and it has not moved me one inch about the stone, and if you go up that hill tonight and think I have come round on that, you have come a long way for nothing and I will not be responsible for it.*' **And then the other half: he has not asked her whether she would be in a thing; he has asked whether she would like to be the one standing there, and that answer is still no and will be no next week and she will say it again at the turn on the sixth before he gets the words out.** What he wants is for her to put her name to a claim about a wall, and she has been asked that four times in nineteen years by people in good coats and said no four times, and three of those men were wrong about the money and one of them was wrong about her.

**And the reason, which she has never said out loud to anybody, and it is not the money and not the guild:** the moment there is a claim about that wall with a person's name at the end of it, that person is the person for it, and not for a season — for ever. **And on this coast a person who is the person for a thing cannot stop being the person for it, because there is no second door and no other counter and about nine hundred people come to a person and not to a bench and not to a court and not to a guild. And in four months the guild will have got a perfectly good reason to put me at a counter for the remainder of my sight, and they will be right to, and I will be the woman who handed them the reason.** And she is not going to be argued out of it by a leg and a mile and a half of stairs and a man quoting her at herself, **because that is not being argued out of anything, that is being worn down, and I have never once been worn down in six years, and the day I am is the day this coast finds out what it has actually been getting, and it will not be a woman of fifty-one on a wall. It will be a queue.**

**And then she sits down on the shingle and says yes.** *I said no to the stone eight days ago in front of forty people and I meant it and I am not taking it back. I am saying yes to the other thing, and you did not ask me the other thing, and I answered it anyway, and I would like it noted that I answered a question you did not ask, which is the first time in nineteen years and is not going to be a habit.* **If I put my name to it, what would it have to say.** *Me.* **It must not say the harbourmaster of Kestrel Reach. It must not say the office at Kestrel Reach. It must not say the guild, because the guild will be right about that wall whether or not it is on a piece of paper and that is not the point either.** If it says a body, then in four months somebody will write to that body, and the body will be answered by a clerk of twenty-two with a brush in his cuff, or it will not be answered, and either way a carter at the near stones on a bad morning has gone to a name and not to a person, and he will do it again in a fortnight and then he will not do it at all. **About nine hundred people on this water come to a person, and not to anything with more than one door in it, and that is the whole of what this coast is, and it is not going to be improved by a piece of paper that has got a building on the end of it.** So it says Bettany Rook, and what she said, in her own words, and not what she thinks about the wall, because she has not been on the wall and has not sounded it and has no number and **is not going to be given somebody else's number and put her name to it.** And the burden has to be a person and it has to be her, and it does not go to an office and it does not go to a harbour, **and when she cannot do it then it stops, and she has thought about that for eight days and knows exactly what it costs and has decided she would rather it stopped than that it went to a building, and she would rather be a woman about that than a coast, and you may write that down and take it away and it will not frighten me.** And: *tell whoever is writing it that the woman has been asked and has said yes to a duty and has said no to an office, and the duty is mine and the office is nobody's, and if that does not close the matter then the matter does not close.*

**She goes back up the lane, across the flat, round the far stones and up the hill, and does four hours of figures with a clerk of twenty-two, finishes inside the hour, and not one person in that room puts a single question to her, then or afterwards.** And at the ninth bell the water comes back over the north end and the flat takes its ninth crossing and a boy of eleven goes over at a penny and a woman of forty-eight writes the ninth down on a slate on her knee and is paid nothing, **and the stone at the head of the flat is standing there with nobody on it, which is the fourth time in ten days, and not one of the four was a day anybody asked for.**

## Chapter 0146 — The Gravel

**The fifth of the ninth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, day fifty-five. Mostly water and weather, in the ordinary way, because a good day is the day a coast works.** Six crossings, the boats out, a lug sail standing along past the point, a boat at the hard coming up on a flood, the drying yard running, a boat's carpenter going over a garboard with a plane. The deep at the north end is nine foot on a day like that and there is nothing on this coast with wheels on within forty yards of the toe of that wall, and a man cannot sound it, cannot get to it and cannot see the bottom of it, **and a wall nobody can get at on four days in seven is a wall nobody has looked at since the fourth year.**

**And on the fifth, out of a good day, the eleven yards moves.** Halse Tarrant walks the top of the bar and stops about ninety yards along it and stands there for about ten minutes looking at the edge. **There is a notch in the top of that gravel. It has been there as long as he has been coming up here — about a foot and a bit deep, about a boot long, and the only place on that bar where the sea ever got over the top — and it is not a notch any more. It is a place where the gravel has come down, about eleven yards along the edge, and it is not a hole and it is not a break and he wants that said first, because he has had four people that morning telling him about a hole.** And he crouches down and puts his heel in where the notch was and **his heel does not find the bottom of it.** He will not put a figure on it, and says why: *I am not going to put a figure on it this morning and if anybody asks me for one I will tell them what I have told you, which is that I can see it and I cannot stand behind a number I got off a boot.*

**Seven days since the number and six days since the breach, and he says he is not going to take back the less-than-two-winters because he has been right about that bar for thirty-one years, but he has never seen it do that before and he is not going to stand in front of two men and say it cannot.** And then the cruelty of it, in the trade's mouth: **nobody drowned, there is no boat gone, there is a wall with a hole in the foot of it and a bank that used to be a foot and a bit at the top, and that is all — and a shore going does not do its work on the days the sea is doing something else. It does it on the ordinary days. It does it on a good day when there is nothing to look at and everybody is busy.**

**Pell Cawley gives the one thing he has got and will not pretend it is more than it is: that hull came up on the ways on the sixth and she is drawing about a thumb deeper at the bow than before he touched her, and he has not taken anything off her and has not put anything on, and he does not know what that is, and he will not guess at it in a yard in front of nine people, because he has watched a man on this coast guess at a thing and be right about it for the wrong reason and then be asked to do it again.** *That is all I have got and I would rather say it than keep it.*

**The rest of the morning is not about the wall and is on the same ground.** The man who keeps it walks the whole length of the top course — a day ago, on the fourth, when the wall was out of the water — and puts the flat of a bar on every stone he goes over, and it tells him that not one stone has moved, and he says so out loud at the outer end: **the wall is sound and has been sound for nineteen years and always will be, and the two things are true at the same time and everybody who works stone knows they are the same thing.** Cleve Gannock comes up the lane with a pair of beasts yoked to a cart, on his way to a landing, and the whole of what is said to him about the eleven yards is nothing at all, **and that is the correct end of that and it has been the correct end of it since the sixth of a tide.** And a man of thirty-one stands in a lane with his weight on his good side and works out in about four seconds, standing in the cold, that this is the cost — **and that the cost is not the wall and not the money and not even the day.** The guild said it in four lines and every word was right: the season's money is not in the arch and it will not be this season. **The one thing on four hundred miles of this coast that could put a number about that wall in front of a body two hundred miles up this coast and make that body have to answer it was glass. And glass is a fire and a year and a person, and the person is whoever stands at the second fire, and the second fire cannot be left once it is let in, and the person at it is a woman of twenty-four who is paid nothing and has not been asked and has not answered a letter, and the year has not started.** Eleven months, paid in advance, paid by her, **paid whether or not there is a pane at the end of it**, and nobody has asked her and he is not going to ask her, and a question put to her about keeping it would have been a direction with a question on the end of it. And that was not in a room. **It was in a lane at the north end, said by nobody, worked out by a man with a bad leg, and it is not going to be said out loud because there is no room on this coast to say it in and it is not his to say and it is not hers to be told.**

## Chapter 0147 — Three Texts

**The sixth of the ninth tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, day fifty-six. STAGE 3, WITNESS BROKER, IS ARRIVED HERE, and it is arrived by a practice, a failure, a relationship and a permanent cost, and nothing about him is stronger.**

**He goes to the flat first and asks Hal Bevan for nothing**, and tells him he is not going to ask the flat again because he asked it once by accident and a woman of forty-eight said no in front of nine people and she was right. **That is the first time in nineteen years that the man on the chain has been told what was going to be done in his name by anybody, and he was told it by a stranger with no standing who was not asking him for a decision.** And then, at the turn, without turning round, the one true thing: he has a thing he has carried for nineteen years — he decides who gets the tide and he does it with a slate and there is no rule anywhere on this coast that says he does, and he has never written it down. Three people who were not together have each said a thing, and nobody has to understand the other two, **and if you put them side by side then somebody four hundred miles off is going to read the page and see one thing where there are three, and he will not be doing it on purpose. That is what a page does. A page is one page.** And Ilyan says: *I am not going to put them side by side.* And Bevan: *Good. Then you have understood more about this coast in a fortnight than a man in a good coat understands in a year, and I have got no more, and that is nine crossings.*

**The yard at the north end, and he does not ask her in front of anybody, which is the first thing he does and she notices it and he does not pretend he had not.** *I am asking you for something I have no right to ask you for and I am going to ask it once, and you can say no and I will not come back with it in different words.* She says yes. Then he asks for her words to go in a record. **And she says no, fast, and not because of him, and she wants that said first and said in the open because there are four people in the yard and two of them have stopped.** *I said no because you said a record, and a record on this coast is a thing with everybody's business in it, and the two things in it that came from me this week were said in a yard standing up with a man of fifty on the other side of me and two men on a net forty feet off, and that is the only place a thing of mine has ever been said that anybody could act on, and I said it because you asked me a question and I am not going to be a woman who gave a thing to a page.* And she has never met either of the other two; one is a man of sixty-one who has never once in forty-one years been asked whether he would like to be the one standing there, and the other is the harbourmaster she has been under that counter about nine times in twenty-four years to say *how much to the far stones*.

**He does not argue with it. He changes the structure, not the ask: not a page with three people on it, but three texts.** Yours is a page of its own and it is yours and it goes in on its own and it is not inside anybody else's and it does not come before anybody else's and it does not come after. **It says the day you said it and the place you said it in and who else was standing there when you said it, and that not one of them was asked for anything. And it does not say what the other two said, and it does not say their numbers, and it is not going to be read next to theirs, because he cannot stop a clerk reading a page and he can stop the two of them being on the same page.** The other two are the same. Three sheets, three names at three separate places, **and nothing at the top of any of them that says the three of them are about the same water, because nobody has asked the three of them that and he is not going to ask them.**

**And then the third thing, which she insists on saying herself:** *A page of its own is not a record of nothing. Everybody on this coast thinks a page of its own is a record of nothing, because a woman a hundred and forty miles up this coast took a copy of a list of weeks home in a bundle and wrote on it that it is a record of nothing, and she was right about that copy.* **Mine will not be a record of nothing, because it will have a day on it and a place on it and my name on it, and it will say what a person in a boat can be shown and what she will not put a number on, and a number somebody can act on has to come off a page or it is a man talking in a yard for a fortnight.** And: *if I am on a page on my own, then I am a person who has said something in a record, and I have not answered a letter in four years and I have done a thing for eleven years that nobody on this coast has asked me the name of, and I would rather have it on a page on its own than in a man's mouth in a room for the rest of my life, and I have been eleven years waiting for somebody to put me somewhere and I did not know that was the shape of it until about a minute ago.* **Yes — to a page of its own. Not to that. And she will say it again that afternoon in the same words, and he may put that down as the second time, because she does not want a thing of hers in a record on the strength of a man saying it in a yard once.**

**The bench at Thornglass. Hollis Thrale says yes, and says what his is going to be: not a number. The third column and the eighth column and the fact that he cannot tell anybody which of two photographs is right, and that for two years he has had a figure in his head about a wall and has not walked down a road to give it to the man who keeps it.** *You are going to write on it that the outer end has come back three yards. I am not going to have that on my page. That is your arithmetic and not mine and it is a thing a man does in a room. What is mine is that the third column says two hundred and sixty-one and the guild's says two hundred and sixty-four, and I have had both of them for two years, and the water moved, and I do not know which one the water left.* And: *it is not better, it is more honest and it is worth less, and you are going to find that out about this in about four days and I have been finding it out for two years.*

**Then the woman at the fire, in about nine seconds and four words, and she does not turn round.** He asks her to keep the three pages — not to do anything to them, to hold them, and to know where each of them stops and starts, **because there is nobody else on this coast who can put two lists side by side and say where one of them runs out, and everybody who has tried has come away with a sentence made out of both and been wrong.** *Yes*, she says, to the fire. And that is the whole of it. He did not ask her what it involved and she did not ask him what it was for, and neither of them has said one word about it since.

**The room a mile and a flight of stairs up the hill, with the door shut, which is the second time in five days that anybody has had a conversation in that room with the door shut for its own sake.** *Put it in*, says Bettany Rook, before he has said it. **What goes on it: a duty, and her name on it, and not an office. And four lines from the guild, word for word, with the fourth line underlined, because that is the line which cost them nothing to write and it is the only thing that office has sent to this coast in the best part of two years that was not a figure of money. What does not go on it: a number. She has not been on that wall and has not sounded it and has not got a figure and is not going to be handed one and put her name under it.** And the cost to her, said once and not again: she will be at a counter for the rest of the working life of a woman of fifty-one because of this, and she is not to put that in either, because that is hers and not a fact about a wall.

**Then the two of them make the thing and it takes most of the evening.** He writes the three heads longhand on three sheets of his own, an hour and a half, the last of the ink he had brought, and the three accounts on them are in three other hands and not in his. Each sheet says what it is, when, where, and who else was standing there. **There is no sheet on top of the three of them. There is no sentence at the top of the three of them, and it took him about a minute and a half every time he started to write one and about a minute and a half to stop.** He had done that before, in a net loft at the north end on the third of the sixth tide, in about nine minutes, and it had held a tide and cost a carter a day and a day's hire, and every word of it had been out of one of three true accounts, and the three had been in three different places, and nobody had been asked to agree to anything, and it was legal. **That is the thing he has been wrong about for a month, and he had it exactly backwards until the seventh of a tide at the post at Wether Cross, when a man of seventy-one took a bar out of a cart, put a chisel in a bag, said *there is a hammer in my hand and there is no rule on this coast that says who holds it*, and put the hammer in his hand — and the whole of the trade is the separation and not the joining, and there is no other trade.**

**The System puts the record in front of him at the ninth bell and gives him the three sheets and nothing that joined them: three accounts of eleven yards of the north wall, given in three places on three days by three people who were not together, nobody has asked the three of them to agree, and the three accounts do not agree. What is not decided is whether three accounts of one wall are one matter or three.** He read it and it told him what he already knew and it certified nothing and it joined nothing, **and he had wanted it to do one of those two things and had not said so to anybody.** Then he puts his name at the bottom of the third sheet, and under it that he asked the three and two said yes at the first asking and one said no to a page with anybody else on it and then said yes of her own accord in the same words, and that he put the three sheets in a bundle and is not a party to any of them, has no standing on this coast, has no office, and asked three people for one thing each and was given three. **And the bundle went up the coast road in a cart with a load of oil, two hundred miles, to an office with a printer in it, and it is not locked and it is not sealed and it is a bundle of paper with three names on it and his name at the bottom, and anybody in that building can send for it and read it and take a copy of it and send the copy to a man on a coast he has never walked.** He works out what the fee was, standing in the wet at about nine seconds, and it was not a thing he had known when he started, and he says it out loud once, to nobody, on a bar day, and does not say it again that week or the week after. **That is the broker's fee. Somebody can send for the broker's own paper.**

## Chapter 0148 — What Pulling It Costs

**The seventh of the ninth tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, day fifty-seven, and the turn.** **Nobody stood on the stone. It comes round twice a week, the last person to stand on it was a carter of thirty-three on the fourth of the seventh tide, that is two tides back, and there have been four days since on which it was standing there with nobody on it — and this was the other sort of day, and the woman who stands on it does not know this is the sort of day she does not know, and there was not one question anybody could have put there and not one person on that shingle would have walked up to try.**

**And there is one thing left to settle and it is the whole of what the thing was: what it costs a person to pull it.** The question came out of a room a mile up the hill on the sixth evening from the one of the three who cannot leave her counter, in one sentence, standing up: ***If it is not revocable it is a licence. And the guild sells licences. And this is not a licence, and if I have put my name to a thing that cannot be pulled then I have put my name to a licence, and I would rather it could be pulled, and I would rather it cost something to pull.*** He asked what it would cost to pull it and she said she did not know and was not going to find out by thinking about it in a room, **and that if it did not cost anything then anybody could pull it and it would be pulled in a fortnight by a person with a grudge and a bad morning, and a thing that can be pulled by anybody for nothing is not a charter either. It is a notice.** So the answer has to be worked out by people who would have to pay it, and he has no standing to call anybody and does not call anybody, and it gets worked out anyway in five places over one day, because five people on this coast cannot leave a thing alone once they have heard of it.

**Pell Cawley, the boat's carpenter, without stopping: a man has to be able to pull it. Not the guild, not the office, not nine men in a room. A man. Any man who goes over that flat. And the minute you say any man, then it is a day.** And then it is worse than a day: it has to be said out loud, on the flat, at the head of it, with about seventy people on it, **and not one word of it written for him, because a man who pulls a thing by letter is a body, and you have gone a week without a building on the end of this on purpose.** So whoever he is has to stand on nine hundred yards of shingle and say why in his own words to every person there, and there is no second door and no clerk, and he is a person who has said out loud in front of seventy people that three people he may never have spoken to got something wrong. **That is a day, and it is worse than a day. That is a day and a thing that cannot be put back.** And then the part that will kill it in about a month and nobody will notice it happening: **the day a man would want to spend looking at that wall is a day he cannot use — you cannot do it on a seventh, you cannot do it on a fourth or a sixth either, not properly, and a man in nine foot of water inside an hour and a quarter is what a bar day gives you — so the first time a man works that out he will not use it, and the second time somebody else will not use it, and a month after that nobody on this coast will believe it can be done at all, and it will be a thing that was.**

**Corrie Tallow, forty yards off, to about nine people at the far stones who were not the least bit interested: the day is not the part that hurts him.** A day is a cart, or a stall, or a boat. What hurts him is that he has to stand there and be asked nothing — he says why, and then about seventy people look at him, and not one of them may say a word back, because they are not the ones who made it and they cannot be. **She has watched about four people on this coast be refused something in the open and it is the only thing she has ever seen here that costs more than a day: it costs him the standing he had at the stall, or the boat, or the flat, and he will get it back in about a year if he is lucky and he will feel it every day until he does.** And the last part, which is the finding: **he cannot pull it for anybody. He cannot pull it for the people who cannot get over that flat on a bad morning. He cannot pull it on behalf of the eleven households or the boy with the lead or a woman with a handcart. He can pull it for himself, and if he does that then it is his grievance and it is a real one, and it is the only shape the thing has.** Nobody objects, **which on this coast is not a consent and Corrie Tallow has said so out loud and about four of the people standing there had heard that.**

**Hal Bevan, at the seventh bell, without turning round: a revocation stops a thing and does not undo it. You know that. You have known it since before you came to this coast and better than anybody on it, and I am the last man on four hundred miles who ought to be telling you something you have not worked out yourself.** And then the part that makes the thing real: **when a man pulls it, the eleven yards is still going. The water does not know. And the person who pulled it will be the person who said three people who were not together were wrong about a wall, and he will have said it in the open, and he will have been right or wrong about a thing nobody on this coast can prove either way, because the two photographs cannot come out of their room and a man with a lead line has not been out on a fourth.** So he is going to be a man who was wrong, or a man who was right, in public, with his name on it, on a coast where there is not one door anybody can show it to anybody else in. **And the reason that is worth paying a day for is that it is the only kind of record this coast has ever had, and it is not a record anybody will look at. It is a record somebody can be got at.**

**And at the north end, a woman of thirty-six comes out of her own yard with a warp over her shoulder, having been out in the deep twice that day and past the wall on the flood — which is the only day of the week it can be looked at from that side and the worst day of the week to be there.** *I am the only one of the three who can get under it, so it will be me that gets asked, and I have worked out what I would want before I would do it, and I am telling you both because you will not ask me and I am not going to wait to be asked.* **She would want the day back, and she has never had one of anybody's days and has had eleven years of her own, so she knows exactly what one is worth and is not being brave about it.** And she would want the eleven yards sounded **before** she pulled it and not after, by a man with a lead line in a boat of his own on a day the sea is standing on the crown, with the number in his book with his name on it and the date on it, and then she would stand on that shingle and say why with the number on the table in front of seventy people. **Without that it is not a revocation, it is a person being angry in public — and she has been angry in public twice and one of them was at a counter about a basket, and there is a thing about being angry in public on four hundred miles that nobody tells you, and it is that the people who were right about the small thing get asked about the big one for the rest of their lives.**

**At the turn, in the ordinary way, as it is every seventh, the lamp-oil man at the head of the lane comes out and leans on his door frame and sings the crossing's song to about nine people and the water. It is a tariff in four lines, it is not written down, it is about a hundred years old, it settles nothing, nobody put a figure out of it, nobody appealed with it, and it was not the subject of anything at all that day.**

**And at the ninth bell a man of thirty-three, who has been standing at the head of the flat since the fourth bell with his beasts' heads up and a load of deals behind him waiting for the flat to take the water off, comes down forty yards of shingle and says the thing he has been working out for a night and told nobody.** He is not going to pull it. **Not because he would not be allowed to. Because he would be pulling it about himself.** He spent the only question he has ever had on this coast on a piece of paper and a woman on that stone told him out loud in front of seventy people that he had not spent it on the water, and she was right, and he has not been able to put it anywhere for a month, and **there is now.** *There is now. That is what I have been working out. Not because I want it and not because anybody put me up to it — I have not spoken to a man off a road in my life and I am not speaking to him now. Because I have been in a fortnight where the only door there was a stone with one question on it, twice a week, and a woman standing on it, and that is a thing you can put a thing into. **A thing anybody can pull is a thing you can put a thing into**, and I have been standing on this flat for a day and a half finding that out, and I am not pulling it.* He would be pulling it about himself, and it would be a day, and a day is a cart and a pair of beasts and a landing, and the one of the three who could answer him back is up that hill and would be at that counter in about four months, **and he would have taken a day off a road that is two days a week for it.** And he asks for one thing: *I would like it said out loud, at the head of this flat, by somebody, that a man of thirty-three worked out that he could pull it and decided not to, and that nobody on this coast has to think the thing is not real because of him. Put that in your bundle, and if the clerk in the city cannot read it then he cannot read it, and that will be his.* He gets his beasts' heads up, goes over in about three quarters of an hour with his beasts used to it, and does not look back at the stone.

**And the chapter's sentence: that is the whole of what it costs a person to pull a thing on four hundred miles of this coast, and it is a day and a standing and seventy faces and no answer, and it cannot be done for anybody else, and the day it happens it is a day the sea is over the crown and a wall cannot be looked at from the side that matters. Which is what a charter is, and not a notice and not a licence.** And a man of thirty-one stands at the near end in the dark with his weight on his good side, having asked four people a question he had no right to ask and been answered by all four in the open on a bad day, and having not persuaded a single one of them of anything, and not said once what any of them should do, and with no page for it, and not going to write one.

## Chapter 0149 — In Their Own Words

**The first of the tenth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, day fifty-eight. THE RESOLUTION, and it is small.** The whole of the making of it took about five hours and a bench and a pot of tea that a man of sixty-one made for two people who had not asked him for anything, in a shed with a cold arch behind two feet of brick.

**And the reason it can be pulled at all: a pane is a picture of one morning taken by a man standing beside an arch, it weighs what a pane weighs, it hangs in an oak frame on the north wall of a room a mile and a flight of stairs up a hill, it has not been out of that building in nineteen years, and it cannot be crossed out. A thing that cannot be crossed out is a thing that cannot be pulled. This is three sheets of paper, written out longhand, three times over, in three hands, with no plate and no form and nothing printed off anything, and it can be crossed out, and one of the three people on it is going to cross her own out with a pen in about four months, in a room with a door shut, and nobody will be able to stop her and nobody is going to try.**

**The charter, in full, in the fiction:** the wall, the hole, the two hundred and sixty yards, the courses, the nineteen years and eight months, the two feet of gravel, the guild's season of about seven weeks with eight men, the guild's own words set down rather than a summary of them and the fourth of the four quoted because it costs them nothing. Three accounts follow, given in three places on three days by three people who were not together, nobody asked to agree, set out one to a page, **not joined, and they do not agree.** THE THREE: Sorrel Gannock, thirty-six, of the hard at the north end, a flat boat, in her own yard; Hollis Thrale, sixty-one, glassmaker, of Thornglass, at his own bench; Bettany Rook, fifty-one, harbourmaster of Kestrel Reach, in her own office. **THE BURDEN: each of the three answers for what she or he said and for nothing else; not one of the three answers for either of the others; each of the three may stop, and where one stops the claim stops with them. THE REVOCATION: this may be pulled by any one person who goes over that flat; not by a guild, an office, a bench or any body at all; not on behalf of any other person, and not paid for, asked for, given to or carried out by anybody; a person who pulls it loses a day, and the day is theirs and not anybody else's, and it must be said in the open at the head of that flat, and no person may write it for them. IT IS PAPER. IT IS NOT GLASS. A PANE CANNOT BE CROSSED OUT AND THIS CAN.** And the System renders it: three accounts of eleven yards, in three hands, on three pages, with the burden on three people and the revocation on any one person, it is paper and not glass — **and what is not decided is whether the guild will sound that wall in a season, or whether a year will go by with nobody asking.**

**Not one of the three asked him what that was.** No bench sat. No council convened, the five-to-four vote was not repeated, and nobody in any room said a figure aloud that had been said in a room in a season four years back. **The guild is in it and is not a villain and does not lose and is not defeated: the money is not there and nobody pretends it is.** What the three pages bought is about a foot of water and eleven yards of wall and no money at all: **a body two hundred miles up this coast cannot answer that claim without answering three people by name, one of them a boat, one of them a fire, and one of them a counter, and a person in a good coat who has never walked on that beach. And it is the first thing on this coast that has ever been made that way** — three people, in three places, in their own words, and not one word of any of it anybody else's — **and nobody in that shed said so out loud, and it was not said as an argument in a room, because it was not an argument.** It was a bench, a pot of tea, and about as long as it takes to make one.

**Hollis Thrale, to the bench and not to anybody: I have wanted two records on one bench in one room on this coast for two years, and I have not got them and I am not going to get them, and what I have got instead is three pieces of paper that do not touch, and I would not have believed on the second of the seventh tide that that was the same want.** He puts the bar into the fire at the mouth of it and steps back, and four seconds later it comes out and goes into the rack on the wall, and neither of them looks at the other, **and that is how it has been between them for three years.**

**And then the refusal that ends the volume, and he did not know it was a mistake until a quarter of an hour after it had happened.** He stayed, because there was tea, and because he had a leg, and because that shed was the only room on this coast he had ever been welcome in, and because a man of sixty-one who had walked two miles to be careful was going to put another fire in and there was a draught off the north end of that building. So he said it out loud, in the ordinary way, in a shed, to two people: *I arranged something two days ago at this bench and I did not put it in writing and I have not mentioned it since and you have not asked me about it, and I am going to mention it now because there is going to be a claim in a bundle and the claim has to live somewhere and I arranged where it lives and I arranged it with you two days ago in about four seconds and you said yes to the fire.* **I arranged it before you knew what it was. That is what I have come to say. I asked you whether you would keep three pages, and I did not tell you what keeping them meant, and I knew what it meant when I asked, and I asked you anyway.**

*No*, says Sabra Trew. *You did not, and I want to be exact about the order of that, because you have come two miles to be careful and I have watched you be careful three times this month and you keep being careful in the wrong direction, and a glassmaker in this building told me on the third of the seventh tide, standing at his own fire, that a person saying no and a person being unable to say yes are two different things, and it has been the most useful thing anybody has said to me in three years.* **You asked me whether I would keep three pages. You did not ask me whether I would stand behind three pages. And I said yes to the first one in about four seconds because the first one is a thing I have done since I was nine years old, and I cut marks for a living, and I have kept a list of firings since I was eleven and nobody has ever asked me for it and it has never once been read. You did not ask me the second one. And I would have said no to the second one. And you knew that, and you did not ask it, and you have not done anything wrong that a person could put a name to, and that is exactly why I am saying this, because if you had lied to me I would have had nothing to be angry about and I would have got over it in a day.** And she puts the bar down on the floor, which she does not do. And then the whole of it, and it is about her work and not about him: **if I keep those three pages, then a person in a city two hundred miles up this coast is going to ask me a question about one of them. Not now. In four months, or a year, or nine years, and it will not be you and it will not be him, and it will be a man with a brush in his cuff who has found something in a page and wants to know which of three people said it, and he will come to this shed because this shed is where the thing is. And then I will be the person who keeps it.** Not him, who is four hundred miles behind her and cannot be got at. Not her master, who is sixty-one and has a bench and a wall and is the man at the fire and has been for forty-one years, and everybody on this coast already knows him, and everybody would come to him about a thing on a bench and would not come to me about a thing in a box. **They would come to me because I am the one holding it. And I have stood at a second fire six days in seven for three years and I have not answered a letter of my own in four and I am paid nothing, and I am twenty-four and I have never once in my life been asked to stand behind anything, and you have not asked me that either, and you have arranged it, and I have found out about it two days late from a man who has been thinking about how to say it.** The work is a fire and a bar and a wall of brick and a mark a week, six days in seven, **and if I take this then I have taken something I cannot put down when I am tired, and I will be tired, and there is no day on this coast when a person is not tired, and this coast has spent four hundred years putting a person who is tired in front of a thing and calling it a custom.** And the terms: *You may write down that I said no and that I said the reason, and the reason is mine and it is about the work and I am not going to soften it for a bundle going two hundred miles. **And you may not write down that I was asked, because I was not.** That is the whole of what I am saying to you and I have said all of it.*

**It is the correct thing and he stood in a shed and did not repair it, and did not try to repair it, and had it in him to say four things and said none of them.** He said: *That is right and I am not going to argue with it.* And she said: *Good. Now go and be somewhere else for a while. I have got a fire.*

**And he walks back down the coast road in the dark on a leg that has not been his own since the fourth of the third tide, and stops at about the half-mile, four hundred yards above the head of the flat, where there is a flat stone nobody has a name for, and sits down on it and takes out the penny exercise book and tears out a page.** *On the first day of the tenth tide, at about the sixth bell, in the shed at Thornglass, Sabra Trew refused to keep the three pages. She said no, and she gave the reason, and the reason was that keeping them would put her in front of a question from a building two hundred miles up this coast, and that she stands at a fire six days in seven and is paid nothing and was not asked. It is the correct answer. I had arranged it on the sixth of the ninth tide in about four seconds and had not asked her what it was for.* *It is mine to write down and it is not mine to decide.* *The reason I am putting on this page is that I arranged it and she was not asked.* The date went on it, and that it was his, and both of those were the same two lines that are on the fifth page, in the same place, because they are the two lines he has put on six pages and he does not have a third. **And that was the sixth page, and it was the first one in six that had a reason on it, and the reason he had put down was the reason he could stand behind in a room, and it was not the reason.**

## Chapter 0150 — Beneath the Weeks

**The second of the tenth tide, a good day, the flat takes six. THE VOLUME CLOSE. Ilyan Vester has been on that coast fifty-nine days.** He walks two miles up the coast road at the second bell and gets to a shed at about the third bell; it took an hour and a quarter, and it had taken him an hour and a quarter on the third of the ninth tide going the other way with nothing in his coat he had not had before. **The arch is behind two feet of brick, cold, and will be cold for a year, and the guild's order is under the batch book with a clerk's date on it and nothing at all under the date.**

**And the wall behind the bench is the one thing in that shed that has never been moved and cannot be. It is four hundred years of weeks.** A week is the only piece of time on four hundred miles of this coast that everybody agrees is a piece of time, so every list on this coast is a list of weeks, and a week is cut as a stroke, and a week that is not there is a space the width of a stroke, and the marks go the length of that wall in a single row at about the height of a man's chest, and there are holes in it that nobody has ever counted. **Two people made copies. The woman who cut most of them is dead eleven years and copied the list out twice in letters; one of those copies went a hundred and forty miles up this coast in a bundle to a school and is in a press in a room with a dry lock on it, and the other is the one her granddaughter used to work from, and the granddaughter is the woman standing at the second fire with a bar in her hand.** And at the bottom of that wall, below the row of weeks, at about the height of a knee, where the brick is hardest to reach and the mortar is oldest, **there is a second run of marks that is not weeks.** *Nobody has touched that in my lifetime and nobody asked me about it until about a minute ago*, says Sabra Trew. *I have been in this shed every day since I was eleven years old.*

**He cannot read and he says so out loud at the bench, to a man of sixty-one and a woman at a fire, and neither of them shows the slightest sign of having heard anything unusual, and a man of sixty-one cannot hear a voice on his right and had not heard the whole of the sentence anyway.** And he does not ask her what it says. He asks her to put her hand on it and tell him what is under her hand — *not what it says, what is under your hand: where the marks are, how big the gaps are, what is a row and what is a column, how many rows, and the shape of the outside of it.*

**And she tells him, and it is not a list and it is not a line.** The whole of the weeks is one row, and about nine people on this coast have stood where he is standing and counted it as a line. **The other thing is not a line. It is in blocks, and the blocks are not square, and they are not even. The first block has a border on two sides and not on the other two, which is a thing you do when you are marking the edge of something and running out of wall, and the columns in it are not all the same width — one of them is about half the width of the one beside it, and it is empty below a point, so the block has a step in it. There are nine columns, and she has counted them about a hundred times because she cuts marks and she counts things. And at the end of it there is a row of about eleven things that are not strokes at all: little marks, every one different, all the same size, in a straight row with even spaces between them, and that is the one thing on that wall that looks like somebody meant it. Everything else on that wall is a count. That is a set.** And her grandmother cut the set, and told her once, when she was about fifteen, and she has said that out loud to about four people in twenty-four years and has never got any further with it: ***that is not mine. I found it. I have been finding it since I was a girl. I have never worked it out and I am not going to guess at it in a wall.***

**Then the rendering comes up on him, in a shed, with his hand flat on about two feet of brick, at the height and at the distance it has been at since the third of the second month in a city four hundred miles behind him: the boards, the table the size of a table a hand could go on, the lamp with the wick down, the window with weather on the outside of it that was not this weather, four people at the table and one of them about forty-four with her back to him.** It does not move. He could not look away from it with two feet of brick under his hand, and it cost him what it costs, and the leg went at the same moment from the hip down, all of it, and did not come back inside the length of a shed and came back the rest of the way worse, and he put his weight against the bench and stood there for about four minutes and said nothing about it, and a man of fifty-eight two miles down that coast has never been told and a man of sixty-one in this room has not noticed and a woman at the fire has her back to both of them. **Nobody was told, then or afterwards, and about nine people on this coast have now watched something happen to him on a road, on a shingle or in a boat, and not one of them has ever mentioned it.**

**And then the System puts something in front of him that it had not been asked for, and that is the first time in his life that anything in it has recognised anything at all.** It does not translate the wall — it has never translated a wall — and it did not begin today. What it did was recognise, and it gave him one line, and the line was not a rendering of a record and not a notice and not anything he had entered anywhere, and **there was no block quote in it and no second line under it, and it did not end in what is not decided because there was nothing in it that was a decision.** The line was a shape: nine columns of uneven width with a border on two sides, and a step in it, and then a row of eleven marks that were all the same size and all different — **a shape he had seen ten thousand times in a building four hundred miles behind him in a life that had ended a very long way back from here, and it was a test grid, and he knew it the way a man knows a door in his own house in the dark.** And standing on its own at the end of it, with nothing attached: **NK-3317.** He could not read. He had not read it. **It is two letters, a dash and four figures he has never once seen on this coast and would not have known from a wall if somebody had cut it into one, and he knew it anyway, because a man does not have to read a thing he has been looking at for years.** And it is **Nadia Kestrel's**: nine hundred test cases in a building with a bad coffee machine and a column of dates beside her name, the signature at the bottom of every one of them her own two letters and a number, the thing she was proudest of and the thing she had been shouted at for, and once, a long way back, she told him that a build was a thing you could hold a person up with and a signature was the only part of a build that could not be argued with, and that if you took the signatures off a build it stopped being evidence and went back to being an opinion. He had filed more of them under her than under anybody, including himself, and had been shouted at for it more than once.

**THE SYSTEM SAYS NOTHING ELSE.** It does not say whose the signature is, or where the grid came from, or what a grid is, or what a set of eleven marks the same size and all different is for, or why somebody four hundred years ago cut it in marks on the wall of a working shed because a stroke is the only mark anybody on that coast had. **It recognised a signature and it stopped, the way it stops.** And the name came up in his head about four seconds later and he did not say it out loud: ***The Sovereign's Wake*** — a game, the last man to test it, a report filed on the final night that had gone up to a machine in that building and had never been merged, and four hundred years of telling anybody that he was right, and something that had begun, a long time afterwards, to move words out of it and out of a few hundred thousand other places, and he had never known that, and it was not about him. **And he could not have said, standing at a bench with his hand on brick, what any of that had to do with nine uneven columns and a row of eleven marks.** And a man of sixty-one at the bench, without turning round, tells him he has gone a colour, that it is not about the fire, that he can tell the difference between a man who is tired and a man who has found something out about himself, that he has had two of the second in twenty years and did not have a good time with either, and **that he is not going to ask — and that is the correct answer and the only one he has ever wanted from anybody, and he is not going to ask him again tonight, and if he ever wants to tell him he knows where the fire is.**

**And the question is asked at about the sixth bell, by a woman of twenty-four standing in front of a wall with her hand flat on it, in the ordinary voice, and nobody set it up.** She has been quiet for about ten minutes, which is a long time at a second fire. *If that is a copy of something, then somebody made it. And the making of it is the whole of what I want. Not what it is — I have not got a use for what it is and there is nobody on this coast who could tell me and I have stopped wanting to know in a shed.* **This coast has people in it who can say no. That is the thing I have never been able to account for and I have been trying to account for it since I was about eleven years old and I am the woman who cuts the marks in this building, so I have had a good deal of time to notice it. There is a stone at the head of the flat and a woman stands on it two days a week and she answers about the water and nobody may ask her a second, and nobody has ever written down that she does it, and nobody has ever asked her how she came to be allowed to.** A hundred years, near enough. **And there are four hundred years of weeks on that wall and there are gaps in the run of them where a week was not kept, and a gap in a list of weeks is not a mark, it is nothing, and I have spent three years being the one person on this coast who can lay two lists beside each other and say where each of them stops, and in the whole of that there is no column anywhere for a thing that was not kept. So a coast where a person can say no and a coast where a thing can be left out without anybody noticing are the same coast, and they have been the same coast for a hundred years, and it has never once occurred to me before this evening that that is a thing a person would have had to put in.** *If that is a copy of somewhere, then somebody stood in front of the somewhere and wrote down the bit about saying no, and then somebody here cut it into a wall in marks because a stroke is what you have. And I would like to know who was standing there. That is all. I have got a fire going and I have got nine uneven columns and a row of eleven things and a grandmother who told me she was not going to guess, and I would like to know who was standing there.*

**The shed is quiet, a man of sixty-one at a bench with his hand on a frame does not turn round, and a man of thirty-one with his weight against a bench has nothing at all to give her and gives her nothing. And nothing on that coast answered it. Not the System, which recognised a signature four hundred years old and said nothing about what it was for. Not the man of sixty-one. Not the man of thirty-one. Not the four hundred years of weeks on the wall behind her, which is a thing that cannot answer anything at all, and which has a gap in it about every nine years where a week was not kept, and which will be there on that wall in a hundred years with the same gaps in it.**

**And the close.** A bundle of three sheets of paper went up that road two hundred miles on the seventh of the ninth tide, in a cart, with nothing else in it. **It is not sealed. It is not in a locked room.
