The twenty-ninth, the seventh bell, at the First Verdict. A magistrate's question in writing takes one of eleven places on a board carrying nineteen names, and a water matter and a name matter lose theirs, and Dessin Nahl writes it on the back of the tally in her own hand. There is no furniture: Oren Sedge's guild lends four benches, the guild is out of money, and Ilyan, who has no standing in the room, carries two of them up the last thirty yards. **The sitting is a category question and fails as one: to describe the ward somebody has to say who lives in it, and Abel Kearn, thirty-five, a builder, stands up and says he lives in it and is in neither of the room's two words, and Cael Rundle says the carters are not the poor people, and both are right about somebody.** Ilyan's language fails him in the worst place yet: this is a hearing about a kind of person and a kind of person is made of words he has not got, and he says so out loud in front of eleven benches and a magistrate and it is entered, and Odile the court translator records that it has happened four times in her trade in eleven years and decides it is not her trade. **The finding comes out of a guild's term book** — nine feet long, two inches thick, four columns, the words a bridge's trade actually uses — and the word is *ward*: a duty a district owes and has not got round to, not a place anybody lives in. It is corroborated in a hundred-and-ninety-year-old standing of the wards in which eleven of fifty-five duties have been performed, in the traveller book's own front leaf, and in the one document in the case that does not use the word at all, Hanne Kolm's sworn volume, because a landlord of a lower road does the duties and nobody on that road calls them a ward. **Rell's entered finding: a duty with nobody in it is not a duty nobody does, and the word that let a woman be paid at a gate for thirty years is a word for a job, and a job is done by whoever is standing nearest.** The clean instrument is promised for the next day; the second text does not exist.

## Chapter 0062 — Payable, and Smaller Than the Truth

The thirtieth. **Form L-3, Halloway's clean sanitation instrument: eight clauses, numbered, published at the fourth bell, written on the back of a weight receipt by Bel Ordway, a weighbridge convener with a cart on the scales, in an hour and a half, and signed at the bottom *not a solicitor and not a drafter and not asked by the court*.** It closes the gate to loads in the district's direction at all times and opens it out of the district on the tide, says a person on foot goes through and *may not* be refused, and pays four hands four hours a day by name in chalk out of the district's road funds, and sets a water rate, and says the ninth bridge's *lawful traveller* does not apply to that gate. **It is good, and it is smaller than the truth, and a person can be relieved by it and diminished by it on the same afternoon.** Cael Rundle seconded it and said the cost out loud: a quarter of a penny a bucket on Weir Lane, the first tax in nineteen years, and a daughter of fourteen who is not in the traveller book. Ilyan is asked for the burden and can give four numbers and no sentence, and names the woman whose sentence he has been using. **Bryn Sallow's answer comes back by cart at the ninth bell in her own words: the rate is the right size, she should have had it thirty years ago, she is not going to argue against it in front of a court, and she is not going to pretend to be grateful either, and both go in the same line.** Rell puts the choice and does not answer it and tells the room that neither text is free. Ansel Nyle, of the low fen, comes down the road on his own business, helps a man off a wall, reads a sheet he has no standing to touch, and says the rate is the right size and that it does not carry from a man with a cart.

## Chapter 0063 — A Count in a City With No Addresses

The thirty-first and the second. Rell takes the court four miles down the fen road to count people, borrowing Harrow's reasoning and saying where she got it. **Wenna Tace asks for the tally before she is asked, so that nobody writes down a number she could have stopped.** The return: forty-one at the standpipe at the sixth bell on the twenty-eighth, nine at the ninth, nineteen by the seventh, nine names in a hundred years in a traveller book with three crossed, two hundred and forty behind a rope, four hundred and eleven loads on the flat counted from the flat by I. Vester alone and stated by him to be wrong by about forty. Tarin puts the finding that a count of a queue is not a district and asks for a number for the number of people asleep; Rundle refuses one and says why, and Rell enters the refusal as a ratepayer's. **The count fails by reproducing the problem: with no address, the court counts by place, and the list of places is the list of the ward.** A builder, a cart, a barrow, a lane, an upper street and two unnamed yards, and the magistrate's finding that a ward is five places and a duty nobody does. **Bram Orren refuses the line book and is right: a line book says what crossed and not who, and a person entered as a number on a road becomes a load, and he is the only record of what crosses and he is not a constituency.** **Bream, fifty-four, is counted, and the count finds that the only list of forty people in that district is his rent roll, unsworn, unwitnessed, and in numbers; he refuses to swear it, says why, and says a court that needs a denominator is not entitled to a name.** The cost that lands: the household column, the child of fourteen entered as a number and never named, Rundle's request to take it out, Corva Silt striking the column through and entering the loss of accuracy against her own district. Wenna Tace says out loud that the court has counted five places, and that a court that does it in front of her cannot have it done in a room in Orison in the dark.

## Chapter 0064 — The Charter That Is Worse to Write

The third to the fifth. A charter is nine sheets and has to be *held*, and being held is a thing no document in this city has ever had to be. Rell refuses to touch it so as not to be its author and disqualified from hearing appeals against it. Harrow refuses to write a word of it: he has drafted a covenant twice and both times it was good and both times he was the man nobody could argue with afterwards. **The reading is the cost: the charter is read on a bank in the mud at about a hundred and ninety people, by the reader of the district, and it fails on words.** *The persons of the low water* goes, because a water is not a person. *A resident is a person who habitually dwells* goes, because it throws a man out of its own front door, and its replacement takes forty minutes. **And there is no *haul* in it, and cannot be, and Wenna Tace makes the court enter that the largest single want of the people she is reading it to is not provided for, and why.** **The paper is two shillings and threepence for one copy, and a charter is nine copies printed, one sworn and eleven served, which is twenty-one copies and two pounds seven and threepence, paid once; the keeping of the page is about a pound eleven a quarter; and the serving of the notices is the largest of the three because there is no clause anywhere in this city for serving a district, so a clerk is paid by the hour to work out who a district is. The whole of a charter is eleven pounds four in the quarter, forty-four pounds and sixteen a year, and that is the number that goes on a pump.** **Sera writes the carrier's clause into the second half of the fourth term and it names Ilyan: he carries the terms to any district that asks, unpaid, and if he refuses the appeal is stayed until another carrier is sworn, and the recordwright tells him she chose the consequence on purpose and wants it in the record.** The fifth term has a holder and there is no holder: a man of fifty-four said no on a bank, a clerk is a body, the gate hand cannot read a page. **Bream comes four miles to ask that the lane not be in it, because every road in the charter runs to Weir Lane and a name on a page is a name for ever; Sera cuts the lane out and tells him what it costs, that a district can be appealed about and a lane cannot and the lane is where the people are, and Bream says then it is a worse charter, I asked for it anyway, and do not thank me.** The System renders the draft and the fifth term has no holder entered. Sera gives Ilyan a night, and says she knows exactly what it looks like and that it looks like a man being given a thing to say at a moment.

## Chapter 0065 — A Burden of His Own

The sixth to the eleventh, on the low water. Tarin does not come and says no in writing: a neutral boundary is for a field and a man on a bank is a man on a bank. **Ilyan asks the gate hand for her hours and is refused, and is refused on the reason that has held for thirty years: a number makes it a wage, a wage wants a rate, a rate wants a body, and there is no body, so the only thing left in the gap is her.** He argues her out of her own protection in one sentence, and the sentence is not about rights: *the whole point of a number is that it gives a person something to be angry at somebody specific about,* and he tells her before she agrees that she will be owed by two people on the bench of four who sleep on Weir Lane. **He counts four hours from the fifth bell to the ninth in a load-carrier's figures, with Wenna Tace holding the clock and the gate hand calling her hours, and partway through the fourth hour the leg goes.** It does not go as a subtraction. It thins. The weight in it changes and becomes his own. **The discovery: the afterimage does not clear when he rests. It clears when he is inside a burden of his own.** He had it exactly backwards for eleven days. It is not a cure; it is a practice with a four-hour limit in it, and when he stops it comes back in a minute and comes back worse. **The token is reached once, on the sill-stone, in fifty minutes, and the third layer resolves: the thing in his nose is not a memory of a low water and never was a smell, it is a rendering, and the rendering is a picture of an oak gate seen from above and behind at two hundred feet in a light with no source, the same picture every time, and a rendering made for somebody has a vantage, and the vantage is a place he has never been.** It comes back with a fourth layer, and the fourth layer is a door at the same height and the same distance, and the vantage has not moved. He has traded four days of smell for one question he cannot put down. **Bryn Sallow then says the cost out loud, in front of two people: she has got a number on a slate that only exists when a man from a bridge is standing on that bank, and he has just made her thirty years into a job for a man who has a leg.** The ninth bridge's seat comes out of its fortnight on the third, the thirty-fourth day of the unloaded period, and the span takes its first load, and Ilyan finds out from a cart.

## Chapter 0066 — The Price of the Telling

The twelfth to the fourteenth. Two clerks arrive with instruments: Ottla Prenn, and **Hulda Vane, fifty-one, twenty-six years, clerk of Coldwater, an Orison chamber district of three spans and a bench of five.** Coldwater adopted the ninth-bridge settlement on the third of the month by copying it out of a district book, on a sealed letter from a clerk at the weighhouse with no name on it saying *this is the one they are all using, if you have not got it, ask* — lawfully, in an afternoon, without reading it, and she admits it in the rain and says she would do it again. She then **reads it after, alone, at night, finds the trespasser sentence, and writes a second clause into her own instrument**, and C-4 closes a slipway lane to loaded vehicles. **Perdy Sill, thirty-eight, of a coal yard, is the template's second body and she has her own want: a cart.** The instrument is not shutting a lane. It has made a man with a sledge sixteen shillings where there were four, and the person it is about is not her and there is no word in it she can take hold of and shake. She ends by asking what a wheelbarrow has to do with any of it. The telling takes a day and a half and produces a page in five hands: nine terms adopted, term six not noticed and not adopted, term three not adopted in its effect, and the ninth bridge's tenth term put in Coldwater's own mouth. **Cael Rundle, driving the cart, says the true thing about why it was worth a day and a half: it is not the page, it is that a bench of five sat down in a room and minuted in five hands that they had not read a thing they had adopted, and in four years there will be a person in that district who reads a paper before she signs it, and she will be able to name the afternoon and the clerk.** **Meridith Vale pays: four pounds and eleven out of the ninth bridge's district road fund, in a clerk's entry with a date and a column for the other thing, and the other thing is the fourth bridge's four props at ninety-six, out of a fund that has a hundred and four pounds and has just lost fifteen.** The fund is four hundred and ten a year; two hands under a deck every change is two hundred and ten. On the way back they pass the fourth bridge, which has four square holes in it and the holes are twenty-five days old, and **Anhalt refuses the cart and refuses the district's new props and says the account is open and on his side of the road**, and that he will not write it on a nail.

## Chapter 0067 — What the Appeal Was For

The sixteenth, the fourth bell, in the open air. The serving worked: four bells, two carts, fourteen names and one seal, and the fact that nobody had ever done it before. **Corva Silt stands eleven minutes in a lane deciding whether a notice may be left with a person who has not asked for one, leaves it on the sill, knocks, does not wait, writes the time.** **The seal was served on a desk with a stamp and no clerk and no incoming index and nothing came back; Dessin Nahl enters the date, and the court takes no view, and nobody asks the court's witness about it.** **Maren Kett is the named advocate, because a claimant with limited standing may only be heard with one and nobody wanted it, and his first nine words are that he is not the man's friend.** The condition disputed is one sentence. Nahl says reception is a thing nine benches would do for nothing; Harrow, the court's witness in any appeal, says a bench may hold a paper it owes nothing to and the mischief is not receiving. **Kett puts the condition: a bench which has received the instrument is not bound by it and may not enforce any part of it against any person unless it has adopted that part by its own act and its own minute — which is the citation-is-not-adoption entry of the twenty-first, and which is precedent against his client's wider request.** The court enters the limit first and on the face of the record: the settlement binds no span but its own, *on this span* is the court's own correct drafting, it will not be widened, and the claimant knew it and filed anyway, in nine words, and nobody improved them. **Then the court enters the thing nobody asked for: a bench that has received the instrument shall answer, in public, within eleven days, whether it holds any page a person affected may swear to in its own room. The court has no power to compel the answer and says so in the same line.** Harrow says a duty to answer is the smallest power he has ever given out and he is frightened of it, and that a right nobody had used is the only thing in the case that ran. Kett then argues against his own client for an hour. Rell says the real result is that a city has now established in public, in eleven hands, that it holds nothing a poor person can swear to, and that is not a remedy and is the first thing in nineteen years. The choice is set for the seventeenth, and a name matter and a water matter go back onto a board with twenty-two names on it.

## Chapter 0068 — Four Terms and a Fifth

The seventeenth, the fourth bell, the volume's climax. **Bryn Sallow does not come, and the reason is entered first and in her own words: if the gate hand is not at the gate the gate is shut, and the gate being shut is a flat nobody has counted since the twentieth and about two hundred people, and she is not going to be the reason a court in Orison decides a thing about her in a room she is not in.** Bel Ordway argues the clean instrument, including the price of his own two seasons of carting, and says the whole of what it does not give anybody is that there is nobody on the other side of it. Rundle votes for it in full knowledge of his own bucket. Wenna Tace argues the charter: a rule you cannot read is not unfair, it is *nobody*, and a quarter of a penny a bucket with no door out of it is not a small thing. **Rell votes her own weight for the clean instrument, in writing, signed, for ever, and says the rest of the case out loud: it is the right answer, and a rule with no person in it has no one who can be asked to say no.** **Sera Quill says the part that is against Ilyan: the fifth term has no holder because she has not asked one person who could hold it, and she will enter her own name against that and it will cost her licence.** Harrow asks the question no one has asked: *who in this room reads.* **Nobody thought of asking her.** The fifth term's cost is that **a holder may not be a party, may not appeal, may not appear in support of one** — so the woman who asked *who do I write to* takes the page and gives up the appeal, and asks for both halves of that sentence to be entered. The positions: three for the clean instrument, one who does not know, a magistrate's own weight, and Ilyan's submission, which is nine words — *I am for the charter, and the reason is that a gate is not the problem* — and which is out-argued by his own advocate for the rest of the hour. **Nell Ambrey enters the cost herself: the bench's time goes to appeals and her sanitation complaint is not heard this year, because it is in her gift only on a day when she has nothing else.** The charter is made at the ninth bell with a person in the fifth term, and the carrier's clause stands with Ilyan's name in it, accepted in nine words, and Kett tells him on the step that he has made himself findable, which took a month and required no good argument, and that this city is going to spend ten years finding out which of those two things it wants.

## Chapter 0069 — What It Cost by Morning

The eighteenth and the twenty-first. **The rate goes on the barrel lid at the gate: half a farthing a bucket at the standpipe, the first tax in nineteen years, and half of what the clean instrument wanted, because the gate is shut and the cartage that feeds the district's road fund has been nil since the fourteenth.** The fund stands at eighty-nine pounds; the charter costs forty-four a year, which is eleven pounds and four in the quarter; the bench sets the rate at the amount that produces it and not a unit more. **Rundle reads it at the bank and says, before anybody else does, that it is the right rate, and then that his daughter of fourteen walks to that pump every morning, and that he asked for a line in a district book on which an hour of work could be paid to a person by name and was given a date, and that the line is full of another woman's name and the bucket is his.** **The props go back on the same cart on the eighteenth at four pounds and no new ones are bought this year**, and the account is carried with a deficiency of eleven pounds four, entered against the district and not against any keeper. **Nobody sent for Anhalt; Corva Silt went, with two clauses in writing, the second of which asks what he wants.** He answers properly: not a page, the account carried, the eleven pounds four against the district, the four words off the frame and into a book, and the last thing, which is that he does not want a page and has been the man who was not on the bridge for twenty-eight days and would like that on a page. **Corva Silt asks to be relieved of the print authority for the fifth time, is refused, and says she will not ask a sixth.** **The first appeal under the charter is sworn at the sixth bell on the bank: Bryn Sallow appeals the office of gate hand.** There is no clause for serving a district; nine hours in a clerk's office find that you serve the person who keeps the district's book, which in Halloway is the clerk who printed H-9, and she serves her own district's appeal and enters that she asked to be relieved and is not. The appeal is **refused**, on the correct rule that a person may not refuse an office and keep it — and then the charter works, because four of its five terms are a bridge's: **Oakes sets the district's own rate, a third of a haul rate, on the balance of the tide from the eighteenth, six and a half hours a day, in chalk, by name, and the thirty years is entered as owed by the district and not paid.** She says it is over a hundred thousand hours and she will not have them counted, and she says the cost herself: the four who set that rate include two people she buys salt off and has lent a bucket to, and she entered her refusal before the rate was set and the order is deliberate. **Nobody sent for Bream either, and Corva Silt sent, and he said no again on the eighteenth to the same question he had refused on a bank a fortnight earlier — a roll says who owes him and a roll is a page with a seal on it — before anybody had got as far as asking for anything else, and then swore a serving list of forty names standing, in twelve minutes, reading them out himself into a page the clerk held up and did not copy into the bench's book, and reading it over once more before the folder closed. It is the first thing he has ever sworn. He got one wrong, apologised to a man of nineteen who has paid a shilling a week since he was fourteen, and asked for the correction to be entered. The cost: forty people in a lane found out that their landlord's mouth had turned them into a page, he is a body now, and if the lane is ever contested he is the first name anybody will ask for. Nobody offered him a shilling of the money that never comes back and he asked for that to be entered too.** The clean instrument goes back into a drawer with a note on the back in Ordway's hand and no note on the front.

## Chapter 0070 — The Fifth Thing He Entered

The eighteenth to the twenty-ninth. **The eleven-day answers come in: nine of eleven answered, seven saying they hold nothing, two naming a hundred-and-sixty-year-old book neither of which anybody can swear to, one answering *yes* — a standing authority of preservation that holds every instrument printed in this city for four years, where no person has ever asked — and one bench not answering at all, which Rell enters as not being in breach of anything.** The charter is five days old, working, and not secure. **A form arrives on the twenty-second with EXCEPTION at the top of it in a bigger letter than any other word on the page, un-ordered, un-stoppable, and a court has no power over a form.** Corva Silt nails it to the gate board with a line on the back saying the word is not in the charter, and Wenna Tace reads it out at the pump and tells a woman that the word came from a hearing. **Bram has sent the charter's terms to Wen Lask at Carrisand, on his own initiative, and told a magistrate before anybody could stop him, and says the cost was the only thing he had: in about a year that gate is a gate a man from a city has written to about a rule, and that is the only reason anybody in that family would ever have heard of us.** **Her letter comes back on the twenty-ninth with one question: *does your paper say anything about a road with nobody in it.*** Nobody answers it. **On the twenty-second a line comes out under the fifth term of the copy in the holder's page — *Continuity preserves the record against the holder* — for the sixth time, with the word changed again.** Harrow refuses for the fourth time, in the same words, and says he will refuse on the seventh, and enters it as an unentered line and names nobody. **Ilyan is on the floor of a room over a pump for a minute and a half with a fourth layer in his nose that is a door, and Wenna Tace finds him and says *you have got somebody's leg* and then, *it is not mine, I have got a barrow's worth in my arms and it is not the same and I know the difference now*, and gets him a chair without asking.** The fifth thing he entered is named with its size: two private sequences, the precedent, the first appeal, and the carrier's clause, and the fifth is the only one anybody can call out and ask to see him, and it cost a district a day and a half of a cart. **Sera says the two things that are hers: a quarter of a sentence on a facing leaf that says a refusal's entry is *open to the ward*, which she has been reading a place into for a fortnight because a place was what she had, and which is a duty and not a place, so there is now a district where a page is open to a person and it is not the same sentence and she is not going to say it is; and a family claim six years old that needs a page that may not exist, which she has not looked for in a month because she is holding a charter.** **The review is the fourth morning bell of the twenty-seventh of the third month after the ruling, and from the twenty-ninth of this month, where the batch ends, that is four weeks.** The batch ends on an unanswered question from a good road.

## Chapter 0071 — The First Ask

The thirtieth of the month. A folded paper arrives on the ninth bridge counter on a cart that was not coming for anything: the bench of four of **Nethergill** — eleven miles from the bridge, seven from Halloway — asking, under the second half of the fourth term, for the terms of the Halloway charter, **and for the paper itself, and it cannot pay for the paper.** Four names and a mark, a wedge-and-chain seal nobody here has seen, and a wet ring over three of the names. **Corva Silt will not certify a bench eleven miles off** and says the mechanism does not know it is the mechanism. The only reader in that district is Wenna Tace, and a holder is not a party, so he walks up the lane on foot and asks her to read it as a reader. **The first ask cannot be verified: a person who cannot read cannot tell whether the paper he has been given is the paper that was asked for.** The clause as it stands: carried to any district that asks, at his own cost, no pay, one named carrier, nobody else may carry them, and if he refuses or is unable or is more than four miles off the appeal is stayed. **Past four miles he is not obliged; past four miles it is a favour, and a favour cannot be entered.** He walks the eleven miles anyway and the leg is his own for all of them and stops forty yards past the end of the lane. **The continuity line is not revealed: the holder has not read the line under the fifth term of the copy in her own room, and she explains that a charter ends at the fifth term, and tells him not to read it to her tonight.** A boy of thirteen meets him on the lane: *are you the one that walks down with paper.* Hester Vole, thirty-eight, a cowman's wife, is waiting in a lit window with a book under her arm, and asks him what he is for, because she has been the door in that district since she was eight and nobody has ever come up the lane.

## Chapter 0072 — What the Word Does to a Man With a Season

The first of the month, at a bench of four's table in Nethergill: a sealed, numbered, un-ordered form summarising the Halloway charter **with EXCEPTION at the top of it in a bigger letter than anything else on the page.** Nobody ordered it, nobody sent it, and a man in the lane has already been told by the reader at the bar that the paper in his hand is the rule he is living inside. **Hal Dreeve, thirty-four, hires out barrows, has put his name down for a season of four months at a weighhouse, and is refused in a yard in ninety seconds by a foreman holding up the word.** **The batch's discovery: the word is not a label on a rule, it is a claim that the rule is temporary — and the charter has no end on it, because it was made out of a bridge, and a bridge does not end.** The clean instrument that lost does expire, at the third sitting of next quarter, and the word is correct and old. Then a second visit to the yard. **Parnell Slee, forty-eight, nineteen years at the weighhouse, is honest about the second reason: two seasons is what a man does at a weighhouse, and the factor will not put a man on for four months out of a season he cannot use, and that is true with or without the paper. What the paper did was give him a sentence instead of the sentence he had, *I do not know you*, and he would like somebody to send the shelf back.** The print shop filled a space in a docket. **Ilyan can follow all of it — the six things the form is assembled out of, all of them correct — and can do nothing whatever about any of it, because the charter is Halloway's, the form is a form, and the weighhouse is not a party to anything.** The reason he may not speak is a rule, and the rule is correct. He has four minutes of work: a definition, a direction, and a woman with a barrow. *Nobody comes out here.*

## Chapter 0073 — What They Actually Wanted

The first of the month, in a cold room in Nethergill. **A bar with a penny in it and nobody at it is not a gate, and that is the whole of the trouble at Lowgill:** a waggon of hedge off the Orison road goes round the chain and puts six feet of a load on the chapel flat, and the bar is not a gate, and the tollkeeper was a waggon of draught. **Jonas Pyle, sixty-three, convenor, says what the district wanted: a person to come and stand in the room, once, so that the bench knows what it is looking at.** He has driven the second reading of nine sheets to a man who cannot read them and has understood about one word in four. **Ilyan's answer to *who are you* is the worst thing he has said in this case, and it is true: he is not a bench, a party, a body, or a district; his standing is confined to one matter about a bridge eleven miles away; and the whole of his use is that the fourth term names him and he is the reason the appeal is not stayed. Jonas Pyle minutes it and says a true minute is always the bad one.** Then a stranger in the room offers to carry the terms and is refused in person, because a clause that cannot be broken is a gate. **And the copy Nethergill already had — brought down the lane on a cart by a drover on the twenty-seventh of the month before, with no seal and no clerk — is short of the second half of the fourth term, because a clerk does not copy a man's name into a district's paper when a district has not asked for a man. Hester Vole finds it, not he, by laying two sheets on a table.** The thing that gets copied is the rule; the thing that gets left off is the person; and the thing left off is always the part with a consequence in it. Six people copy out a fair complete copy by candlelight from the seventh bell and swear it themselves, and a drover signs as a witness because he wants to be on a page. **The only complete copy of the charter of the district of Halloway in the world made in a kitchen is in a kitchen four miles up a lane, binds nobody, and cannot be come back for by anybody.**

## Chapter 0074 — A Burden in the Body, Taken

The second of the month, on the low end of a lane. Eleven barrow-loads of stone off a chapel flat, four hundred yards each, with a woman of fifty-one who kept the chapel book and did not ask him. **The whole job is about two and three-quarter cart-loads, about two shillings and sixpence, and the fund has elevenpence in it and it is promised to the candles.** The ninth bridge's tenth term says a district which pays a favour has learned to rule by habit, and the reason he is on that flat is a sentence he said in a room, and the term applies to him because there is nobody to hire him and he refused to be hired and said why out loud. The leg is his own all morning; the token goes away on the sixth load and the low water and the leg come back; it goes again in the ninth load on the flat with a barrow in his hands and he is watched by about nine people and nobody helps. **His left hand opens in four places and is a scar at the base of his thumb in four years, and there is no line anywhere in this city that pays a man for a hand.** The token, once, on a chapel flat, at the fourth bell. For fifty minutes the four layers clear — and the picture has a floor. **The vantage is a room: boards, a table, a lamp with the wick down, a window with weather on the wrong side, four people at the table, and one of them about forty-four with her back to him and her right foot up on something. It is a gate hand, and he has never seen her at a table, and the vantage has not moved.** **The cost is the words. From the second of the month until the sixth he could see a line of writing and could not get sense out of it, and when Corva Silt hands him four sheets and asks him to read the fourth line, he says *I cannot read that*, and she sits down and puts the pen down and does not enter it, because a clerk who enters a man as a device is the mechanism, and the cost is that a second head now holds it and it is not on a page.** He read the charter to a bench of four in a cold room and got *holder* wrong. And on the fifth, at a pump, about two hundred people, Wenna Tace reads all nine sheets aloud for the first time and says so before she starts and asks for it to be entered, and a man at the back asks whether the terms travel, and she says they do, and he asks can she come, and she says no, and the reason is the fifth term.

## Chapter 0075 — The Door

The sixth to the seventh, in a room over a pump. Five people come in a day and none arranged it. **A man of about twenty-six has had a notice eleven days and cannot read it and does not know whether a notice may be left on a door; a man of forty has a carter's chalk line and wants a rate changed; a boy of nineteen has heard the word and wants to know whether his wage stops, and the answer is that the paper saying it ends has nothing in it about a wage stopping at all and his wage is a carter's and this district has no line for it.** Then the fourth. **A man of fifty wants an appeal and needs a person named in it, and a holder may not be named in one, and she is the only person in the district who can read — so the rule that keeps her out of the door is the rule that puts her in the middle of everything.** Her finding, in the page, in the middle: *five people came to this room and none of them had asked to come to it, and I am the result of it, and a holder cannot be relieved of the fifth term by anybody, including me, including a court, and that is not a protection.* Hollis Dune walks seven miles from Nethergill to ask the question that has been in a record for three weeks: **a rate for a haul, of which the district has a hundred and fifty in eleven months and no book.** The chain: a holder is not a party and a party is a person who asks and asking for a rate is asking. **She asks anyway, and states the cost three times and in order: the rate will be set by the person who wants it, she must read it aloud at the pump every quarter, and no person in the district can appeal it — because she wrote the clause, and cut the barrow out of the charter herself, and the absence is hers.** Her fourth entry in the page, in her own hand, in the middle: the cost of the first ask, the fact that the terms travel and there is now a reader who is not her, a want for eleven months written down because a thing she wants is a thing she can be used with, and **a second barrow — the first time she has said it to anybody.**

## Chapter 0076 — What Thirty Years Is Worth, Wrongly

The ninth to the fourteenth. A bench of four cannot carry a debt without a figure and cannot get one, because the woman whose debt it is refused to have it counted. **Ilyan has six days of a real working tide, and multiplies them up, and says the three things that are wrong with it before he does it.** The figure: **a hundred and thirteen thousand four hundred hours at threepence the hour is one thousand four hundred and seventeen pounds and ten shillings — six years of the district's road fund and thirty-two years of the cost of the charter.** Wrong three ways. **Bryn Sallow gives the first correction herself and unasked: the working tide of the turn is one day in seven and the week comes to about sixty-three hours, not seventy-three and a half — which takes out sixteen thousand two hundred hours and two hundred and two pounds and ten shillings, about two years of a lane of forty roofs at a shilling a week.** The second cannot be found: threepence the hour is not a rate this district has; it is a day's figure of nineteen and a halfpence divided by the six and a half hours the district does not pay for, and there is no rate in this city from thirty years ago, and the third of a haul rate has not moved since the year a woman on this street had her mother buried. The third is that thirty years of three hundred and sixty days is a number he made up. It is entered in three hands in the district's book, with the refusal on the same page and a line saying the figure is wrong by two hundred and two pounds and ten shillings and by an amount nobody can name, and that it was not written by a clerk and that the person it is owed to refused it and cannot be compelled and cannot be overruled. **On the bank she tells him what a number is: a claim, and a claim wants somebody to answer it, and she has gone thirty years without being a claim.** She makes him count again with the line down the middle of the slate, and on the fourteenth writes both sets of figures side by side and says *a person is not a measuring instrument, I have said that on a field and it goes on a page and it is still true.* **He enters the sixth thing he cannot un-enter, with its size, in his own hand: a figure of thirty years' work entered without consent, wrong by at least two hundred and two pounds and ten shillings and by an unnameable amount; he is now a witness in one more matter and a witness can be produced; nobody has thanked him and he did not ask.** Sera reads it back, does not tidy it, and says the reason out loud once: a man who enters six things against his own name is not a careful man, he is a man frightened that if he waits for a better room he will not say them at all, and he is right to be frightened and wrong about the reason.

## Chapter 0077 — What It Costs to Have Said No in Public

A clerk of the Orison chamber has become a person who is stopped talking to in a corridor, because when a person says *where can I swear this*, four benches in nine say *not here*, faster than the question, with the expression a person makes when they have read something of their own in a public record. **Dessin Nahl walks eleven miles and pays two of the six days out of her own, and enters the price in the same place as the answer, because a thing with no cost in it looks like evidence.** She does not ask nine benches to hold a page. **She asks one bench one question, on one matter, on a day the bench appoints.** Fallowditch, a bench of three, its clerk Perditta Nye, twenty-nine, three years in the post, answers in four lines and **withdraws its answer of the eighteenth in writing, because a bench that will not say who it did that for is a bench that decided the answer was free — and the who is a woman called Adela Cray.** The hearing on the eleventh, in the bench's own room over a shop that was a chandler's, two of three present, the landlord not there and not a party: the agreement is real, it is entered, it is dated, it is signed — and a fortnight's notice is between a woman and a man and no bench in this city hears that. **It is the first time in nineteen years this city has had a hearing that produced nothing and a room that produced everything.** The other six are reported, not staged; three of the seven have been asked by a person in their own district to produce a page and have had to say, from their own letter, that there was nothing in their room. **A public answer is a price: a bench that has told the truth about itself has priced a room in its own district at one answer in eleven days.** In Fallowditch a landlord puts the bench's own letter face up on a table and says he is not doing anything wrong, and reads it out, which he did not have to, and does not want it known that he read it out. And then the tenth question, put up a stair four miles from the First Verdict: **will the standing authority of preservation receive and keep a page sworn by a person in a district?** Answered in the same office hand for the second time in fifteen days: **no — it holds what is printed, a sworn page is not printed, an authority that keeps records of persons is a bench, and an office with no person in it is not a door, it is a shelf — and it has said twice that it can prove a rule was printed and cannot hold a person.** Nahl's own finding in her own book and in no record: a city that has established it is empty has established that the one thing it is not empty of is a shelf; anybody who needs a page sworn can now name the one desk that said yes; the leverage is real, it is on a desk with a stamp, and it cannot be used, and in about a year somebody will try anyway and there will be a form about it. Four sentences from a magistrate in the well of the court: the duty to answer has cost a woman a room and got a bench a hearing, and the scarcity has made a public answer into a price and one desk into a monopoly, and neither on purpose, and a city that has said out loud that it is empty is a city in which a person with a document and nowhere to put it has a problem no bench can hear.

## Chapter 0078 — Twenty-One Names

The fourteenth. **Abel Kearn's matter has been on the docket board since the eighth of last month and has never had a place, and the wall that is going is a hundred and forty-one years old and in a book in Fallowditch, which has publicly said it holds nothing anybody can swear to.** A timber merchant stacking elm against it is not a criminal, is right, and will not be repaid for the crack. Nobody can stop him: the court has no power over a yard, a district cannot move a boundary or swear a survey that is not in its own book, and Fallowditch's answer is true. So Kearn put his name on a board, and then worked out what a name on a board is. **A name on the board is not a thing that gets you heard. A name on the board is a name in the record, and a record is a thing a clerk can come back with, and he has no holder and no page and cannot swear anything, and he has gained nothing from a name on a piece of chalk except the ability to be produced.** He says it in the open air, standing four feet inside the rail with chalk in his hand, having told the room in advance that he is not asking to be heard. He asks permission to do it before he does it. **He rubs his own name off the board with his thumb, which takes most of a minute on a board that has been gone over about four hundred times, and the board goes to twenty-one names, and the eleven places do not change, because a place is made by a matter being ready and not by a name being on a board.** Nahl enters it in her own hand, moves the matter up a place, and he tells her to leave it where it is. Then the mechanism nobody has: **a matter is heard when a document can be sworn, a document is sworn where it is kept or in a room that holds it, and there is no clause anywhere in this city that allows a person to carry a sworn page four miles to a room.** Hollis Dune prices it — two shillings and threepence for nine sheets, read out twice at a pump to two hundred people, done in a kitchen, unpaid. Rell says what a hauler's room over a pump would do with a document, and then his last question, six words long, which he could not get in front of the court a month ago: **can I be counted as a place.** No — and the reason given in the plainest words she has: a place is not a person, and a court that has just spent two months taking places out of its arithmetic cannot put one back in, and if it did then in about a year there would be eleven places with names in them and no documents, and the way to be heard would be to be a location. Then she enters what he actually did, in her own words, as not a ruling, not a precedent, and binding nobody including him.

## Chapter 0079 — Nine Yards With No Name In Them

The fifteenth to the eighteenth. **A man with a cart has been lifting the chain at Lowgill bar and lifting the pennies out of the slot into a tin under his seat for ten weeks, and there is nothing anybody can do, because a bar with a slot and a chain and nobody at it is not a gate.** Rennick Hale, forty-one, says so in a cold room to a magistrate's clerk, in four long passages, and says he has not spent a penny of it and has eleven pounds of arrears in a house coming free in eight weeks, and asks for the four pounds and six to be accounted for under his own name while he is standing there saying it. The word *ward* is on a post at the end of that lane, painted once, in guild colours, a hundred and ninety years old, and nobody in the room needs it, and it is described correctly once by a man who does not know the word. **The Nine Yards: sixty-one people, nine gates, one well with a bucket, no district, paying Nethergill's rate for a hundred and forty-one years for a bar they do not own, because a rate followed a road and a boundary followed a hedge.** And a matter comes before the First Verdict, because a carter is a person and a place is not, and a carter who had come up to ask about a boundary heard eleven benches say out loud that they hold nothing and asked a question of his own. **The matter is against nobody; the respondents are a bench of four and no keeper of Lowgill bar, and there is no keeper and none has ever been sworn in this city. The place is entered as a fact in the pleadings and is not a party, because a place is not a person.** Service is made by nailing the notice to the post, in the open air, and the court accepts it, and there is no clause in any instrument in this case for notice to a place. **Merrin Coke, twenty-nine, with sixty-one people in charcoal marks on a shutter, walks four miles to Nethergill and seven to a room over a pump and is told she can be read and not held, and asks for a page and is told there is one reader in one district at a time and a place is not a district.** Ilyan offers to hold it. **Sera Quill refuses, and the reason is the whole of the chapter: hold a page anywhere and you are a person in a district, and a holder is not a carrier, and a carrier is the only person who may carry the terms, and there is no instrument in this city that says how a second carrier is sworn in — so the day he opens a page the appeal in Halloway is stayed for about four hundred people, and nobody in this city will be able to tell him he did it. She entered that she did not see it when she wrote the fifth term, and that a man cannot be a door in a lane and the only door at the same time.** Harrow enters that the court has no power over the charter and takes no view, and that this is the third procedure he has invented and none of them is a power. And the four pounds and six goes back into the slot in eleven days, in a cart, with a docket in a clerk's hand, one penny at a time, in front of nine people, by the man who took it out — the only thing in the chapter that got fixed, and it got fixed because a man with a cart is a person, and nobody put anywhere the answer to how long a tin would have stayed under a seat in a lane with no address in it.

## Chapter 0080 — What the Review Bought

The fourth morning bell of the twenty-seventh of the third month, at the span, on public notice, in the open air, with a cart-board across two trestles. **Not called early**, and Tarin Keel's written refusal is in the file: a callable early requires a named reading, he has read it at every change since the third of the second month, and he is a field man and there is no field. The span has carried at every change for fifty-four days; the band was inside it on a light load at mark and a half and a heavy one at two and three, every figure taken with a moving cart; the pale permanent set has not moved. The fourth bridge has been propped for thirty-nine days by four old timbers that have already stood under the ninth bridge's west seat for a fortnight. **The fund, in Meridith Vale's hand: four hundred and ten a year; two hands under a deck every change, two hundred and ten; the telling, fifteen; the charter, forty-four pounds and sixteen; the fourth bridge's four props, ninety-six. Remainder after all four: forty-four pounds and four shillings. Short of the props by fifty-one pounds and sixteen.** Oren Sedge says the guild cannot take half a hand and that a count taken by one man is a count and not a record, and that he is choosing between a bridge that has moved and a bridge with holes in it and will not call that a principle. **The review buys four new props, takes the old four out of the fourth bridge's deck, leaves that deck open in the weather, and reduces the under-deck watch to one hand for the year — and the man who loses the second hand is T. Blore, twenty-two, eleven months under a deck, and he is entered in the disposition, which is the only name in it, and nobody had entered him anywhere.** Vale enters that he did not ask the keeper of the fourth bridge and would not have known what to do with the answer. Then he is produced in person, reads the two copies of his own sequence out loud to about ninety people — nobody in Orison has ever heard of the game — and names what the record cost and who paid it: Sallow, Tace, Bream, Anhalt, Silt, Ambrey, Rundle, Kearn, Hester Vole, and Tam Blore first of all. **Wen Lask's question is read out and answered: the terms say something about a person and nothing about a road, and the court has no power over a road in a region with no district, and the answer is entered as not being the answer, and the road is not touched.** Then Harrow's fourth procedure, invented in public and named as his own: how a carrier is sworn in, where an instrument says *until another carrier is sworn in that district* and no instrument says how — and it is not a remedy, because a right that costs a district money, has no standing outside one bench, and depends on a man of thirty-one being within four miles is a favour with a seal on it. **The continuity line appeared for the seventh time and was refused for the fifth, in the same words, nobody named, and the holder has not read it.** And on the first of the fourth month the quarter's rate is read at a pump: half a farthing a bucket, six and a half hours a day in chalk, the thirty years entered as owed and not paid, the figure of a thousand four hundred and seventeen pounds and ten shillings with the line that says it is wrong, and **a new rate for a haul, at three times the district's own rate, two thirds short, asked for by the holder, unappealable by anybody, arrears entered as owed and not paid because the district has no money.** A boy of nineteen works out that a hauler's barrow is a quarter of a cart and the man on the fourth is a quarter of a man, and she says that is right and that she cut it out of this charter herself and it is going in the next one. And she tells him the question the batch ends on: **there is no procedure in this city for what happens to a page when the holder cannot keep it, she is twenty-nine, she hauls two hundred loads a year, and a court is going to sit in about four years and ask how an appeal survives in a district that is being squeezed, and the honest answer is *as long as a woman of twenty-nine can still read and haul at the same time*.** And in a clerk's own book in a chamber in Orison there is a question nobody has read: the only district in this city with a carrier is the only district that does not need one.

## Chapter 0081 — The Deck That Is Open in the Weather

The second of the fourth month at the fourth morning bell, on the fourth bridge's deck, which has had four square holes in it since the twentieth of the first month and has been open to the weather since the twenty-seventh of the month before last, six days. **Wist Anhalt, forty-nine, twenty-nine years in the post, is sitting on it with a piece of chalk he does not use, and nobody asked him what he thinks, and the new timbers bought at ninety-six pounds are lying in the mud on the north bank because they were cut on the road above the fen three days ago and there is no water in the river to float a timber up to a deck.** He walks a loaded peat cart over the second hole at the pace he has walked that bridge since before the carter's father had a cart, and he says the deck is open and he is not going to stop a load, and he says he is the man on it and nobody asked. Down the fen road a letter has come from **Meridith Vale, clerk of the ninth bridge's district**, which is a bill and not a notice: the charter of the district of Halloway costs forty-four pounds and sixteen a year out of a fund of four hundred and ten, a little under a ninth of it, and the year as set out at the review leaves forty-four pounds and four shillings, so the charter costs twelve shillings more than what is left. **One hand under a deck is a hundred and five pounds and came back on the day of the review, and it is in a different line, and a clerk cannot move a line into another line without a bench's minute — so the ninth bridge's district has a hundred and five pounds it cannot put against the charter.** They will pay it twelve shillings over the line. A bench of four in a room over a pump decides what to do with a figure it entered and cannot discharge: **keep it, and enter what the law costs, not what it costs us** — four hundred and ten, forty-four pounds sixteen, the figure of the thirty years, a hundred and sixty-year-old book, and the two hundred and forty people Halloway has never counted itself. *A debt that cannot be paid and cannot be refused is entered here as a debt this district owes.* On a wet fen road Ilyan says he is sorry about the props to a keeper who tells him they were the ninth bridge's, notices that he has been standing there trying to think of something else, and is sent down the road.

## Chapter 0082 — Half a Day

The third of the fourth month, the second bell, nineteen miles off at the cut banks. **Oren Sedge has walked it himself, because a letter from a trade comes at the change and a man on a deck reads it standing up and takes it for a summons about something else, and because the guild is out of money, has lent four benches to a court it has no standing in, and has six crews that have not voted for anything.** He could not come before: the count is taken at every change and if he is not on the deck at the change there is no count. **T. Blore, twenty-two, eleven months at the west end, has been under the deck at the cut banks for two months working for Harl Mecken, sixty, who has had nine apprentices.** Sedge tells him the disposition, and that the second hand is gone, and that **his name is the only name in it — and that a man called Vester put it there at a review and Sedge did not, and would have taken the hand just the same if he had not been in the room.** Blore says the figure for him: four hours a day at the ward rate, a hundred and five pounds a year, worked out from a mate's arithmetic in the winter. The resistance is not the loss — **it is that the loss is recoverable-looking: a fund with forty-four pounds and four shillings in it would pay him back about five months, a district can be written to, a chamber can be asked, and a man can be sent for who is not a bench and cannot make anybody answer.** He works out that there is no job either: one hand under the deck at the ninth bridge this year, no cartage on the fen road, four square holes in the fourth. Sedge tells him the true thing nobody has told him — *you were not let go, the crew list went to one name in the winter because the guild ran out of money, and the review took a second hand out of a fund and bought four timbers with it* — and says it so that a man of twenty-six does not carry a false story about a trade. The chapter ends on a shipping docket written in the dark in a room over a smithy: **T. BLORE. FOUR HOURS A DAY. ONE OF TWO SEASONS.**

## Chapter 0083 — A Right With No Door In It

The fourth to the sixth of the fourth month. **Merrick Vaunt, nineteen, a lighterman's mate who was taught letters at nine and can get most of a second sentence, comes to the room over a pump because Corva Silt sent him, and he is there because he has been told two different answers to *what is a haul* and he is the one people ask.** The chapter's discovery is a hole in a rate nobody had looked at: **the district's own rate is a rate for a *load*, and this bench has never once written down what a load is a rate for, and nobody has ever been asked** — and so the hundred and fifty hauls have no figure at all, and Corva Silt has gone over the entry four times and cannot make it come out. **Wenna Tace asks the question: may the rate for a haul be appealed by any person. The answer is no** — it was set by a bench of four, the only person who asked for it is the person who asked, a holder may not be a party or appeal or appear in support or be named, and no person outside the district has standing. And the other half: the pump is a public place, and it is not a hearing, because a convenor of about sixty is not going to call a pump a hearing in a district with no money and then have *Halloway* put against *a hearing* in a column in four years. **The shape of the whole thing: an appeal is a thing a person can carry; a rate is a thing a person has to be inside of; and the machinery in this city has a procedure for the first and not one word for the second.** The cost of asking is that the answer goes in the district's own book and will be read out at the pump four times a year, and she did not get the money, she got the sentence. **On the fifth she takes on a second reader — a boy of nineteen who is not a holder and is not a clerk and is not paid and was not asked — and the finding is that a page with one pair of eyes on it is a page that cannot be checked, and that there is now a second pair of eyes on it.**

## Chapter 0084 — The Only Room In This City With Anything In It

The sixth to the ninth of the fourth month, in a room in Fallowditch over a shop that had been a chandler's. **Adela Cray, about forty, comes back with the folder, because the bench's own book says the bench held it and the bench could not keep it and there was nowhere.** Aldis Wray, a landlord with four rooms, has come a second time with nothing. **A bench that has told the truth about itself has priced a room; the only clerk in the district is the holder, and the page is a record about a person and a bench is a body and a body is not a person.** A coal man brings a hundred-and-forty-year-old weighbridge sheet out of his mother's things, and the bench takes it with a column that says *not legible to this bench*. A man of about sixty wants an entry without a name, and the bench writes a third term for it — **an entry that says UNNAMED is an entry everybody in a district this size can identify, and this is theatre, and this bench is doing it anyway** — and it is used the same day, and it is worth what it is worth and not a penny more. **On the ninth the bench of three makes its own rule in its own hand, in its own room, in public, on a day it appointed: a document goes back to the person who brought it, the entry stays, and a person may ask for an entry without a name.** The fourth term says the day is paid out of the three of them out of their own purses at no rate, *because we are not going to be a bench that says it in one place and not the other*; the fifth term enters that this bench is eleven miles from the fen road and four miles from its own farthest place **and a room with a rule in it reaches the people who can get to it and not one person further.** A woman of forty-one with a fortnight's notice is told plainly that holding is not hearing, and that a fortnight's notice is a date and a man with four rooms, and that there is nothing in this city that hears a date and a man. On the tenth Perditta Nye, twenty-nine, three years in the post, writes an ask for the terms of a charter she has never read, because a room with a rule in it needs to know what a page is for — and puts it in her coat, and goes out and does the boundary.

## Chapter 0085 — The Second Ask, Past the Fourth Mile

The tenth to the thirteenth of the fourth month. **The third member comes back from the hedge, reads the ask standing in wet clothes, and says send it, and says *I would like to send it anyway*, and Nye enters that he said it out loud.** One sheet, four names, a mark, a wedge and chain pressed on the tenth; down a lane on the eleventh. **The carrier's clause is used for the second time, and the licence's third term is the mechanism: Sera Quill says the ask is valid, says she may not send him because she is expressly not his advocate and a recordwright's carrier is hers and not a thing she hands to the man the fourth term names, and says that if he goes he goes because a clause with his name in it says he goes, and that those are two different sentences and she will not let a ledger read the second as the first.** He walks to Fallowditch on the thirteenth, more than four miles, where the clause stops obliging him and a favour cannot be entered; the leg stops two hundred yards short of the shop and he goes on. Nye reads the nine sheets out, word for word, in about fifty minutes, **and at the foot of the ninth sheet, under the fifth term, in the copy and not in the sheet, there is a line, and she reads it out because the ninth sheet was being read out.** He tells her three things and asks her for one: enter that you read it out, enter the words, enter that you do not know whose hand it is, and write it to the chamber with nothing in it that asks for anything, because nothing can be done about it. **The finding of the chapter: a clerk of twenty-nine has made herself a holder of a room nobody made her a holder of, in a district of about two hundred and forty people, and her bench's ordinary work has stopped, and a hedge eleven miles of boundary is not going to be done this spring.** And at the door he says the thing he came across the county for: there is one man in this city that four hundred people can be sent for, and nobody has ever told anybody what happens to that when he cannot walk, and it is not in a charter and it is not in a bench's book. **Nye writes her letter the same afternoon; it goes down to the fen road on the fourteenth, and it sits on a tray in a chamber in Orison with nothing on it asking anything, and it is stamped on the twenty-seventh, and the number on the spine is the only thing that happened to it. The line takes a fortnight to come anywhere at all and nobody is waiting for it.**

## Chapter 0086 — Three Rates and a Hole in the Middle of One of Them

The fifteenth of the fourth month, in a room over a pump, and the room is the only one in four miles with anybody awake in it at the first bell. **A slate with three sums on it and a fourth scratched out four times: what the district owes, what it has, what it is paying with.** One thousand four hundred and seventeen pounds ten, less the two hundred and two pounds ten a gate hand corrected herself, is one thousand two hundred and fifteen pounds, and the correction for the rate cannot be found in any district in this city. The district's fund is two hundred and forty pounds a year on paper and nil in fact since the fourteenth of the second month. **And the third sum is the water: an eighth of a penny a bucket, forty-four pounds and sixteen *shillings* a year, of which the largest single part is the serving of notices, because no instrument in this city says how to serve a district.** The sum that will not close is a haul. **A haul is a barrow's load; a barrow's load is four loads to a cart; the district's own rate is a rate for a load; and this district has no carts and has not had one in the living memory of anybody in it — so a barrow here is four loads to a cart that does not come, the four is a fact about a cart and not a price, and a haul in this district cannot be priced at all.** Oakes is right that this is a hole and not a mistake, and a hole is worse and lasts longer, and it is his because he did not want to be the man who asks a bench of four what a barrow is. **Bryn Sallow comes to the door she has never gone through, having heard from the boy who cannot read that there is a sum about her thirty years and a fourth that will not come out, and she is not angry at a number: she is angry at Oakes, because a duty with nobody in it was the only thing that had ever protected her and a bench of four and a man from a bridge took it off her in three weeks, and she has to be angry at a person about it for the first time in her life.** The change is a definition — **the district says in writing, for the first time, what its own rate is a rate for** — and the cost of that is fourpence a year to write down and eleven days to find out whether it is fourpence or nothing. And at the end the district is named in a number it has never counted itself: about two hundred and forty people, out of a clerk's estimate in a letter from a bridge four miles away, against about a hundred and ninety on a mud bank on the third of the third month. **At the gate, at the ninth bell, she gives the thirty years a third figure in the only coin she has, and it is hers and not the hauler's: six thousand hauls at two thirds, got by watching the barrow go past her gate about two hundred times a year for thirty years, because there is no arithmetic in this county that turns ten and a half hours into a barrow. She asks to be read out four times a year, and she says she has never once in her life been six thousand of anything.** Then she asks him whether he knew the units were wrong when he put the number in her book, and he says yes, and she says she would have gone on with it, and that she has been counting her own tide in her own feet since she was fourteen and there is no slate.

## Chapter 0087 — The Man Who Took His Name Out of a Rate

The sixteenth and seventeenth of the fourth month, in a room over a pump, and there is no speech. **Garvin Slade, thirty-eight, a salt seller out of a booth on Weir Lane since he was thirteen, which is twenty-five years, shuts his booth before the fifth bell for the first time in a long while and puts a slip with a bench's entry copied onto the barrel lid, and asks for it to be read.** He is not withdrawing from the bench; he is withdrawing from *that* rate, and he dictates two sentences in the flat voice of a man who sells salt: *a customer asked me if the water was going up and I said I did not know, and a man who sells salt and cannot say whether the water is going up has had the one thing he sells.* **The rate cannot be withdrawn, because it was read aloud on the first of the month to about two hundred people, and nobody may take a rate back off a district that has heard it; and a clerk does not rub out a name, so his name stands twice, on purpose.** The bench cannot set the rate again, with three of its four or with four, because it has no money, and it enters that so that nobody in four years reads the page and thinks a man fought his own bench and won: **HE DID NOT WIN. HE PUT HIS NAME UNDER IT.** A rule is then made for itself in twenty minutes — a withdrawal after a rate has been read aloud is read aloud again inside the quarter, at the pump, by the holder — and the third line is Oakes's and is correct: *this is not a right. a right is towards a person. this is towards a barrel lid.* To Rundle, who is the other of the two rate-setters who live on Weir Lane, Slade says: *you are going to stand up there on your own and I will not be able to stand next to you, and I have thought about that for two days and I am doing it anyway,* and Rundle says *I know, I have watched you not ask for two days.* And the cost lands the next morning at a counter, on a woman of about thirty with a job at the second bell who does not want to stand in a crowd, and Slade tells her it is going to be read out whether she is there or not. **The woman with the barrow says that on the first of the seventh she is going to read a man's name out at that pump four times a year, in a district where she is the only person who can read and cannot appeal from one word of it.**

## Chapter 0088 — The Second Reading of a Quarter's Rate

The nineteenth of the fourth month, at the sixth bell, at the turn, and about two hundred and forty people go up a mud bank in the cold because a man who sells salt put his name under a rate. **The lid is read first and it has not changed: half a farthing a bucket, and it is the right rate, and it buys the things that let you appeal.** Then the book's entries, then the rule of the seventeenth, *this is not a right, a right is towards a person, this is towards a barrel lid*, and nobody laughs, and then the numbers — and **the thirty years is read as six thousand hauls at two thirds, twice, and the gate hand says where it came from and it did not come from the bench and it did not come from the holder, and it came from the gate — and the hauler's own hundred and fifty of the last eleven months is then read as having no figure at all, which is a different debt by a different woman in the same unwritable unit.** Two hundred feet away, at the gate, Bryn Sallow stops working it for four seconds, which she does not do. **The rate has not gone up and has not gone down, and about two hundred and forty people have been to a pump in the cold to hear a man's name, and nothing whatsoever about the water has changed.** The only thing that has changed on the ground in three weeks is a woman of sixty-one, **Ottoline Fenn, who takes in washing at the upper end of the street and has drawn at that standpipe for nineteen years with a yoke and two buckets, four times a day. Eight buckets is a penny. A penny a day is seven pence a week, one pound ten shillings and fourpence a year, and in a district of about two hundred and forty people that is eight times what the rate falls on most of the rest of them, and this bench has never asked how many buckets a household takes and cannot afford to ask.** Since the sixth of the month she fills at the ninth bridge's pump, four miles up the fen road, where there is no rate, at the fourth bell before the count, and walks back, and there is nobody at either end of that walk with any power over any part of it. **She told one person: the man who withdrew, at his counter, on the eighteenth, because he asked her. She has not told this bench, and the district still believes she comes down to that pump every morning.** At the ninth bridge a builder with lime in his apron watches her go past for the fourteenth day running, and works out that a mechanism you take off a person who has not offered it to you is the machinery that put his name on a board, and does not use it.

## Chapter 0089 — What It Costs To Make a Document

The twenty-fifth of the fourth month, at the fourth morning bell, at the First Verdict, in the open air, with about sixty people. **The docket board has had twenty-one names since the fourteenth of the third month and twenty-two for the nineteen years before that, and the sitting runs from the fourth morning bell to the seventh.** Maren Kett, nineteen years in the Orison chamber, named advocate of the first appeal, who argued against his own client for three hours and knew the outcome on the day he took the brief, has not been in the case since the seventeenth of the month before last, and he asks the question nobody has thought of asking: **what does it cost, in money, to make a matter ready, and who pays it.** A place costs about two days of a clerk's time and about four pounds of cart hire, and **the whole of twenty-two names in four months has cost this city less than ninety-six pounds of timber** — four pounds fifteen against the four new timbers bought for a bridge four miles away, **which are still lying in the mud on the north bank of that river because there is no water in it to float a timber up to a deck.** The half nobody has costed: every one of those names is a name a person may be called out by, a matter is ready when a document can be sworn, **a document is made by a person who can read, and this city has no line on which a person is paid for reading** — and in this case the price has been paid by a refuse hauler, a gate hand, a cowman's wife, a landlord's mouth, a clerk, a boy of nineteen who is not paid and was not asked, and a washer of sixty-one who has stopped using a standpipe. **Ansa Rell says the other half and nobody likes it: a body that pays a person to prepare the paper has bought the paper, and this city can afford eleven places and has always been able to afford eleven places, and what it cannot afford is the work that makes a matter ready, and it is not going to, because that work is done by people who are not paid, not named, cannot be produced, and whose names are in no column anywhere.** Kett pays for his question, and the price is not a name on a board: **a counsel who enters a question is not a witness to it and cannot be examined on it, and the only people who can answer his question are the people who wrote the words, and a question of his does not travel to either of them without a party, and there is no party in this.** The board goes to twenty-two, as it has been for nineteen years. Then Vale's letter arrives, entered the same day: **the cost of the whole sitting is entered against the ninth bridge's district road fund, which is not the district asked**, and the ninth bridge's district has a hundred and five pounds in a line it cannot move and forty-four pounds and four shillings in the line the charter is charged to, and there is no way to charge it to Halloway, because a holder may not appeal and a bench of four has already answered.

## Chapter 0090 — What a Page Is For, Which Nobody Has Answered

The twenty-sixth to the twenty-ninth of the fourth month, and then the second of the fifth. **They produce him.** Corva Silt reads the production out in the flat voice of a clerk producing a witness and says she put the three questions to him in advance and why, and it is because she has read four sheets of his own figures out loud in her own office by herself and was not going to do it again with him in the room. *Did you count it yourself. Do you know that it is wrong. Would you write it again.* Yes, yes, and yes. **The notice is that he is going to stop counting**, and the reason he gives is that counting is the only thing that ever cleared his leg, and he has worked out that what he has actually been doing is holding a woman up for three hours a day so that a bench of four can read a number, and the number is wrong, and the woman said no. **The seventh thing he enters, with its size: a witness can be produced twice, a witness can be produced three times, there is no line anywhere in this city that pays a man for having been produced — and the only man who could have made the figure more right by counting more carefully has stopped, and the number stands, and it is wrong, and it will be read out at a pump four times a year.** Corva Silt says the thing that made it necessary, out loud, once: a number can be wrong in a way that can be argued about, and that is the only door out of a shape anybody has ever found, and it is not a good door, and she would rather he did not make a face. **Bryn Sallow, at the gate, at the turn: he has made himself a man who cannot be produced on a number; and the other thing, which he did not ask for — she has been counting her own tide in her own feet since she was fourteen, there is no slate, she would have gone on with it, and somebody in four years is going to read a book in which a man says he stopped for a woman and there is going to be nothing in it at all about the woman.** Then the letter from Fallowditch, **written on the thirteenth, down to the fen road on the fourteenth, and stamped in a chamber in this city on the twenty-seventh**: **the ninth time the line has come, and the sixth refusal, in the same words, nobody named, and the holder has still not read it and reads a charter in order.** On the twenty-ninth four people make the smallest instrument anybody has made in this case: **the book is open at the pump two days a week, any person of the district may ask for it to be read aloud to them, and a person so asking is not to be asked why** — and the reader is nineteen and unpaid and was not asked, and the bench enters that it is unpaid so that a clerk in four years does not read a generous district. And what it buys is that a washer of sixty-one who cannot read the book can come to a pump and have a page read to her, **which is a rate that has not gone up, a page a person can use, and not a right that survives a district being squeezed.** At the sixth bell, on a bank, with about two hundred people going up in a line, **Wenna Tace asks the first question anybody has asked out loud in four months in this city: nobody has ever told me what this page is for** — and sends it to a recordwright, pays for the paper out of a pound and eleven a quarter, and puts her own name at the top of the letter and the recordwright's at the bottom, because she is the holder and is not allowed to be in a support of anything and is not going to start in a letter. In Orison, a recordwright reads it, does not answer it, puts a licence with four terms on the table, and reads the fourth one twice, and writes four lines in the back of her own book that are in no record but hers, and does not go out, and does not open the book.

## Chapter 0091 — A Question Is Not a Matter

The second and third of the fifth month, in a stone room off the second gallery in a city four miles from a bridge, with a stove that does not draw. **A letter comes down the fen road from the bench of four of the district of Halloway: on the sixth, at the second bell, in the room over a pump, in public, the holder's question will be read out whether a recordwright attends or not, and the bench has asked her to attend and has not asked her to come, because the two are not the same thing.** The licence's four terms are read out in full and the fourth is one line with no date on it: *she takes no matter of her own while she holds this one.* **She writes the six heads of a matter on a sheet and fills none of them and one:** a claim, a subject, a burden, a witness, a remedy, an exit.
