The one that is filled is the subject, and the subject of the question is a person who is not the person asking. **So the hauler's question is not hers, and answering it would put her inside a matter, and the fourth term stops her, and a term that stops a person from her own claim is not a technicality.** Ilyan offers to walk the four miles and is refused with the reason: that room is four miles from where she stands, he is four miles from a bridge, and those are two different four miles. **Sel Harrow brings a view on the court's own motion — that a question by post from a district that has entered it in its own book is not of itself a matter, and that a recordwright may answer such a question once in writing — and she refuses it**, and gives three reasons: a term that can be read a second way on the day it bites is a wall with a door in it; the day a licence's fourth term can be varied for the person it binds, no fourth term binds anybody; and a sentence about what a page is for, entered with a licence against it, is a thing that will be used against a person. **Harrow confesses that he drafted the fourth term and has used it against her four times without knowing he was doing it.** She sends four questions to a standing authority of preservation, three of them about paper, and at the fifth bell on the fifth of the month she walks four miles down a lane in the rain and a man sweeping a building with a broom asks her whether she has come about the volumes or about the printed shelf, and when she says the volumes he puts the broom against the wall and says, *then nobody has asked me for that one.*

## Chapter 0092 — A Day in a Building With No Name On The Door

The fifth of the fifth month, in rain, four miles from the court, at the low end of a street of counting-houses. **Corrin Vask, forty-one, sweeps the building for nine years at ninepence a day and opens the cold room for anybody who asks, and nobody has ever asked for the volumes.** He says out loud, unasked, that he has wanted somebody to ask him for nine years and has never once asked anybody for it. The resistance is not a locked door: the door is not locked, there is no person in the building, and a hand is willing to turn a page because nobody has ever asked it not to. **The price is a shilling out of a drawer for the opening of a volume, entered in the building's own day book in the same column as the sweeping, with the name of the person who paid it — and the sweeping of that day is not done, and there is one man for the floors and he is the man with the lamp.** The volume is on the shelf he said. Three holes, and the middle one is the slot, the thread is cut, and the inventory mark beside it is in a newer hand. **On the facing leaf, four lines below the shadow she entered on the twentieth of the second month, there is a second shadow, about four inches long, and in three hours with a lamp held low it yields not one character.** The leaf has to be held flat and the hand that holds it is his and his right hand is six years gone at the wrist and he cannot open it afterwards without doing it one finger at a time. Ilyan waits ninety yards down the lane in the rain, in a doorway, for a whole day, because he is a person in no district and cannot go in; the leg goes at the third bell and does not come back, and the rendering is at the same height and the same distance it has been at since the third of the second month, and the token is not touched. **The day's finding: it is going to take days, and a lamp, and a hand, and an opening, and an opening is a shilling, and a shilling is a day of a man who sweeps floors.**

## Chapter 0093 — What a Rule Said

The sixth to the eighth of the fifth month, in the same building. **The standing authority of preservation answers the fourth question no, for the third time, in the same hand, with a reason and not a silence, and says that a thing asked three times with a reason is a thing a person ought to stop asking.** It says what is printed is not lost and what things are for is not printed anywhere. The first three questions are answered yes, and the answer takes two days, two shillings and a hand that is six years gone, and the honest reason for two days is a man opening two cases and holding a lamp. **The yield is about paper and not about a person: a form for lodging a matter in the Quiet Chamber, printed three times in four years, of which the middle printing carries eleven words at the foot that the other two do not — *and the keeping of it is a duty of the ward* — put there by a copy-holder who could not read the line to the end and named its subject themselves.** A thing copied from a thing copied from a thing loses whatever was not in the pen. The finding entered in her own book: **a shelf can tell you what a rule said and cannot tell you what a rule was for, and that is the third time in four months the same answer has come back out of the same kind of place — a bench of three that holds nothing, this desk, and a room over a pump that holds a page — and the honest thing to do with a shelf is stop asking it questions it has already answered.** What she gives up is written down: the only desk in the city that said yes has now said yes and no and a reason three times, and a person who needs a page sworn can no longer even point at it. And the eleven words are the worst thing that has happened to her in a month, because they are the right eleven words and they will be sitting in her head telling her what two illegible characters on a leaf are. **On the sixth of the month, four miles down the fen road, nine people were in the room over a pump and the bench read the holder's question out as it was entered, without improvement, and nobody answered it.**

## Chapter 0094 — One Quarter of a Sentence

The ninth to the twelfth of the fifth month. Four days in a cold room with a lamp held low, a leaf held flat by a hand that is six years gone at the wrist, and eleven correct words face down under the weight of the volume because **a person who has eleven correct words and four inches of faint grey is not reading a leaf, she is checking a leaf, and those produce two different records and only one of them can be sworn to in twenty years.** On the fourth day the crease gives her the rest. **Entered under licence, dated and signed, on the facing leaf beside the empty slot and not on the removed page: [  ] OF THE ENTRY IS A DUTY OF THE WARD, AND [  ] — one quarter of a sentence, entered as one quarter of a sentence, and it is not the clause, and it does not restore any right of anybody to answer no, and the characters the crease takes she did not supply, and the form's eleven words would have given her two of them and she did not use them, because a form is a copy made by a person who had to leave a volume and a copy is not a witness.** The consequence: **a ward is a duty and not a place, so the keeping of an entry is a thing a district owes, and a thing a district owes is done by whoever is doing its work at the time — and in four miles the person doing it is a hauler of twenty-nine who cannot appeal from one word of it and who has never been told.** The comparison of the twenty-fourth of the second month stands unamended: one quarter of a sentence more does not make a better comparison, it makes a longer one, and a reader in twenty years may prefer the earlier. **The two costs are named: four days, four shillings paid, four days of sweeping not done and not doable, and a right hand that takes nine seconds longer than it did in the spring; and the second cost, which cannot be paid back in paper — she has made herself a door again, in a building four miles off that nobody in four miles can find, and she knew it on the ninth and did it anyway, and the only reason she can give is that nobody was going to ask, which is the reason a desk gave her.** And she asks Vask his question, and he spends it on the only question in this case that has no answer, and tells her about a box of nine hundred and forty forms on the fourth shelf with a word at the top in a bigger letter than any other word on the page, and does not tell her the word.

## Chapter 0095 — A Claim That Is Not Filed

The thirteenth to the sixteenth of the fifth month. **The collision is found by a man sweeping a building who had been given four questions and had spent them all, and who asks the fifth on his own and cannot unask it: *then why have you not got it out.* She says *mine*, and tells him, in a cold room, in about a minute.** The family claim is six years old and unfiled and needs a page. **Her duplicate is a second event on a second date and is not the same event as the removed page, and the reason is written down twice with both dates on it: one was cut out of a bound volume by somebody who left a mark in a newer hand, the other was carried out of a house by somebody who left nothing at all, and the only thing they have in common is the subject, and she wants them to be one thing every day of her life and they are not.** The fourth term is the obstacle and it is not a technicality: a claim is a matter, the fourth term says she takes no matter of her own, she agreed to it in front of three hundred people, and **she can hand a district ten words with a crease in them and cannot hand her own family a page, and the difference is that a record is what a person knows and a claim is what a person wants.** She goes to Harrow and tells him and asks for nothing, and he refuses correctly, and it costs him: he is the man who drafted the term, he has looked for two days for a reading that gets her what she wants, and he enters that he drafted it and that a thing a person drafted they do not get relieved of by deciding to be bound by it. **She writes two letters with no reason in them to two people who have kept paper for her for six years without being asked, because a reason would be a matter.** On the sixteenth she does not open the book, and she cannot tell you whether she refused herself or was refused, and she will not write it down as either. **And in a lane, for the third time, Ilyan says out loud that he is not going to chase the sealed preservation item, and why: the price of going on after that desk is that records stop coming, to everybody, and none of those three people would be owed anything by him if he went up a stair with a tray.**

## Chapter 0096 — A Question the Appeal Cannot Carry

The seventeenth to the twenty-first of the fifth month. **Garvin Slade, thirty-eight, a salt seller, comes up the bank with four feet of a chair leg he has been holding without noticing, and asks to appeal the rule of the seventeenth of last month — not the water, the reading.** He is right about the cost: about two hundred and forty people go up that bank in the cold, and a woman on his lane with a job at the second bell said she would rather not stand in a crowd, and he does not know whether she went because of the rule or because of his name. He is refused four times over and every refusal is correct: a person may not appeal a thing that person made; the clerk is not appealable either; the holder may not be a party or be named in a support; and the only person who can be named in a thing in that district is a man of thirty-one confined to the ninth-bridge matter. **The only person who can be named is carried a notice down the fen road and back, and for the first time since the twenty-sixth of the fourth month — twenty-two days; the chapter prints "two months and nine days" and that is a defect corrected in state by the Volume 02 audit — the leg clears for about four hours — and it is not a gift. It is a trade: he has been holding a gate hand's weight out of his leg for four hours by carrying a piece of paper up a road, and the paper was the weight, and the woman was not.** The finding, entered by a counsel of nineteen years in his own book: **a thing that is ready gets a place; a rate is not a document; and the day a district agrees what a thing is worth it has not fixed the rate, it has only fixed the unit.** The board has twenty-two names and eleven places and it has twenty-two tomorrow. The court has no power over any rate in any district, which it said on the face of a field about a gate and is now saying about money. And Slade, at the door, to a man he has never met: *I have got your name on a thing and I did not want to and I have been told I cannot do anything with it.* To which Ilyan says he is sorry, and Slade says: *do not be sorry, be the man who turns up. That is all any of us wants and there is not one procedure in this city for it.*

## Chapter 0097 — The Aggregate Bill

The twenty-second of the fifth month, in the room over a pump, with a clerk of a road fund four miles away standing up with a slate. **Meridith Vale, nineteen years in the post, who has never once entered a false reason, walks four miles and pays his own way, and gives the second half that cannot be given on paper: a fund is a list of lines and a clerk writes them in the order of what they buy, and in the spring he will have to rank the charter of a poor district against a hand under a deck, and he will put the charter below the hand, because he cannot put a room above a body, and he says so in advance, with a date, having been asked by nobody.** He refuses a minute of his own bench in advance, in the room, in front of a witness. **The person to be angry at is named, and he is doing something defensible, and the price of the charter in the spring is the price of a hand under a deck, which is a man of twenty-two nineteen miles off who is not named in the charter and is not a party.** The other half of the afternoon is the unit. **A bench of four agrees what a load is, in its own book, in its own hand: a load is a barrow, and the four in the guild's sentence is a fact about a cart and a cart does not come — and it does not agree what a load is worth, because the price of a load is the price of a tide and there is no day in this district that is a day.** The cost of the deciding is not money: the bench asked the only person who knows how many loads come off that bank in a day, in public, with her name on her answer, and she gave it, and **she is now a woman in a bench's own book who has said out loud that her own work has no day, and it will be read out four times a year for the rest of her life.** one hundred and fifty loads and about six thousand loads, in one unit for the first time in four months, and neither of them in any coin.

## Chapter 0098 — The Price of a Form

The twenty-fourth to the twenty-eighth of the fifth month. **The box comes off the fourth shelf on the twenty-fourth: about nine hundred and forty forms, all one print, all one shop's typeface, all with the word EXCEPTION at the top of them in a letter bigger than any other letter on the page, delivered on a cart in the winter before last with a bench's seal and a docket and never called for.** A man who cannot read the word has read it four hundred times while dusting it. **And the first district to print another district's charter terms on a form headed with that word is Coldwater, and the clerk of Coldwater ordered four hundred and sixty-three of them herself, and says so in the first four words of everything she says that day, and enters the whole of it against herself — including that she read the word before the print came back, thought about it for the better part of a minute, and printed it anyway twice more.** Her reason is the right one and the sum is right: a form is a shilling and fourpence against thirty-one clerk's hours a quarter of serving notices by hand. **The finding: a form is a habit with a seal on it, the word is a claim that a rule is temporary, and such a claim does not take the clause — it takes the paper, and the paper is the only thing a poor person in this city can put in front of a man who has a cart.** Perdy Sill, thirty-eight, of a coal yard at the bottom of the slipway, is right about six of her nine sentences and wrong about the rest, and she is asked for her pitch back in writing in front of five people, and she gives it, and her ninth sentence is that she would still be in that lane if the word was not on the form. **No court and no bench has power over a form, and every bench that has said so has said it in a room with no reader and no pump and no rule and about nobody.** Corva Silt, who has asked five times to be relieved of a print authority and been refused five times, does not ask a sixth time, and says the thing that matters: a note on the back of a form is a thing a person says in a lane, and a form is a thing a person holds up in front of a carter, and the carter has a cart.

## Chapter 0099 — What the Volume Is For, On the Ground

The twenty-eighth of the fifth month to the twenty-eighth of the sixth, and the middle of it is three weeks of nothing, which is part of it. **A person nobody in this case has met comes down a lane on the fourteenth of the sixth with a slate under her arm: Salla Kern, forty-four, of the district of Nethergill, who has kept a slate off the wall of a weighhouse office for eleven years for a man of sixty-one who writes figures on it and takes it home on his shoulder, and who has read the eighth term of the ninth-bridge charter and believes that the work record is portable.** The eighth term is real and the promise in it is real, and the thing that fails is that there is no record to be portable: the lines on the slate are figures and have no names on them, and the man who writes them has no obligation to write a name and there is no form for it and no line on which he could be paid. **This is the case's own first problem in a different shape: the child under the bridge was safe inside four chapters and the household was not, and the household's difficulty was never that it was refused, it was that there was no page on which the person could be found.** The finding: a copy of the charter travelled seven miles with no seal and no clerk and is short of the second half of the fourth term, and it is sworn and true and binds everybody in a district of about four hundred people, and the part that was left off is the part with a consequence in it, and the giving was done by a drover on a cart and nobody will ever be able to say who gave it. The carrier's clause is invoked and the man named in it is produced for the third time, in a district he does not live in, and the sending costs four shillings and sixpence of cart, a clerk's day, a day of a boy of nineteen who is not paid, and a day of a man's leg. **He refuses to answer one question in public — whether the Ro household's access term is the same as this woman's claim — because a household in that case has a standing right to stop everything it is part of itself, and nobody presses him, and that is the one line about it in the whole of four months.** Maren Kett is asked what has actually changed in four months in a poor district, and reads out ten things, and not one of them is a ruling, and not one of them would survive a district being squeezed, and four of them cost somebody an hour a day or a day a week or four days of a man's sweeping, and one cost a woman a pitch, and one cost a recordwright a shop and two rooms and a business, and one cost a man of thirty-one the only thing his body ever gave back.

## Chapter 0100 — The First of the Seventh, at the Sixth Bell, at the Turn

The first of the seventh month. In the open air at the First Verdict, at the fourth morning bell, before about ninety people, **a copy of nine sheets copied word for word out of a copy comes up from a bench of three seven miles off with a docket that asks nothing, and the line is at the foot of the ninth sheet in it, and it is the tenth appearance and the seventh refusal, in the same four words, nobody named, in front of ninety people instead of nine.** The difference between the ninth and the tenth is that nobody put it anywhere: a clerk copied nine sheets from a copy and copied the bottom of the ninth one as well, and did not know what she was copying, and has entered that she copied it and would like it entered that she did not know. **The holder has still not read it. She reads a charter in order and a charter ends at the fifth term, and she asked a man not to read it to her, and nobody in this case will.** The eighth thing is the form: **his name is printed four hundred and sixty-three times in a district eleven miles off, under a word that says a rule is temporary, in a document that cites him when his standing forbids him to be cited as anything, and a man with a sledge has used it to take a pitch at the bottom of a slipway off a woman of thirty-eight, and he is the only person in that sentence who cannot do one single thing about any part of it.** The leg does not clear, the rendering is unchanged, the vantage has not moved, the token is not touched. He walks the four miles and nobody meets him. And at the sixth bell, at the turn, on a mud bank, at the second full quarter's reading: **the lid has not changed; a load is a barrow and is not priced; one hundred and fifty loads and about six thousand loads are in one unit for the first time; a man who sells salt has his name read out with his own two dictated sentences under it; a woman of thirty-eight has given up a pitch; an eighth of a penny is read out against a washer of sixty-one who has walked four miles for eighty-six days; and the quarter of a sentence is read out, with the gaps, and the holder says what it is and what it is not, and says that a page is for keeping the record of a duty and that she worked that out on her own, in about four minutes, twice a week, for four months.** And then **Ottoline Fenn, sixty-one, who has a right to ask under the rule of the twenty-ninth and has never used it, asks to hear the haul read again, and when she is answered she says the thing nobody has said: I did not tell the district because the district does not know. She worked it out in four minutes on her own step. If the bench knew that a washer of sixty-one was paying a penny a day, then the next time there was a hole in the fund somebody would sit in that room and work out where a penny a day was coming from and it would not be a person, it would be a ratepayer. So she walks four miles, every morning, for as long as she can walk four miles, and she would rather walk, and she is sixty-one, and she says it on a mud bank in front of about two hundred people, and not one of them can do anything about it, and that is correct.** The answer is hers and it is in the book with her name on it. And the last thing read out is not in the district's book at all: a boy of seventeen who runs a line puts a question in his own book and reads it out, because a duty is done by whoever is standing nearest when the work is done and a form has a line on it for a person to be named against each term and eleven names have gone in on a form at a gate on a good road and all eleven are dead and the last of them is a woman of seventy-one's father and she keeps writing over his name and does not know why. **The volume closes on his question, which is not about the road and not about the toll and not about the bridge: whose name is the last one in that line, and what is a person doing on a form that nobody has to answer to, and how would anybody find out, and why does she keep writing over it.** Nobody answered it. The right of refusal is not restored. The road is not touched.

## Chapter 0101 — The Road Above the Reach

The sixth day of a tide, from the second bell, on a cart road four hundred miles from anything Ilyan Vester has ever argued about, in rain, with ordinary people doing ordinary work. **Danno Ray, forty-five, a carter, is sitting on a load of quicklime at the head of a drop with his hands between his knees, because the water will be on the bar at the seventh bell and nine hundred yards of it is between him and cover.** And the whole geography of the Nettle Straits is given in one working man's mouth and is not explained in narration: *we go through on the sixth, the fifth, the second. On the fourth, the sixth and the seventh the water owns that bar, and it is not a matter of opinion, it is a matter of the moon, and no clerk in that guild house is going to write it on a piece of paper for you, because if it is written on a piece of paper then somebody has to be wrong about it on the fourth.* **Corrie Tallow, forty-eight, rope, oil and tallow at Wether Cross, has sent a cart eleven miles up the road at four days' hire out of four days she does not have, to fetch a man. She saw him on the second of the tide and he came out of the cart on the sixth, which is the four days.** Nobody sent for him. He goes because somebody asked, and the asking was a favour with a day in it, and the day was hers. **The asking is locatable and this is its final form: she had been in the city four hundred miles off and come home, and in a lane in that city a counsel of nineteen years told her, for no reason he gave her, that there was a man in it who could look at a document and find where it was bolted to what; she wrote to him at a counter, he came down the coast road, and she walked the eleven miles herself and then sent a cart up the same eleven miles.** **On the fourth of the tide Wend Garrow, thirty-three, went through the channel on a season's passage paid for on the first, with eleven hundred yards of bank, and the guild's chart is right, and four hundred yards of the bank is not on the chart, and the salt boards and the cart he built himself in the winter are in the water.** The first honest problem is on the page in the first chapter: the coast keeps its charters in a record neither the carter nor the stall-holder can read, and a man who argues cannot navigate. **And there is a sheet in his coat, given to him at a counter in a city by a recordwright who would not come and would not give a reason he could use, and it has two questions on it in one hand: WHOSE ACCOUNT ARE YOU CARRYING, AND WHO WILL HEAR IT.** He understands on the sixth that they are not questions at all.

## Chapter 0102 — Seven Days and a Wall With One Door

The seventh day of a tide, from the first bell, in the guild house at Kestrel Reach. **Bettany Rook, fifty-one, harbourmaster's clerk for nineteen years and harbourmaster for six, in a hood she wears on the water side because the light off the Reach has been going for two seasons, and she has told nobody, including the glassmaker.** The guild house has four rooms and a loft over the loft and the upper room is not opened to the public, and the reason is not secrecy and she says so in nine words and is right about it. **The engine of the volume is a licence.** A season's passage is sold at the first bell of the first of the tide for seven days a week of the tide, and the licence does not name the days and cannot be refunded on a day the water is on the bar, and the price of finding out which three days those are is a day at the crossing, and the price of not finding out is a cart. **The System panel, the first of three in the batch.** She is right about the strait and says so: last year a boy of fourteen went out on the fourth in a punt with a bad lock and came back, and if there were no guild there would be a great many fourteen-year-olds finding out where the channel goes, one at a time, on a bad day, with nobody on the bank. **The guild voted a reader at the crossing four years ago at two pounds a year out of the crossing's own takings, and had Seth Garrick, twenty-six, for eleven months; the crossing took ninety pounds eighteen of tariff that year against a hundred and six the year before, which was a kiln year, and a wage comes out of what the crossing takes, the crossing took less, and the reader was taken off, and she voted for taking him off and has not voted for it twice.** The free things are the wall and the painted staff and the chart, and the day is what they cost, and the people who cannot find a day are the people who need it. **And the coast works out he cannot read, and Bettany Rook is the one who does it: he asks her to say the third line again, and instead of saying it she asks him what the painted bar staff reads at the second bell this morning, in front of the counter, pitched to carry as far as a fishwife — and he has walked past it and not read the number — and she says *that is the first thing I have said to a stranger in six years that I have had to be right about*, and she never says anything about it again, ever, and in Chapter 106 she watches him read her own sentence back one piece at a time and says nothing whatsoever. His second question is *read me the part about who holds the passage when the water comes over the bar*, and there is no such part, because on the fourth day nothing happens, because the fourth day is not a passage, and a charter is a thing that says what a thing is. **His finding, said to her in a yard in the rain: a guild which holds the only safe water on this coast is not a crime, it is a house with one door in it and no other, and every person on this coast is standing in that house tonight, and there is going to be a week the door is shut and not because anybody in that building wants it shut, and in that week there is no second route and no second office and no person on this coast who can read the one they have, and there is one of them and she has a hood on because of the light on the water.** She takes the hood off and puts it back on and tells him she has two seasons of sight left in that eye and has not told the guild because the day she tells the guild is the day she is moved to a counter, and the guild would be right to do it.

## Chapter 0103 — A Penny a Tide, and the Cost of a Day at the Counter

The turn at the sixth bell on the first of a new tide, at the market on the quay and then at Wether Cross. **The coast works out he cannot read twice, and neither time is a confrontation: a boy of eleven reads the fish board to him twice, the second time slower, and a fishwife looks at him over the boy's head and says nothing at all; and Corrie Tallow reads the guild's tariff sheet to him once and he says say the second one again and she says *it is one penny a tide and I have read it to you twice*, and picks it up and gives it back and says *never mind. It is one penny a tide and it is the same penny on every day and you have got it*.** The rate, found on the ground and not announced: **the flat at Wether Cross is crossable six times a day on the first, second, third and fifth and nine times on the fourth, sixth and seventh, because on those three days the strait is shut and every cart that has to get round comes over the flat, and the tariff is one penny a tide on every day of the tide, so the days the water is worst are charged exactly what the days it is best are charged, and the guild has refused twice to write a different price for a day of water because a tariff that says dangerous is a tariff that can be refused and a man who refuses a tariff is a man you have to argue with on a bad day, and there is nobody at that counter to argue with anybody on a bad day.** **Nim Hask, eleven, makes thirty-nine tides a week at a penny — three shillings and threepence, eight pounds nine a year — because he is sent on five days and it is two of the low days and three of the high ones. The ten other households that send somebody are threepence a week between them, and the commonest figure on that road is threepence, and three shillings and threepence is thirteen times threepence.** Corrie Tallow counted it with a stick off the back of a stall board over two afternoons. **Bel Hask, forty-four, mends nets, wants the boy off it, and the reason she cannot is arithmetic and not sentiment: a cart that does not come round does not earn, and the alternative to a boy on that flat is a cart going eleven miles the other way by the shore road, which is a day and a half of a horse, and a boy at a penny the tide is cheaper than that, and that is the entire arithmetic of her life and she is right about it and she hates it.** Jory Vance, twenty-two, of the tide office, keeps a list of the carts that paid and there is no line for a guide. **And Corrie Tallow's own want: thirty-one years of tide in a penny exercise book in pencil, the only right one on that road, and the guild will not fire a stall-holder's hand into a pane, because a pane cannot be crossed out, and the guild is right, and that is the whole of it and it is very small.** He asks what a matter in one place is worth and she tells him it is not enough and that the second place is Slackwater, nine miles by water, three hours, and that he should go on the second or the fifth and not the fourth.

## Chapter 0104 — Three Hours and Nine Miles of Water

The second of the tide, nine miles by water, three hours, with **Dell Gannock, forty-two**, the eldest of the three brothers of a ferry family four generations on the water. **Sorrel, thirty-six, has the flat boat and takes people over the shallow on the fourth, sixth and seventh. Cleve, thirty-one, is a dealer in what the flat boat brings back and does not go on the water at all and has a house with a good roof, and the whole coast knows about the roof and none of them says anything in front of his mother.** The fare is fourpence and a bag of tobacco Dell does not smoke. **The leg goes in at the second of the three hours and the low water is in his nose in two layers with a third under both, and the room with the boards and the table and the four people and the woman of about forty-four with her back to him is at the same height and the same distance it has been at since the third of the second month, and on a moving deck he cannot look away from it.** **His left hand goes round a line and the line comes half a hand's length through his fist and the coil goes over the side, and Dell Gannock takes it out of the water without a word and gives him a length of manila, which is the right thing to hold and is not a rope, and says *you cannot grip*, and does not ask. ** He tells Dell about Alma, eighty-one, who keeps a crossing on the bar road above the north end and is not on any sheet anybody in the guild has. **At Slackwater the argument is old.** He says the count a third time, in the same words, which he is sorry of. **Efa Lund, thirty-four, who keeps the shore's own count of what crosses the flat in a book of her own, eleven years, no pay, not asked, comes in at the back and says one thing: *seventeen.*** Eleven households and seventeen crossings, both true, and the difference between them is a difference of *what you would do about it*, and there is no road on this coast that can carry that. **A number is a thing a person said, and what the person said it for comes off it when it gets out of the room, the way a name does.** **Pether Voke, fifty-four, speaker of the Slackwater assembly, ferryman's mate for thirty-one years, picked four times, says before the man has spoken that nobody sent him, that there is a court eleven miles up the road with a tray and a clerk and no power over a strait, and that he will be told both of those things properly, and he says he has not decided and will not pretend to, and will not have anybody say afterwards that anybody was talked into anything.**

## Chapter 0105 — Thirty-One Seats and a Day Off the Water

**The batch's spine. The first refusal of consent.** The third of a tide, the first bell, in a net loft with the doors open at both ends, before thirty-one seats. Pether Voke loses a tide before he starts and says so out loud at the first bell, which is eleven pounds of freight and two men's day, so that nobody can say afterwards he did it for a romance. He says no once, in the open, and sits down, and lets anybody say anything they like, and will not take a word of it back. **His reasons, in order: being served is a day of a boat and a day of a cart, and there are four dates and four sums in the loft's own book and the third is a woman whose papers went into the creek and were sworn out again out of her own memory; consent is not a yes or a no, consent is a door with a person in it, and a person in a door can be moved, and the way a person gets moved is that somebody stands in the doorway and it is awkward and bad for everybody; and the real one, which is that one matter ends in one answer, and the guild's price for a tide is a penny and Slackwater's is a day, and one of them is going to be wrong in public, and it will not be the guild, which has nineteen years and a pane on a wall, and it will not be a man off a road, so it will be Slackwater, in front of thirty-one people, holding the wrong end of the only water any of us can use — and after it is over Slackwater will be a body that was in a matter about the water and was wrong.** Ilyan is let at him three times and fails three times, correctly, and is the worst of the three and knows it inside the first sentence. **The System panel, the second of three.** The refusal is recorded in full: the assembly's own practice is that a refusal is written on the back of the notice and read at the next sitting, and Pether Voke asks who will write it, and Efa Lund is the only person in the loft who can, and she writes it **on the back of a fishmonger's notice** and reads it out twice, and gives it to the man it is refusing, and says: *it is not in your book and it is not in mine and it is not in any clerk's hands in a city four hundred miles off, because there is no clerk in this Reach and there is no book in this Reach. Read it and take it with you. It is no use to anybody. That is not a complaint. That is what it is for.* **The four things it cost: eleven pounds of freight, two men's day, four years of a woman who wanted a thing written down, and a boy's thirty-nine tides, which is outside the matter from the moment the door closed, because a tariff on a road is not in a matter about a strait and never was, and he knew that on the first day.** And Pether Voke's last of it: *you will not be able to fix the flat. You were never going to be able to fix the flat. It is not in anything, and it has not been in anything for four hundred years, and it does not belong to the guild because the guild did not make it and it does not belong to a road because there is no road on the flat, and there is not one body on this coast that owns the nine hundred yards of shingle that your boy is on nine times a day. That is not a complaint and I have not got a remedy. I have got a lighter with a tarpaulin on her and eleven pounds gone.*

## Chapter 0106 — Eleven and Seventeen, and a Boy Who Is Not There

The fifth of the tide, and **the number gets back before he does** — by boat, in a shop, off a man who was not in the room. The sentence he carried nine miles and said twice in a loft *is the front of a sentence*: eleven is about the households that pay a penny a tide, seventeen is about the bodies on nine hundred yards of shingle at the turn, both true, not the same claim, and the difference between them is a difference of what you would do about it. **Corrie Tallow, told on a lane in sideways rain, does not improve it and does not break.** She cannot correct it either, because a correction in a room is heard by one man as seventeen and one man as eleven, and in four months there will be a difference of six on a board at that counter and **the only name anybody in this Reach has for that flat is a boy of eleven, and a boy of eleven is the easiest possible thing in the world to make a difference about.** **And she refuses the door, hard, and says why: she has been asked four times in four years to go to that counter and be asked the question by somebody who counts, and she has gone twice, and if she goes up there with a figure it will not be a figure, it will be her, and they will hear her, and it will be a sentence about her and not about the tariff, and she has spent two days being the woman who asked and both of them came back with nothing. So she is not going. I am going to be here at the sixth bell tomorrow with a board and a stick, and the number that is right is eleven.** **Bel Hask has not sent Nim since the second — three days — and will not pay the rate, and the reason is not money.** A cart the long way round on the fourth cost a day and a half of a horse and about nine pounds of freight she did not earn, and she has done the sum four times in three days and it comes out the same way every time, and the sum says the boy is the cheapest thing on the road, and she says plainly that it is not the money: a boy is a thing you can send and a boy comes back and says it went, and if he does not come back then something is wrong, and she would rather pay three shillings and threepence a week than find out. **Bettany Rook's paper — a sheet, not a pane, and therefore arguable — goes in the book of a bench four hundred miles off about a clerk in a city who entered, in a book, that the licence's two clauses are not the same sentence, and was right, and is not the person who sold it.**

## Chapter 0107 — A Pane Is Not a Copy, It Is a Year

The sixth of the tide, two miles along the coast, to the glassworks at Thornglass. **Hollis Thrale, sixty-one, forty-one years at the fire, **deaf in the right ear, as his father was and as his father's father was, since the kiln was built in the year of the bad seventh, and nobody in the family has ever been asked about it,** the volume's counterpart to Corva Silt with the failure inverted: he is not the mechanism and he cannot be produced.** The process, with nothing in it that can be hurried: two feet of sand and salt out of the pans, a batch turned about forty times over eleven months, and **a letting day about once a year that the tide chooses and the fire agrees to.** **The fifth pane is on the order sheet with a date against it and no money against it; the letting day was eleven weeks ago and it has gone, so the fifth pane is not late, it has not begun, and the guild can write on anything it likes for eleven months and not one word of it will be the charter.** **THE PANE IN THIS CHAPTER IS NOT THE GUILD'S. The guild's charter pane and the guild's tide table both stand in the tide office at Kestrel Reach and have never been out of that building; the pane on the wall of this shed is Thrale's own — a second tide table he made for himself three years ago because the guild's does not come out of there — and the guild has never seen it and is never going to. This is the only other pane in the world and no later batch may put the guild's two outside the tide office.** His craft is right about the guild's reason for refusing Corrie Tallow's pencil — a pane cannot be crossed out — and he will not put a stall-holder's hand into glass. **He is wrong about what a pane is for, and he says so twice and is argued with twice.** A pane is a photograph of a river: a yard and a half by three feet, carried four hundred yards by the man who made it, ruled with brass, in nineteen years and eight months, and set in its frame in the guild house with his own hands. **Ilyan Vester reads the seam of Thrale's own pane and it is not what he expected and it is not wrong: the authority of the class of thing does not rest on the guild. It rests on the man who stood beside the arch on one morning, and if he dies the coast cannot check the guild against anything, and the guild does not know that and neither does Thrale.** The cost is in the body: a hot floor, a cold place in the left hip, the leg gone, six seconds out of the left hand, and a woman of twenty-four he had not asked taking him down a step. **And the old man's answer, which is the best thing anybody says on this coast in three weeks: *a photograph is the only thing on this coast that cannot be argued with, and if you cannot argue with a thing then you cannot ask it anything*** — and the guild holds the only legible copy because it pays for the only copy, and there are two people alive who can read it, and one of them is sixty-one and deaf in one ear and has not had a cart in his yard in nine years, and the other is fifty-one and losing the one eye she has got and has told nobody including him. **AND THE THING HE HAS TOLD NOBODY, WHICH IS THE BATCH'S SECOND PLANT: his pane and the guild's do not agree. Two columns out of eleven — the third and the eighth, the two that record a place and not a depth — differ; his is the later one and he would swear to his; he had it on his wall for a year before he saw it, and he did not see it because nobody in the world makes a second copy of a thing in order to check the first, and because he is sixty-one and he is right about glass. He cannot get at the guild's to put the two side by side, and he cannot be sure his own is right either, because a channel moves, and there is no instrument on this coast that would say so in a room, because the only thing that would say so in a room is a pane and the only pane in a room is theirs. He told a man off a road, in a shed, and nobody else on that coast knows.** This does **not** make the guild wrong about the water: the guild's people are right in their hands and the guild's glass is a photograph of a morning, so what is stale is the record, not the harbourmaster.

## Chapter 0108 — What the Marks Are For

The seventh of the tide, and the coast's own version of a page. **The crossing's song is sung by Seth Garrick, thirty, who was the reading man at Wether Cross at two pounds a year for eleven months four years ago, taken off in the eleventh month because the crossing took ninety pounds eighteen of tariff that year against a hundred and six the year before, and who now sells lamp oil. The song has no other occasion and no other singer, and there is nobody alive who will say where it came from.** *Six on the low and nine on the high / fourth and sixth and seventh — nine, and do not stand on the bar / A penny the tide and a penny again / Count your doors, and count them plain.* It is a tariff in four lines, and Ilyan, who can follow about four words of a pane, follows every word of it. The Slackwater song is a different song about a different thing: *Three hours down and three hours up / and if you are wrong about it you will not fill the cup.* **Ilyan's only contribution is one question — what are the marks for — and the answer is for counting, given to him twice, and then Seth Garrick gives him the second answer nobody alive on that road has: the post is the honest count, the returns are the money count, the two have never agreed, the post is wood and rots and goes into the sea or a kiln every nine or ten years, and the old one is leaning against the wall of the tide office and nobody has ever looked at it. I could count it. I could tell you the figure and the figure would mean nothing to me and nothing to the guild.** **The appeal, used by somebody who cannot use it: on the seventh day, at the turn, at the tide stone, the custom is that anybody may stand on it on the fourth or the sixth and ask the harbourmaster one question and she answers there, on the stone, in the open, to whoever is on the shingle, and nobody may ask a second, and it is written down nowhere and the charter says nothing about it. The song is a different thing, on the seventh, and a great many people on that road have never worked out that those are two customs and not one — which is why Bel Hask came on the wrong day, and nobody stops a woman with a net. Bel Hask stands on it and asks about the seventh day, and Bettany Rook says no — not because the guild will not sit, but because *you have asked me on the seventh about the seventh, and I cannot sit on it*, and the reason is the water and a boat on the rocks at the north end with a man in it who is not dead because he was seventeen and angry. She is right and has no legal standing whatever, and the song is not a document, and the asking is free and the answer is not.** She offers the guild's own book, a key on her belt, first bell on the first, and then refuses that herself, because the guild's book is a fire going to happen and a question about a road is not a question about her house, and reading it out is not answering it. What she will do is paint the days on the post at the harbour mouth, herself, in a bad light, with her hood up, and he is not to be there. **And the batch holds its pattern: Sabra Trew, twenty-four, third year, has nineteen notches cut on a kiln wall that is the only thing in the shed never moved, and a gap of four days in the row where a bad seventh banked the fire, and a grandmother who kept hers in a book in a school in a town a hundred and forty miles up the coast that nobody she has ever met has opened. Ilyan writes the address on the back of a torn-out page in a penny exercise book with the heel of his right hand and tells her the truth about himself: that he has not done it yet, and will probably do it about the fourth of next tide, and that it will be the only useful thing he has done on this coast in a week.**

## Chapter 0109 — Sixteen Carts, and the Line He Copied Off the Sheet

The first of a new tide, four miles along the coast, on the bar road above the north end. **Alma Gannock, eighty-one, Dell's mother, knits, has a boy of eight in the hut — the card was filled in the winter of the year he was born, eight years ago — has kept that crossing for eight years with a chain and a stool and a tarpaulin, and it has never once been shut, and four carts go over it in a good week. She keeps the count on a slate herself and brings the slate in a basket to the tide office once a quarter, and the tide office writes the number down and does not check it, because there is nobody at the other end of that road to check it against.** **The toll was short twice in eight years, sixteen carts each bad week, thirty-two pence between them, paid out of her own box — and she did not pay it to hide the shortfall. She paid it because a cart standing on a bar in a bad week comes apart, and a bar road that shuts loses a day and a half of a horse per cart, about nine pounds, and the road staying open is the whole of the job. The money is nothing. The finding is that she has decided alone, every week for eight years — four hundred and sixteen weeks, two of them bad — whether four carts or sixteen go over, and nobody on this coast knows she is deciding, and she would rather be the whole of the thing, and she has never been asked why.** **The discovery, and every part of it is in the mouth of the man who is the crossing's reading man and reads everything on the tide-office walls: Jory Vance reads the bottom line off the card out loud and then the same fourteen words off the head of a column on the charter pane, so the two are the same fourteen words in the same order — Ilyan is never shown reading the pane, and his own contribution is that he does not need it told twice. The guild's crossing card and the guild's tide-office card are printed from one plate in one shop, one under the other, with a hair's width between them, and Wend Garrow — the carter who lost his cart on the fourth — filled in the crossing card in his first season because Ivo Gannock could not read, and copied every line off the sheet, including the bottom line of the second card, which is the tide office's burden line: THE COST OF A WEEK IN WHICH THE WATER IS NOT ON IT IS NOT BORNE BY THE GUILD, and the person at the end of the line is the person who is. Alma Gannock has been entered, by a man who cannot read, on a form nobody asked her about, as the person who bears the cost of a bad week. It is not a mystery. The guild is not hiding anything, the clerk did not ask, the shop had one plate, and the guild's own charter does say that the passage-holder bears the passage.** **She will not give a figure for what the sixteen carts would have cost going round: that sum is sixteen households' and not hers, she has never made it, and she knows only that it is a great deal more than the sixteen pence.** He asks her how many carts in a bad week and how many in a good week and how many bad weeks in eight years and how many the rest came to, and writes the four numbers down, and does not ask her the other question. **Efa Lund comes up the road having heard it in a shop in three days and demands to know why, and he gives her two reasons and neither is good and the second is the one he would have used, and then a third: he has found the pattern three times on this coast — a person standing nearest to a duty, nobody having written the duty down, the person doing it — and Corrie Tallow, Sabra Trew, Bel Hask, Alma Gannock, and every one of the four has cost the person something nobody put in the book. So she does not go in the book. Not this season. He writes down that he did not, and the reason, and the date, and that it is his, and hands it to a woman with a ledger who will tell him a man off a road is nobody, and she will be right, and then he has to find out how a person on this coast is supposed to be asked, and there is nobody here to teach him.**

## Chapter 0110 — The Ninth Tide, and No Name on the Line

**The batch close.** The second to the sixth of a tide. **A pot of paint goes out on the counter at Kestrel Reach with nobody's name on it, and Bettany Rook paints the days on the post at the harbour mouth herself, alone, on the fourth, in a bad light, with her hood up, and it takes her about an hour, and it is the only work done on that coast in a fortnight that nobody asked anybody to do — four white bands, three black, and under them, in her own hand, one, two, three — bar — five — bar — bar, with the numbers painted with a brush, and the seventh gone over twice.** **The case's own first promise, tested by somebody who is not in the case and does not know what it cost, and the test is a small physical thing: Jory Vance, twenty-two, will not let a boy of eleven through a chain with a hand-lead because the boy is on no list, there is no line on his board for a person, and if the turn does not get counted then the fourth does not get counted either and the whole of the flat does not count — and there is no villain in it, there is a young man with a board and no line and a tariff he cannot make come out right. Bettany Rook says move the chain; the cart goes; and she makes him enter, dated the fifth of the tide, in the day book and not in the returns, that a person who guides a cart over Wether Cross on a tide is not a trespasser by that fact alone and is not counted as a cart — on paper, in a bad light, so that if it is ever wrong a man can draw a line through it, and she enters the reason in writing too: a name on that line would be a name on a line the boy could be found on, and she has spent six years refusing to be that. It costs an afternoon of a young man's day and an hour of a woman's bad light and nothing in money, and it is in the day book and not in the returns, and that is the whole of what this coast has done for a boy of eleven in fifteen days — and it costs about two pounds a year in a wage, out of a margin of four: the crossing takes ninety pounds eighteen, eighty-one pounds sixteen of it is tallage taken at a counter by people who do not live on that road, nine two is the eleven households (the tallage in this summary and in Chapter 110's prose was corrected from seventy-eight pounds fourteen by the Batch 0002 review repair pass; seventy-eight fourteen does not subtract from ninety pounds eighteen and the figure that does is eighty-one pounds sixteen), of which eight pounds nine is one household and thirteen shillings is the other ten, three two is the chain and the oil and the tarpaulin, which leaves six, and a wage of two leaves four. She has never told the council and the council will not like it.** **Corrie Tallow's thirty-one years of pencil is copied into the paper book, dated, in her own hand where it will go, with her name at the top — and is deliberately NOT the guild's record and is not on the list for the fire, because a pane cannot be crossed out and a wrong thing on a shelf can be taken down. She pays five days of her stall and a half-crown of custom off a crew of eleven years, and says both numbers out loud in the room. And she is told she was wrong about that too, nineteen years ago, in her own hand: Bettany Rook once carried a woman's column to the fire herself and it went into the tide table that is on that wall, and it is still wrong, and the pane was fired once and cannot be fired again.** **The same scene carries the date Ilyan put on a promise in Chapter 108 and did not keep: the address of a school a hundred and forty miles up the coast is in the back of the exercise book in his coat, written crooked with the heel of his right hand, it is the fourth of next tide, he does not write the letter, and he writes down that he did not and sends it to nobody. The plant is still only a plant and the batch-0002 prompt's statement that he has done nothing about it holds.** **The leg clears for four hours in a shed at Thornglass on the fourth, carrying an iron bar out of a fire and back forty times, which is the same practice and the same four-hour limit and the same trade as the notice he carried on a road between a bridge and a pump: the paper was a duty that was not his own, the bar is his own work, nobody's name is on it, and the woman it belongs to is four hundred miles off and is still in his leg. It is the first time since the eighteenth of the fifth month and the only one before this. He picked it up again. He told nobody here and is not going to.** **Bel Hask sends the boy on the sixth at the ninth crossing and watches him the whole way for the first time in eleven days, and says what the three shillings and threepence bought, and it is not in the tariff: Corrie Tallow took the slate down to the far stones, four hundred yards down, because the ninth crossing of a day is the only one on that flat worth a slate, and she counts every one that goes over and she sees everything, and so if the boy is not on the flat at nine she will know before he is off it, and Bel Hask has not had that since he was six, and she is paying for a thing nobody is going to put in a tariff, and do not tell the guild.** And the ninth tide comes forty minutes late and four inches high, and the boy comes off it wet to the waist, and Bettany Rook says the thing the whole batch has been walking towards without knowing — the guild cannot change its own charter, because the charter is a pane and a pane is a year, and the fifth pane's letting day went twelve weeks ago, the money for it is not in his hand, the third of next tide is the last day it could be, and between now and a letting day a year off she can do nothing with the word *charter* but read it, and nobody has stopped her, and the man in the cold arch two miles along the coast is not her enemy and has never been anybody's enemy and would have done it for the same money off the council. **The System panel, the third of three, and the batch ends on a question that is not about a person, which is the first time in fifteen days that the worst question on that coast has not been about a person: WHAT DOES A BODY DO FOR A YEAR?**

## Chapter 0111 — The Press

**A hundred and forty miles up the coast, the third day of the fourth tide, in a schoolyard, and no Ilyan Vester at all in the first three quarters of it.** A carter with a cart of pan salt in the yard and a limp boy and a load of lime at the top road gives the whole of the northern geography in the negative, and it is the whole of it: the low road at Sallow Bight is the best road on this coast for about fifty weeks a year, and in the turn of the year the ground under the salt pans goes to soup, and it is not passable for a wheel for a week and a bit, the same nine or ten days every year, and the only way round is Nether Head, eleven miles longer and a climb the whole way. **He also says the thing that is the chapter's finding: in those nine days a school at the top of that coast does not open, and not because anybody is ill, and nobody has ever put that on anything.** **Belva Cray, fifty-one, has kept that school eleven years, teaches twenty-six children in three rooms, the third of them being the coal store with the door taken off, and is paid for by eleven families at nine pounds between them. The board met in the village at the last turn and voted nine families to two to send the children down the coast to the school at Culler Head, which has a wall and a stove, and she has until the turn of next year to stop them, and she has a figure in her head and no number: nine days, which she said twice at the meeting, and a man said a fortnight and another said a week was a week, and all three of them were talking about the same nine days.** **A feeling does not stop a board.** The press behind the schoolroom door has four holes: the subscription list, forty registers, the letters, and a fourth hole for things nobody has claimed. In the fourth hole is a book, and it has been there eleven years and she has never read it all the way through. It is written twice, in two hands, in order, and the two hands do not agree; where they differ the second hand has put a small mark in the margin. **It is a list of weeks.** A mark at the head of a line that is a week, a reason against a line that is not. She has never been able to date the first page: a man in town who deals in old paper laughed at her, then looked at it longer than he laughed, asked three questions about the ink, and said a thing like that cannot be dated, only guessed at, and told her to go home. She went home about four times in four years. **In the last ninety years of the book, which she has counted back twice and got the same answer both times, thirty-one weeks have a reason against them in four different hands saying the low road could not be crossed. Thirty-one times in ninety years — a count of weeks where she needs days, and a bad week is one day or nine, and she has had both and they are the same word.** The last thirty-one of the ninety are in her own mistress's hand; her mistress died six years ago and said nothing about the book in eleven years of standing in that room with it in the wall behind her. **She takes the book out of the hole for the first time in eleven years and it leaves a clean rectangle of unfaded distemper in the dust. The going costs eleven shillings, which were in a box under her bed and were the money for a new desk; four days on the road; and a boy of eleven in the village who will have to be told a thing that is not a lie and is not the truth, twice.** She goes down the bank, and the low road is soft at Sallow Bight to within forty yards of the crossing, and the ruts from the year before are still in it under water, and the water is not deep. **Forty ruts — somebody crossed it forty times last turn with a hundred people going up and down and knew it was soft. That is the number she needed and it is the wrong shape: forty crossings, not forty days, and nobody could tell from the ruts whether one of them had been in the water for an hour or a cart had gone over at speed and thrown a wheel.** Nobody on that road ever intended to count them either.

## Chapter 0112 — The Margin of Four

**The fourth day of the fourth tide, a bar day, so that a body of nine which meets on a day it cannot travel is a body nobody can be got hold of on. That is the practice and it is about four hundred years old and it is the only piece of administration on this coast that has ever improved anything.** The council has not sat since the turn before last. Bettany Rook puts the day-book line from Chapter 110 on the table standing up, which she has not done at that table in six years, reads out all nine lines including the reason she wrote no name on it, and then reads out the cost before anybody speaks: about two pounds a year in a wage. **Two seats on that table wanted the money spent for about a year; the other seven wanted it spent in the sense of wanting somebody else to be the one who said no, which is what nine people on a coast most of the time.** Dunn Pike, fifty-eight, two boats and the best berth, says it is not a thing to be argued about. Nell Crake, forty-one, a chandlery, the only woman on the council, says it is good and cheap and that the guild has been getting the counting done for nothing for four years by an accident of a boy being cheaper than a wage. **Gell Roke, seventy-one, who paints the guild's bar staff, asks where the money comes from, and then asks what the crossing takes, and Bettany Rook — who has had a day to get the numbers in the order she wants them — gets them out in the wrong order and says so afterwards.** The figures, said out loud in a room for the first time in six years: the crossing took ninety pounds eighteen; eighty-one pounds sixteen of it is tallage on carts and goods taken at a counter by people who do not live on that road, and ninety pounds eighteen less eighty-one pounds sixteen is the nine pounds two the eleven households pay, which is what Gell Roke asks the next question about (corrected by the Batch 0002 review repair pass, which found seventy-eight pounds fourteen did not subtract); eleven households send somebody over the flat; the chain, oil and tarpaulin come out of that and not out of the tallage, three pounds two, because nobody has ever paid for a chain on that flat out of a cart; the other households' crossings are worth thirteen shillings between them and eight pounds nine of the whole is one household of the eleven, a boy of eleven who goes over nine hundred yards of shingle thirty-nine times a week at a penny; the road gives the guild six pounds, the guild spends three pounds two, **the margin is four and the wage is two.** She says plainly that she has never told this council the four pounds in six years, and that she is not going to stand there and be praised for saying it out loud in the worst week of the season. **The four who vote no are right about the money: two pounds out of four leaves two, the two is what a bad year eats, the passage money is three pounds two short that season, and a council that votes a wage it must withdraw in the winter has told the road it does not matter what happens on it. That is the argument and it closes, and the cooper and the fish factor used it and neither had anything to put on top of it.** Then Gell Roke says the other thing, in nine or ten words, without looking at her: **he has painted that staff for the guild for nineteen years, nobody has paid him a penny of it, he has never asked, and he would like the table to notice that he is the only man at it being asked to vote against paying a stranger two pounds a year for standing at a post, and he has not got an argument that will carry in a room, and he is voting no, and he knows exactly what he sounds like.** He then says it is not a good reason, that it is nineteen years of a man who does not want to be told what he has been doing for nothing, that he would not have voted no on the money alone because a bad year is not a reason to leave a boy on a flat by himself three days a week, and that he would rather the table had only the one. **The vote is five to four. For: Dunn Pike, Nell Crake, the man of four carts, the ship's carpenter, and a boy of twenty-three who has one seat because his uncle died in the spring. Against: Gell Roke, the cooper, the fish factor, and a man of sixty-eight who has not voted for anything in nine years. The five are not wrong: there is nobody on that flat on a bad sixth with a chain across it and nobody at the far end. The four are right, because the money is the money and four people at that table could add.** **And the finding comes out of a man of sixty-eight in the voice of a person asking where a thing is: can the guild's charter be changed this year. No. Then what are we voting about. A wage.** Nobody answers that, and nobody had to, and it went into the minute book in four lines, and the four lines are the true shape of it. **The one thing nobody at that table knew, including the woman who had been harbourmaster for six years, is that the money for the fifth pane was due at Thornglass the day before and did not come** — because there was no pane on the order anybody at that table had ever seen, and because a rule that cannot be changed for a year is not a thing a council is asked about in a season, and because nobody had thought to ask. **The System panel, the first of two in this batch, arrives on the step outside at the fourth bell: the council is nine; the vote was five to four; the wage is two out of a margin of four; the season is short; and what is not decided is whether a body that votes a wage it cannot pay has voted at all, and whether the four who voted against knew what the road takes and the five did not.**

## Chapter 0113 — A Word on a Piece of Paper

**The fifth day of the fourth tide. Bettany Rook writes five words on a slip of paper — *Come up to the counter at the second bell* — and gives it to a man buying lamp oil who is going up the lane anyway, so it costs her nothing.** No seal, no form, not signed with a name, in a hand two people in that building could read and a third could not. Seth Garrick takes it without reading it because it is not addressed to him. **Corrie Tallow comes down to the counter at the second bell with a hand-lead in her fist and a boy of nine holding the other end of it, and has shut the stall for a day.** She says both costs out loud before she is asked for either: **a day of that stall is four shillings and twopence; a day and a half of her sister at the fish quay that she does not get back; and the boy gets his dinner and no wages because he is nine and she pays him in bread.** Bettany says she has one thing to ask and will ask it badly, because she has had a day to do it well in and used it on the ledger. She tells the boy to let the rope go slack, then says what the council voted, that it will not be paid, that she has nobody to put in the place of the wage, that nine people on a council have never stood on that flat and two of them could not tell her the depth of it in a bad seventh without a rule, that a line in a day book saying a person is a crossing is going to be read out at the first bell on the first of every tide for as long as she holds the key, and that every one of those people is going to be told the flat is being counted and it is not. **Then: I want you. I have wanted you for eleven years and I have not asked you once, and the reason is not a compliment — it is that you would have said yes.** The room makes no sound. She then names the three others she has never asked: a woman at Slackwater nine miles down the water who has counted bodies on that sand for eleven years for no pay and is not going to be standing at her counter; a boy of eleven on that flat who is not in anything; and a woman of eighty-one on a bar road four miles along this coast who takes a penny a cart herself out of a basket and sends the guild a slate of the lot once a quarter, with no second person behind her (both halves of that made explicit by the Batch 0002 review repair pass, so that Chapter 115's slate in a basket cannot read as a slip), who is not hers to send for and she will not. Corrie's answer is no, at once, and Bettany has the look of a woman who expected it and wanted it anyway. **Corrie's reason is her own and is not a refusal of consent to a matter: two pounds a year is forty shillings and a day of her is four shillings and twopence, so it is nine days and a bit — corrected by the Batch 0002 review repair pass, which found forty-eight shillings and four days in the prose and in this summary, and 480 pence against 50 is 9.6; she has been counting since she was seventeen, thirty-one years, because her mother's book was a bad book and she could do it better; and nobody has ever paid her, nobody has ever asked her, and she has never wanted to be asked. The reason underneath: if she takes it, it is a wage, and a wage can be stopped, and when the season is short again whoever holds that council will find the money on this road and take it off the eleven households, and she will be standing at the far end holding a slate saying *this is how many* while the price of being there goes up under her feet — and she asks to have it entered that the last one said so first. And then the other half: she is not going to be a woman who took it, for the rest of her life, in every stall and every lane; she would rather be the woman who counted it for thirty-one years and was asked, and said no, and had a day of her stall, and said the number out loud.** Bettany writes it in about four minutes with her own pen, reads it back in about ninety seconds, improves nothing, and turns the sheet round — and there is no name on it, because Corrie told her not to and she did not argue in a room. **She says why twice: it is the only entry in nineteen years made on the word of a person in that room who is not a guild member and has no standing of any kind on this coast, and if it is ever wrong somebody can draw a line through it and it will be wrong with the line there.** Then the finding, stated in the chapter in full: **there is a guild and it has a counter and a counter can put a person in front of it; nobody has ever been compelled to come and nobody has ever been summoned; there is no hearing, no penalty for coming and no penalty for not coming, nothing to appeal to and no second door; and the whole of the machinery is a slip of paper with five words on it carried by a man who was going that way anyway. It worked. That is the finding and it is the whole of it.** The woman came, on the day, at the bell, heard the offer, said no, and did all of it in about nineteen minutes. **Nobody tested it. It was not tested by refusing, because refusing is the only thing a person in that position can do and it stops nothing and the paper does not come back. It was not tested by asking. It was not tested at all.** Corrie walks back down the lane, does not go to the stall, stops at the tally post, does not touch it, and goes and gets the slate she has counted on since she was seventeen and goes down to the far stones.

## Chapter 0114 — Nine, and What a Song Is For

**The sixth day of the fourth tide, the second bell to the turn, at Wether Cross and the harbour mouth.** Danno Ray, forty-five, down off the high road with a load of lime behind him and about four hours before the turn, sits on the end of the post — which nobody does — and says there is a third song. Corrie Tallow says there is not, because a coast of four hundred miles has about nine songs and no coast has ever had a reason to compare them. **The third song is at Sallow Bight, a hundred and forty miles up, and Danno Ray has been up that road twice a year for nineteen years and had never heard theirs until a carter at a school told him there was a carter at a crossing who sang one.** *Nine days on the low road, nine to mend it, / and after that the carts go, and that's the end of it. / Count it going down and count it coming up, / nine, and no more, and don't be caught in it.* It is a working song, sung on the second morning of the turn by whoever gets to the top first, because there is no post and no mark and nothing else on that coast that says whether the low road is under. Corrie writes the four lines on her slate and Danno Ray tells her that is the words and not the song, and that the difference is the same difference between him saying a thing to her and her saying it to a man in a shop three days afterwards: **a song is a thing about nine hundred people agreeing, and if five people had all copied it off a slate it would look exactly the same, and you cannot tell the two apart.** **Then the finding: it is not a number. It is three in a dry turn and eleven in a wet one and nine in an ordinary one, and there is no turn in a hundred years that has been nine every time. The song cannot hold three numbers so it holds the worst one, because a song about nine when it is eleven gets a cart into the water, and a man will not put a number in a song that will let a carter drown in it. So it says nine, and has said nine for a hundred years, and is wrong most years and dangerously wrong about one year in nine — and nobody up there believes it. They believe the ground, the same as everybody on this coast, and they use the song the way you use a bar staff. And the trouble is that there is nothing else.** A hundred years of families have shut a school for nine days when the truth was often three, and have let a cart down on the third when the truth was nine, and one year in about nine a carter has gone into soft ground to Sallow Bight in the turn because a song told him nine and the song was wrong. His name is on the thing about him and the song is still what they sing. Can it be changed. **No. Here is how you change a song: you sing a different one. And then there are two, and every person on that road has one of them, and the only person who can tell which one you have is the man standing at the top of the road who is going to get into the water. The only authority a song has is that everybody sings it, and the moment there are two, the road has no number at all, which is worse than a wrong number, because a wrong number can at least be doubted on the day.** Corrie leaves the slate face down on the stall board and goes up the lane and waits for the turn. **The second use of the tide stone, and this time on the right day.** The stone is for the water: it has been there longer than the guild house, the guild has never interfered with it and does not know what happens on it twice a year, and the custom is that anybody on it may ask the harbourmaster one question and she answers there in the open and nobody may ask a second, on the fourth and the sixth at the turn, written down nowhere, the song being a different thing on the seventh belonging to a different man. **Corrie Tallow, who asked once on the wrong day and was told so, asks: can a number be taken out of a song.** Bettany Rook refuses, in the open, on about sixty people, and gives the reason and it is not the reason Corrie expected: **the stone is for the water, it works because there is one answer and she is the person who has it, and it is worth nothing the moment the question is about anything else. A song is not the water. She cannot rule on a song, has no standing over one, and no body on this coast has standing over a song — not the guild, whose charter says what a passage is and nothing about what people sing at a crossing; not the assembly at Slackwater, which would refuse it in about four minutes and Pether Voke would refuse it out loud before he started and would be right to; not a school, a parish, or a man on a coast road. There is nobody, and she is the only person on four hundred miles that anybody can ask.** She adds, and Corrie is owed it: **if somebody could change that number, the person with most to gain is Corrie, and if that song were the record of this coast she would have found it four years ago and been at the counter inside the week.
