The song is not a record, and the reason she has spent four years trying to get thirty-one years of pencil into one is the reason a song cannot be one, and they are the same fact, and you know it.** Corrie stands on the stone with the water coming up on both sides of her, about sixty people watching a woman of forty-eight find out that she was right and got nothing, which happens on this coast about twice a year. **Then she says, out loud, to about sixty people, which she had not intended to do: then it is nine, and it is wrong most years, and I have written it down, and I am going to copy it on to about nine boards up that road over the next year and put the number on every one of them in my own hand, and it will be nine boards and not one, and that is the best a person can do to a song.** Danno Ray, from the end of the post: *You have understood nothing about songs and I am glad of it.*

## Chapter 0115 — What Comes Back

**The seventh day of the fourth tide, the first bell, at the head of the lane at Wether Cross.** Belva Cray arrives with a bundle under her coat the size of a good armful, four days on the road, two changes of cart, and the eleven shillings that were the money for a desk. She stops at the tally post and asks whether it does anything, and Seth Garrick tells her it counts, that nobody knows what for, that he has been counting it in his head for four years and has nobody to check him, and that is a poor answer to a stranger and it is the only one there is. **Then the letter, which is four lines and is signed with two initials and no name: he is sorry to write to a school; there is a book in her press that came from a glassworks two miles along this coast from a crossing called Wether Cross; the woman who sent the second copy has been dead eleven years; the woman who made the copy is her granddaughter and is twenty-four and there is a letter of her own that she has not answered; he has no way of reading it and nobody on this coast who can read it for him; if anybody can come and look at it they should, and it costs four days and eleven shillings, and he is sorry about the order of those two things.** Nobody had ever written a letter to that school that was not about a roof. The new mistress opened it on the fifth in an empty room, could answer nothing in it, carried it nine miles down the coast to Culler Head to a woman who keeps the district's business, and wrote two lines on the back: the book is a book, the school does not know what it is, and would be glad to be told and would rather somebody came. **So Belva Cray came, and she put the bundle on the counter at the second bell with four days and eleven shillings and thirty years of wanting gone, and Bettany Rook did not touch it for about a minute.** What she wants: **the number of days a year the low road is under and her school does not open.** She has said nine days at a board meeting twice, and a man said a fortnight and another said a week was a week, and all three were talking about the same nine or ten days and not one had a figure. Nine families out of eleven have voted to move the children. **And the answer is no, and it is the whole of her answer: there is no record of days anywhere on this coast.** Bettany, standing up, hood back because the counter is on the land side: there is a pane on the north wall upstairs with every tide in it since before she came, a bar staff two hundred yards down that road that is painted, a slate that goes from Alma Gannock in a basket once a quarter off a road four miles along this coast — **and there is not one thing on four hundred miles of this coast that will take a week and give me back days.** There is a song up that road that says nine and a man called Danno Ray who has been up it in a cart for nineteen years and says the number is three in a dry turn and eleven in a wet one, and a hundred years of people up that coast have gone on believing the ground and gone on using the song. **There is not one written thing about that road and there never has been, and the reason there is not is that the road is under for a different number of days every year, and a record that does not come out the same twice is not worth the keeping to anybody who has ever had to keep one.** Corrie Tallow is sent for because she reads, and Corrie reads the school book out under the awning at Wether Cross for an hour and a half to anybody who came, and Belva Cray sits on the bench Seth Garrick lets people sit on and listens to the whole of it, and it is a list of weeks. **What came back is not an answer.** Four hundred years of it, and nobody able to say where it begins. And the reason it is useless to her: the coast has never kept a record of days, and the reason is not carelessness. **A day is a thing that is only ever true once, and everybody on this coast understood that a long way before she did, and it is the only sensible thing anybody up her coast has ever done, and it has cost every one of them a figure they can stand in a room and swear to.** And then the reversal, in her own mouth: **she has kept that school eleven years, there are forty-one registers in the press and in the top corner of every page of every one of them there is a column for the days the school did not open, and she has written in it herself, in her own hand, every day, for eleven years, and has never once looked at the bottom of it, because she thought the old book was a better hand than hers and was ashamed of wanting a number badly enough to go and get it out of a thing she had made herself.** She has about nine days a year in it and has never added it up, because adding it up takes a quiet evening and she has had about nine of those in eleven years. **She went a hundred and forty miles to be told the coast has never kept a record of days, which she could have told anybody, and would have been right, and it would have been worth nothing, because a rightness with no number in it is not a rightness, it is a mood.** Corrie Tallow says: then I am going to copy it. Belva says there is no need. **Corrie's answer is that Belva is not going home off this coast with nothing in her hands, and if Corrie does not copy it then Corrie has spent a morning listening to about four hundred years of a stranger's coast and got nothing, and she would have been the woman who got nothing, and there is enough of that on this road already.** Tomorrow, in the lane, from the second bell, and Belva can read it and check her and take it home, and there is no charge because you cannot charge for it and because she is not doing it for you.

## Chapter 0116 — Thirteen Shillings

**The sixth day of the fifth tide, at the harbour mouth.** Gell Roke, seventy-one, is on a ladder repainting the mark at two feet under on a staff that has stood in the same place for about four hundred years, and he does it when he feels like it and had felt like it on the fourth. Jory Vance, twenty-two, comes down the slip with the returns board because Bettany Rook sent him and because he is the only person in that building who does not know what she wants either. **Gell Roke gives the answer before he is asked twice: on a bad seventh the flat takes nine crossings instead of six, six on the first, second, third and fifth and nine on the fourth, sixth and seventh, and the reason it takes nine is not that the water is worse. The reason is that on those three days the strait is shut, so every cart that has to come round this coast comes over nine hundred yards of shingle instead of going through. And the tariff is a penny a tide on every day of the week including the three days the water is standing up in the middle of it, and the guild has refused twice to write a different price for a day of water, and their reason is good and he has said so to two harbourmasters and a clerk: a tariff that says *dangerous* is a tariff that can be refused, and a man who refuses a tariff is a man you have to argue with on a bad day, and there is nobody at that counter to argue with anybody on a bad day. So the price of the danger is exactly nothing, and he has waited nine years for somebody to write it down.** Then the sum, and the chapter's finding, and the reason a reader is needed: **the boy at the lane end is eleven, his name is on nobody's list, and Gell Roke knows his name and will not say it on a slip because he has been painting this staff for nineteen years and does not put names on the water.** He goes on five days — two low and three high, and that is six and six and nine and nine and nine, which is thirty-nine — thirty-nine tides at a penny is three shillings and threepence, and fifty-two weeks makes eight pounds nine a year whatever the water does. (Both figures corrected by the Batch 0002 review repair pass: the prose said six and six and six and six and nine twice, which is thirty-three and not thirty-nine, and it said a hundred and fifty-two weeks in a year.) On a bad seventh it is forty-two tides, three shillings and sixpence, nine pounds two. **The difference is three pence a week and fifty-two weeks, which is one hundred and fifty-six pence, which is thirteen shillings, and Gell Roke makes the young man write it on the board rather than do it in his head.** He insists the figure be said in the same breath as its own impossibility: **thirteen shillings is what a bad seventh costs that crossing if there is a bad seventh every single week of the year, which there has never been and there never will be, and he will put his hand on it that there has not been one in any of the four hundred years anybody has kept anything at all.** Two pounds is forty shillings, and thirteen is not two. (Corrected by the Batch 0002 review repair pass; the summary previously said thirty-eight.) Thirteen is not two. **Then the shape of it, and it is the whole of what is wrong on that road: the thing on that road that would make the boy safer is a different price on a bad day, and that is worth thirteen shillings a year, and thirteen shillings is not worth a fight, is not worth a season's bad winter, and is not worth a council arguing about in public and being wrong in public and having it in a minute book for ever. And the thing on that road that is not worth a fight is a man to stand at the far end of it, and that is two pounds, and the council has just voted for the two pounds and cannot pay the two pounds, and Gell Roke voted against it and was right about the money and wrong about the only part that mattered, and says he would like it recorded that he knew that on the fourth and voted no on the money.** And the finding inside the finding: **the guild takes three shillings and threepence a week off a woman who mends nets for a boy who runs a lead over that shingle, spends three pounds two a year on a chain and oil and a tarpaulin, keeps nine hundred yards of flat it does not mend or survey and has never been asked to mend, makes six pounds a year on the whole of it, and that six pounds is the only thing on that road the guild is proud of and has never once said out loud. The guild is not making money out of that boy and never has and could not if it tried, and he has been waiting nineteen years for a person to notice it and there has not been one.** Then the middle beat, and it is the answer to the question Jory Vance has to carry up the hill: **he asks Gell Roke whether he will take the two pounds a year, and is told no, and the reason is that the guild has not paid him a penny in nineteen years and he has kept the staff true by doing it anyway, and there is no way on this coast of explaining to nine men why a man of seventy-one should be paid two pounds a year to go on doing a thing he has never been paid for and would not stop doing. If they pay him, then for nineteen years he did it for nothing; and if they find out he did it for nothing, then it is on the staff that it is on the board, and a painted thing a person put there for love is not the same as a painted thing a person is paid for, and the day somebody needs it to be the second one it will not be. He would rather be a man who has never been paid and always has, because that is a thing you can say out loud in a room, and in about nine years there will not be anybody else on this coast who can.** And the last of it: **there is no person on this coast to be it, so it will be whoever is on the stones — and you will write that down as though it were a rule, and it is not a rule, it is a thing happening, and a rule has a person's name at the end of it and this has not got anybody's name at the end of it, and in a year one of those two is going to be in a pane and the other one is not going to be anywhere.** Jory Vance writes that on the back of the returns board, and does not find out until the third of the fifth tide that a sentence of his own came back to him off a cart, written down by somebody else, and was not the sentence he had said. (Repointed by the Batch 0002 review repair pass: the sheet in Chapter 118 is Bettany Rook's and he never wrote on it, and the fourth of the fifth tide is Chapter 119's day.) **The ninth crossing of the sixth day, about seventy people on the shingle. Nim Hask goes over at the ninth with a hand-lead and a cart behind him that is not his and has four carts' worth of business because the strait is shut; Jory Vance is at the chain with the board and no line on it and writes nothing; Corrie Tallow is at the far stones counting every one that goes over, and is not paid and never will be.** Gell Roke watches a boy go the length of nine hundred yards of wet shingle with a channel down the middle, on the worst day of the week, for the ninth time, at the best price of the week, and says it out loud once to nobody: thirteen shillings, that is what it is worth, that is all of it, and it is not worth a fight, and he is still on it nine times, and there is nothing in that anybody can do. Nobody heard him except the boy, who was eleven, and who was wet to the waist, and who went home.

## Chapter 0117 — What a Copy Loses

**THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL LANDS HERE, on the second day of the fifth tide, in the second half of the batch, and it lands in the middle of the chapter and is not the last thing in it.** The head of the lane at Wether Cross from the second bell, on a bench against the lamp oil shed that Seth Garrick lets anybody sit on, with thirty people on the shingle by the third bell for no reason any of them could put into a sentence. Belva Cray brings a chair, a shawl, a pencil, a register and a piece of string and checks the copy line by line. Corrie Tallow is there with her slate and says the thing out loud before she starts: **her hand is not a copying hand, she has copied out her own thirty-one years twice, it took a fortnight both times and she could not read her own a week after, and if she does it in chalk she will do it wrong and Belva Cray will swear to it. She cuts marks for a living and she will do it in an hour.** Sabra Trew comes down from Thornglass at about half past with her heel black with scale, has lost a day of the shed, has nobody's leave, and Hollis Thrale let her go and did not say why, which she has turned over about forty times on the way. Two lists, and she is the only person on the coast who can lay one out and say which hand is which. She does it in marks, because that is what her hand does: a stroke for a week, a space the width of a stroke for a week that is not there, reasons said out loud once and written on a separate paper because a stranger from a school does not need four hundred years of other people's reasons. **About a third of the school list is a list of weeks and the other two thirds is a list of nothing with a few marks in it, and the join between the two hands is visible on the slate half an inch apart, and that is the only place on either slate where you can see the join.** Ilyan stands at the end of the bench and is not reading, because there is nothing on the bench he could read, and asks the question he asked on a wet shingle nine days ago. **What are the marks for.** For counting. For counting what. Crossings. And then Seth Garrick stops and puts the hammer down and turns round: **no, that is what they are for and that is not what you are asking, and you are the second man in nine days to ask me that question in that order and I have got the same answer ready and I have given it twice. The tally post counts crossings. That is what it is for and that is not what you are asking. I have been counting that post in my head for four years because there is nobody to check me and I have begun to think I have been getting it wrong, and if you stand at that post at a turn with a hammer and a piece of paper you will have the only count of it that has ever been made, and it will be wrong, and it will outlast the post.** And then the reason anybody marks a week: **the weeks are the count, because there is nothing else on this coast to count by. The water does not give you a number, a boat gives you a day, a year gives you four hundred weeks, and a week is the only piece of time on this coast everybody agrees is a piece of time. A crossing is a tide and a tide is not a week. A school counts terms. A salt pan counts weeks. A kiln counts firings, and a firing is a week, and a week is not a batch and never was.** Ilyan asks a third time and is told it is the same answer and that he is asking it in the wrong order. Ilyan says: I am looking at a yard. Seth Garrick picks the hammer up and goes back to the hinge. **Then nobody saw the moment.** Thirty people in the yard, nine within twenty feet, and it took about four seconds and it happened because two slates were lying on the same bench. Sabra has brought her grandmother's list, four hundred years in two hands; the firings are nineteen of them and one of the nineteen is a hole four days wide; the grandmother's book that went into the school has four hundred years in it. **She looks at the two at a foot's distance and can feel the shape of a thing the way a person can feel the shape of a room, and goes the colour of the wall, and stops with the chalk an inch off the slate.** Corrie asks what it is. There is a gap in it. There are about four hundred gaps in it. **No: there is a hole in that one and there is a hole in this one, and I have been looking at that one every day since the spring and I know it is four days and I know what made it, and it is not in hers. I have been down that list for an hour and a quarter, I have got to the part where her hand stops and the next one starts, and I have got all the way through, and the four days is not in it. It was not copied.** **What was lost and why: a four-day hole in a list of weeks is not a mark, it is a smudge, it is a gap between two weeks, and a copyist reads a gap between two weeks as nothing, because nothing is what a gap is, and she did not know it was a hole and she did not ask, and she was right not to ask, and that is exactly how it was lost.** And then the fear she cannot name and is not wrong to have: **it is the only interesting thing in four hundred years, and that is what I have been frightened of since the spring and I could not tell you why, and it is not the number. It is that if the four days did not get copied then something did not get copied, and nobody knew, and there are two books in the world out of that one wall and both of them are wrong in a different way, and I am the woman who cuts the marks.** Then the part that is not what it looks like from outside, and it is worth nothing as a shape: what Ilyan sees is a row of strokes and a row of strokes, one of them about nineteen long and one about two hundred, not the same length and nobody claiming they are, and between the strokes in both the same run and the same holes. **A coast is a coast, and about four hundred years of people on two stretches of it have all been counting the same piece of time the same way, and every one of them has holes in it for the same reason, which is that weeks are not like that. He knew what that was worth. It was worth nothing: any counting of anything produces the same shape.** And underneath that, in about half a second, without any decision in it, the other thing. **Its name was *The Sovereign's Wake*, it was a game, he had been the last man to test it, and he had filed a report on it on the final night and had never once been told by anybody that he was right; it had a map on it that most of its players never opened, the map was not of anywhere the game let a person walk, and the people who had made it had built it out of the parts of the world the game did not show you and had then written a song about it.** He could not have said, standing at that bench, what any of that had to do with four hundred weeks in chalk, and he was not going to try, and he put his palm flat on the bench and thought about a pair of cold feet, which is what he thinks about when he wants to stop thinking about something in front of people. Then he asks, in a completely ordinary voice, to a completely ordinary yard, whether the grandmother's book has the four days in it. It does. **She wrote it out twice for me when I was about eleven. She said, that is the one you will remember.** And then the chapter does not end on the pattern. The copy is finished at the fifth bell and Belva Cray checks every line herself for most of an hour, and says the finding of the two days: **she is not going to swear to this in a board room. She is going to swear to the column in the back of her own register, in her own hand, for eleven years, and that is the one she will say out loud. And that is not a humble thing to do and she would like it understood that she is not doing it because she is frightened of this coast — she is doing it because this coast has kept four hundred years of weeks and has never once kept a record of days, and there is nothing whatever on this coast that can turn one of those weeks into so many days, and that is not a sad thing, it is an obvious thing she had not seen from a hill.** Corrie tells her to take it home anyway: it is hers, it cost nothing, she may show it to anybody. Belva will put it in with the register and it will not be a record of anything, and she will write that on it in Corrie's name so that whoever finds it in a hundred years knows. **And Sabra Trew washed her slate out in the yard trough, put the chalk on the wall, and went up the coast road to Thornglass, and she was an hour late for the second turn of the arch, and Hollis Thrale was standing at the fire with a bar in his hand and one pair of hands and no apprentice, and he did not ask her why, and she did not say.**

## Chapter 0118 — The Sentence That Became a Thing

**The third day of the fifth tide, at the head of the lane at Wether Cross, with nine or ten people within twenty feet of the bench and thirty more nine hundred yards off watching the water come in. The batch's false merge, and the cost of connecting two places, and the cost is in the body and in a person.** Corrie Tallow, tired, after two days of going round it: **the two of them are the same list. They are the same stretch of coast and they are the same list, going back. The wall is only the part of it you can see.** Belva Cray, who has checked every line: they are not the same list, the wall begins with the kiln and the book does not, and there is four hundred years of that book that has no kiln in it at all. **Then Jory Vance, with a brush in his cuff, says: then the two records agree, and we can put it that way — and about four of the nine people there agreed with it.** Bettany Rook says no to writing it and then writes something else: the true ones are that a woman with a stall counted the flat for thirty-one years, and a woman at a school kept a list, and a woman at a glassworks cuts marks on a wall, and not one of the three has ever laid eyes on the other two, and every one of them is about this water, and they do not agree, and there is no arithmetic that will make them agree, because one of them is counting tides and one of them is counting firings of an arch, and those are not the same count and never were. Yours says we know it. Mine says we do not. She takes the sheet and writes it herself, in her hand and not a clerk's: **three accounts of the same stretch of water have been compared on this coast and none of them is an instrument; one counts tides and one counts firings of an arch; a list of four hundred years of weeks kept in a school a hundred and forty miles up this coast and a row of marks cut on a kiln wall at Thornglass are about the same ground and were kept by people who never met; one of them has lost four days and the other has not, and nobody can put the four days back; this is on paper, it is not the guild's record and it is not on any list for a fire, it may be wrong and it may be taken down.** **THE JOIN, AND IT IS LEGAL: Bettany goes up the hill; Belva Cray goes up after her with the register, because the copy has to be checked in a room with a table in it and there is no table at the head of a lane. There are three people in that room and Corrie Tallow is not one of them — she is forty yards away under her awning with a line of people waiting, and what she has learned in thirty-one years is that a sentence said in a room with a table in it is a different object from the same sentence said on a bench in the open, and the difference is not in the words. Jory Vance asked her one question about it, because he was twenty-two and had a brush in his cuff, and Belva Cray answered him, and the answer was a woman's sentence and it came back out of him in his own grammar and she took it without noticing, because she is fifty-one and had been on a road for four days and because he had asked it in a kind way. By the time the copy was finished, Corrie Tallow's sentence was in that room in two versions and the one that was on the paper was the one that was false.** Seth Garrick carries the sheet up the coast road in a cart at the fourth bell because he was going that way to buy oil, and it costs him the oil, and he reads it twice on the way because he is a man who reads everything. He puts it on the bench at Thornglass with his hand flat on it for about a minute and says: **there is a woman at the end of this piece of paper who says you are one of three accounts of the same water, and she has said it in the guild's own office, and the harbourmaster wrote it out with her own hand.** Hollis Thrale, sixty-one, forty-one years at the fire, reads it twice and then a third time and keeps his hand on it. **He has a pane on the wall of his own shed the guild has never seen that disagrees with the guild's in two columns out of eleven, and he has known that for two years, and there is no instrument on this coast that would settle it in a room, because the only pane anybody will act on is the guild's and the guild has never let it out of that room in nineteen years. There is a second one. A list kept up this coast by somebody's grandmother, and it is on paper, and paper can be got at. I have held a thing for two years that I cannot use.** **And then Seth says the rest of it, because Hollis Thrale will not let a thing go unsaid: there was a woman at the crossing in a yard yesterday with about nine people round her, and a young man at the counter with a brush in his cuff said out loud that the two records agree, and about four people said aye, and I have not got a way of telling you it is the wrong words because I have been standing at a post for nineteen years and I am not an authority on a sentence. I am telling you that the harbourmaster would not write it down and that a man with a brush in his cuff said it, and that the two of them were about nine feet apart at the time.** Hollis asks him to say the last part again, and asks which part, and listens, and then says: **say that last part again, because a man with a brush in his cuff, because I have been in this shed for two years with a piece of glass on my own wall that does not agree with the only glass anybody will act on, and in that entire two years the one thing I have wanted is a second record I could put beside it, and you have just told me that this morning a man on this coast said the two records agree, out loud, in a yard, and that four people said aye. That is not a witness, that is a man agreeing with a sentence, and I have not asked for that and I would not know what to do with it.** Then he says the thing that cost: **send her up the road and ask her for the book. Not a copy. The book. And I will not put it in an arch and I will not put it in a pane, and there is nothing on this coast that could get a hundred and forty miles of paper into a fire, but I will put it on that bench beside mine, and then there will be two records on one bench in one room on this coast for the first time, and I have wanted that for two years, and you may tell the counter where I got it.** Sabra Trew, from the arch, with a bar in her hand, says **you have not got it.** He has got a piece of paper with a sentence on it that a man said in a yard, and the sentence is in the same yard as the truth, and he has got about four minutes' work of it and does not intend to give it back. **And then the line that keeps her whole and costs her nothing to say: you have got the word *witness* about a piece of paper in a school, and I read that book out in a yard on the second of a tide because I was asked to copy a list of weeks, and I would like it said somewhere that nobody asked me to be a witness to anything and I would have said no.** He says she would have been wrong to, because she cuts a row of marks for a living and is the only person on this coast who can lay one list out beside another and say where the two of them stop and start, and he will not stand in a shed at sixty-one and pretend that is not a thing he wants. **I am going to want it. That is a thing about me and not about you.** Then the cost, and the two halves of it. **The back is his: he went up the coast road with Seth Garrick's cart at the fourth bell and walked back because there was nothing to sit on, and two miles of that road on a leg carrying a gate hand of forty-four's weight out of a city four hundred miles off is about an hour and a quarter of a thing he does not remember afterwards; he came into the yard at Thornglass at about the eighth bell and there was a man of sixty-one at a fire and a woman of twenty-four with a bar and neither of them asked him about it, and nobody on this coast is going to.** The words were a woman of forty-eight's, and she was not in that room, and she was forty yards under an awning with her board turned to the weather, and what had happened to them was that a woman of fifty-one had answered a question kindly in a room with a table in it. **He wrote it down that night in the back of a penny exercise book, on a torn-out page, with the two versions side by side and nothing else on the page, and was never able to find the join, and is quite sure there was no join at all** — the second was not a copy of the first and not a distortion of it either: it was a different sentence made out of the first one by a woman of fifty-one who did not know she was making it, out of a sentence of a woman of forty-eight, by a man of twenty-two who was being useful. **What she said: the two of them are the same list, going back. What came back to her: the two records agree.** He put the page in with two other torn-out pages of the same book and with a sheet of writing paper from a city four hundred miles behind him that has two questions on it in a hand he asked her not to write in. And then, later the same third day, at about the seventh bell and after the sheet has been read out at Thornglass, a carter with an empty crate comes down the coast road and asks at the stall whether the woman is in, and gives her the false version back, and tells her the woman at the crossing said it. **That is not what I said. I said it was the same list going back. There is a difference and it is the whole of what has happened. I did not say they were the same. I did not say they agreed. And I would not have used either of those two words in a yard, and I have kept that count for thirty-one years and I know what a man does with a word that says *agree*.** He says it is on a piece of paper, and he read it off the paper. **Then it is on a piece of paper, and I said it to a person.**

## Chapter 0119 — The Fourth Week of a Season

**The fourth and fifth days of the fifth tide. The fourth week of a season is when the quarter goes up, and the quarter is four weeks of passage money in a hand that has not changed since before Bettany Rook came here, and it is read by the guild's own office in a city two hundred miles up the coast and by nobody else at all.** Nine sheets, about two hundred and eighty figures, and on the fourth day at the first bell she puts them on the counter in a stack and does not open the first one. **There is a thing to say and then there are four hours of work, and I would be obliged if you let me get to the work first. Jory Vance asks what. And this: I have two seasons of sight in this eye and I have had them since the spring, and I have told nobody in this building, and I am telling you because you are the only one who can do the other half of this and I am not going to stand in front of nine people and be asked questions about it. I can read a big number on a good day. I cannot read a small one. I have been reading them for six years by leaning, and leaning is not a thing a person can do for four hours, and I did it for three on the second of the tide and I could not do the last page, and I did it anyway, and it took me until the ninth bell and I do not know what I put on it.** Then: no, you will not read them to me, because you do not know my shorthand and there are eleven things in it that are not money, and if you write a figure from my mouth in your own hand then in a year a clerk two hundred miles up this coast is going to read a figure I did not mean. **I will read them and you will write them. It will take four hours. If I lose a line you will not know and I shall know and I shall tell you and we shall do the line again. And you will not look at me while I am reading, and you will not help me, and I want that in front of us now rather than in the middle of the fourth hour.** And it is four hours and forty minutes. **The room has four people in it and about nine come and go, and nobody asks her one question, and about half of them count. Nobody on this coast can work out how she does it, and that is the finding: a woman of fifty-one reading a column of two hundred figures and saying the odd one out loud and the even one silently, and getting to the bottom of a page and going back up it, and starting a page again, is not a thing that is about her eyes. It looks exactly like a thing that is about her hands.** And at about the seventh bell her hands are not doing it. Stop. Give me the pen for a minute. You have the pen. Then stop reading and do not look at me, and go and stand at the north wall for about a minute, and when you come back we will do the ninth sheet, which is the last one, and we will do it before the office fills up, because I would rather be slow in front of four people than fast in front of nine. **Then the pane, and the order, and the year becoming a sentence instead of a fact.** The order for the fifth pane comes back up the counter that afternoon on the season's own paper with the date struck out in a clerk's hand and no figure against it at all. **Then the year is a year off and it is settled, and I did not settle it and the council did not settle it and nobody in this building asked the council about it, and there is a pane on that wall that cannot be changed until a letting day a year off that I have now got written down in a clerk's hand in the middle of the word *ordered*, and it will be read by a man in a city in about a month, and he will not know what it means either.** Do you want it entered. **No. If it is entered it is a thing this office has decided about, and this office has not decided about anything. If it is not entered it is a piece of paper about a piece of glass, and in a year a man in a shed will put a batch in an arch and the council will be told afterwards if anybody tells it. Put it in the drawer with the other orders and write the date on the outside of the drawer, because I am going to forget, and because the day I forget is the day the fifth pane does not happen at all, and that is a worse sentence than the one in the charter.** **Then he puts it to her in the yard at the sixth bell, in about four minutes, and it is the only thing he does on this coast that is entirely his own idea and entirely useless.** A man at Thornglass has a second pane of his own on his own wall, made three years ago, eleven courses, the same brass and the same hand on the ruling as the guild's, fired about sixteen years apart, and two columns out of the eleven do not agree — the third and the eighth, the two that record a place and not a depth. **I have not finished. No. First: his is three years old and mine is three months old and the charter is nineteen years and eight months old, and all three are photographs. A pane is a man standing beside an arch on one morning with the weather he got. The third and the eighth columns record a place, and a place that has moved is a place that has moved since somebody wrote it down, and that is not a disagreement between two men, it is the channel doing what the channel does. If you could put them side by side in a room tomorrow it would tell you what the water was doing in two mornings and it would not tell you which of the two men is right, and I have been on that water for nineteen years and the answer to that is *both*. Second: the guild's people are right about the water in their hands. That is the whole of the guild and it is a good thing to be. Third: the moment I take this to the council I am a woman saying the guild's own record is bad, and a record that is bad is a record that has to be replaced, and a replacement is a year, and I have a year of sight left in this eye.** **And the last one, and then I am going inside: this office is the only office on this coast that anybody can be answered in, and if I go into a room and say the record is bad, then in about four months the guild will have got a perfectly good reason to put the harbourmaster of Kestrel Reach at a counter for the remainder of her sight, and they will be right to, and I will be the woman who gave them the reason. There are about nine hundred people on this water and every one of them comes to a person. Not to a bench and not to a court and not to a guild. To a person. I am that person and I have been it for six years and I have never once been sure it was a good idea and I have never once had an alternative, and I will not hand in the alternative in a yard to a man with a bad leg because he has been three weeks on this coast and has been careful with me.** He has nothing for that and it takes him about nine seconds to find nothing for it and the nine seconds are the worst of the whole three weeks. **You are right. And there is one more thing I have been carrying since the first day and I would rather be refused on something I chose than go on holding it. The guild holds the only legible copy of the charter because it pays for the only copy of the charter.** Flat, to a person and not to a room: **not because it built it, not because it holds it, because it is the customer. And the customer owns the copy, and no living soul on this coast can ask that a copy of anything be made, and everybody who has needed one in three hundred years has been told that a pane cannot be copied.** The yard was quiet; inside, a pen was going on a sheet and it did not stop. **That is not mine and it is not yours either. You have been on this road three weeks. Whoever taught you to say that has not been on it. Go and stand at the far end of something.** He goes and stands at the far end of the flat for about an hour, on a leg that has a whole of her voice in it out of a city four hundred miles off, and does not get it back for about a minute, and then gets it back worse, and nobody on that coast is told and nobody on that coast is going to be. **Then the volume's antagonist, named as a category in a mouth, by an ordinary person, and not answered.** Danno Ray has a cart at the Quay at about the seventh bell and a load of lime that is not going anywhere for two hours, and he comes down the lane and puts the beasts in and stands with his back on the stones where he can see the whole of the flat. **Here is why it cannot be put anywhere. You cannot put it in the charter, and the charter is a pane and a pane is a year and the year is not ours. You cannot put it in a song, because a song is a thing people agree on and about half of them would get it wrong, and that is not a thing you build, it is a thing that happens to you. You cannot put it in a book, because a book goes. You cannot put it in a return, because a return is a number and there is no column for it. And you cannot put it in a man, because a man is seventy-one and paints a staff when he feels like it and has never been paid a penny of it in nineteen years. So it sits in about nine heads on this coast and nowhere else, and every one of those nine heads is on its way out. I am forty-five. That is the whole of the difficulty and there is no office for it. A rule that cannot be changed for a year is not a rule. It is a season. And a season is a thing that happens to you and a rule is a thing that a person can be told, and the guild has got one of them and calls it the other one, and I say that as a man who has been under both.** Corrie Tallow, forty yards off, has not looked up, because a woman of forty-eight on this coast has learned in four years exactly how far a sentence like that carries and what it costs to be the woman who answers one. Bettany Rook is up the lane with a stack of nine sheets and about four hours of work still in them and has not heard a word of it. Seth Garrick is at his shed door with a hammer in his hand. **Nine people on that shingle and not one of them answered him.** The System panel, the second and last of the batch, arrives at about the ninth bell on the far side of nine hundred yards of dry shingle, and is the third thing on this coast that was a record of a thing nobody had entered anywhere in particular: the charter is one pane in the tide office, nineteen years and eight months old; the guild's own tide table is the other and is about three months old; **a pane is a picture of one morning taken by a man standing beside an arch, and a channel moves, and what is old on this coast is the record and not the harbourmaster**; the order for a fifth pane came back with its date struck out and no figure against it and was entered in a drawer and not in a minute book, and nobody had asked the council for either; and **whether a rule that cannot be changed for a year is a rule or a season is not decided, and it was said out loud on a shingle by a man with a cart and nobody answered him.**

## Chapter 0120 — The Ward of That Road

**The sixth and seventh days of the fifth tide. Ilyan Vester is on the coast twenty-nine days at the end of it. The batch close.** The post at Wether Cross is inspected at about the turn on the seventh day of a tide, once a year, by a man with a hammer, and the old notches are filled flat with a chisel and beaten level and then the new ones are cut. Gell Roke comes down the slip from the harbour mouth with a pot of paint and a chisel and a hammer in a bag and a boy of about ten to hold the pot, and about thirty people are on the shingle because there is nothing else to do on the seventh. **They do it in fives: a notch for each crossing, a hole for each ten, a bar cut across the face for each hundred, and the whole year of that flat goes into a post about the height of a man in about two hours, and then it stands until the turn of next year and is beaten flat and cut again.** Seth Garrick has a piece of paper and a stick of coal and has been waiting four years for the day, and he has a bed over his lamp oil shed and a man with a bad leg sleeping in it, and neither of those things has anything to do with the post and both were paid for in the same currency, which is a day of somebody's trade. **Nobody had ever done this. That is the finding and the whole of the morning: the post had been inspected once a year for as long as there had been a guild at Kestrel Reach, and in the whole of that time there had never once been a second person counting while it was cut, and no figure had ever been written down, and the number that was cut on it went into the wood and stood there and was beaten flat a year later and gone.** Gell Roke cut and the boy called them out in fives and Seth Garrick wrote them in fives, and twice he lost his place and went back three notches and started again, and the second time Gell Roke waited for him without saying anything about it, which is the greatest courtesy available on that shingle. **At the bar. That is a hundred and a bit. Eleven hundred and sixty. It is four thousand and sixty-eight**, and about fifty of the notches are on the underside where the water gets at them and a man cutting in a hurry cuts those twice, which is one of the places the number is known to be wrong and the only place anybody has been able to name. **Two hours and eleven minutes, and it is the only count of that post that has ever been made and it is the wrong count in at least one place and there is no instrument on this coast that could find out which one.** Then Seth Garrick's four-year secret, out loud, in the open, on about thirty people, without being asked: **I have had that in my head for four years. Five thousand, round about. And I have told nobody, and I told a man off a road about it in the rain because I had begun to think I was getting it wrong, and I was. I was wrong by about nine hundred. I have been wrong in my head for four years in a shed on my own, at night, with a lamp, and there was nobody on this road to check me and there was never going to be, and I did it anyway because I have been doing it since I was twenty-six and I did not know how to stop. Nine hundred is more than a fifth of it. That is not a man being a bit out. And I want it written down that I said it out loud on about thirty people on the seventh of a tide with the number on a piece of paper, and that the number on the paper is not mine and mine is on the paper as well, and both of them are now two things instead of one, which is the first improvement I have had in four years and I got it by being wrong in public.** **Then the returns, and the difference between the two counts, which is the batch's other finding.** The returns are made out at the counter from the carts that paid; the tally is of carts and not of persons and has always been; the two have never agreed and the post is not one of the two the returns are made from. Jory Vance brings the year's board down to the shingle because he was told to and because it is a thing you can carry. **Four thousand and sixty-eight. That is not the same thing. No, and it is not a mistake and I have known that for four years and I have never had anybody to say it to. The post is everybody's boots. The return is carts. They were never going to agree and nobody ever counted the difference and the difference is every person on this coast who has ever put a foot on that shingle. Which is about nine hundred people. Every one of us goes over that flat at some point in a year. I have gone over it about two hundred times since I was eighteen and I have never once paid a penny for it, because it is not a cart.** Nobody had ever said that before: the nine hundred people of Kestrel Reach live above about half a mile of water and every one of them crosses a shingle flat to get to a boat, and not one of those crossings has ever been on a piece of paper anywhere on this coast, and the only count of them is a row of notches on a post that has been beaten flat and cut again a dozen times since. **And one boy of eleven is two thousand and twenty-eight of the four thousand and sixty-eight.** Then the board, nailed to the front of the post under the chain at about the fourth bell, where it is in the way of anybody using the crossing, which is the only place anybody on that coast would put anything. Two feet by one, fourpence of nails, two men and about eleven minutes, and Gell Roke has to keep stopping to explain to about nine people: **the count was cut and written down for the first time; the post four thousand and sixty-eight; the returns three hundred and ninety-one in the season and some outside it; the figure that was in Seth Garrick's head for four years was about five thousand and he was wrong by about nine hundred; a figure cut on this post cannot be checked by anybody and a figure in the returns cannot be checked by anybody and the difference between them is everybody; and the count is made at the turn, once a year, by whoever is here.** And under all of that, in the same hand, in smaller letters, because Gell Roke wrote it while the others were arguing about where to put the board: **that last line is the only rule anybody has ever written on this coast about a mark and I am not sure it is a good one.** **The cost, in full and in nothing but days:** an afternoon of a man of thirty's shop, a day and a half of a woman's slate, fourpence of nails, about two hours of a man of seventy-one's daylight, the oil a man of thirty spent on a cart up the coast road that he did not have to go, and nothing at all in money otherwise, because there is no wage for it and the council voted a wage two pounds a year on the fourth day of the fourth tide and cannot pay it and the vote is in a minute book in four lines. Seth Garrick shut the shed for the afternoon and about nine people who buy lamp oil on the seventh were told by a boy of nine that the oil man was busy. Corrie Tallow went down to the far stones four times, once for each tide, and counted every body that went over, for nothing, as she has done for thirty-one years and as she will go on doing now that a board says *by whoever is here.* And a woman of fifty-one in a hood had four hours of a season's figures dictated to a clerk of twenty-two, and everybody in that room counted and nobody asked her one question, and she has not told the guild and is not going to, and the guild would be right to move her to a counter, and she is fifty-one and it is the fourth week of a season and there is nobody on that coast she can hand any of it to. **Then the test, and it is a small physical thing and not a speech.** At about the fifth bell Gell Roke puts the chisel in the bag and the hammer in his hand and then stops, and looks at the post, and then along the shingle at about nine people, and then at a man of thirty-one standing four feet off in a coat with a leg he has been favouring since the third bell. **Nobody had said anything to that man for four hours. He had counted nothing and cut nothing and carried nothing and asked no question and had been useful about nine times in twenty-nine days, and there was no office in this guild, or in any room on this coast, that had ever been given to him or could be, and nobody had asked him to stay and nobody was going to, and his name was not on the board and would not be and could not be.** Then Gell Roke, in full: right, I am seventy-one and I have been cutting that post since before you came off a cart, and there is a hammer in my hand and there is no rule on this coast that says who holds it, and I have not got the pair of hands for a year off and I have never asked anybody for a year off in my life. **He holds it out. That is the ward of that road now. That is all it is. You cut it in a year and you put the number on the board and you do not let anybody tell you it is a different number. And I am telling you it is not a favour and it is not an honour and there is no word on this coast for what it is, because I have looked for one for four days and there is not one, and if you have a word in a city four hundred miles off I would like to hear it and I will not use it.** It went about four feet. There was nobody between the old man and him. **He was the person nearest.** The leg went in about the second swing and went all the way, from the hip down, the way it went, and the hand he could not grip with is the one he got it in, and the weight that arrived in his nose in two of its layers with a third one under both of them was a gate hand of forty-four's out of a city four hundred miles behind him and she was still in it, and the room with the lamp down and the four of them at the table and one of them with her back to him was sitting exactly where it had been sitting since the third of the second month and it did not move. **Gell Roke took the hammer back off him at the fourth swing, without a word and without any expression at all on a face of seventy-one years, and put it in the bag, and closed the bag. All right. That is enough of that. Boy — get my pot.** Nobody on that shingle said one word about it, then or afterwards, and about eleven of the thirty-odd people standing there had seen a man of thirty-one go down on a post and get up on a post, and about four of them had worked out what it was, and not one of them said a word to him about it, **and that is a thing four hundred miles of this coast does about a man it has no standing over.** Then the last night, and the inventory, and the finding, and the question. What he has: a sentence put to a road nine miles by water and refused in the open correctly in front of thirty-one people at eleven pounds of freight and two men's day and four years of a woman who sold rope; a line in a day book with no name on it; **a copy of a woman's thirty-one years on a shelf in a room where it is not the record, on purpose, and a copy of her pencil in a school a hundred and forty miles up this coast in a register where it is a record of nothing, also on purpose, both of them put there by women who knew exactly what they were doing and have told him so in about nine words each**; a sheet of paper with two accounts of the same water on it, one of which he wrote and one of which came back, and he was never able to find the join, and he could not have read either; **a man of sixty-one two miles up the coast holding a pane of his own that disagrees with the guild's in two columns of eleven, offered to a woman of fifty-one on the fifth day of the fifth tide and refused, correctly, in a yard, in about nine sentences, and he is not going to carry it to anybody else, because there is nobody else, and because he was told by a woman of forty-eight at a crossing in the rain that the only way anybody ever moves a person is by standing in a doorway being awkward, and because he had stood in a doorway once on a road between a bridge and a pump, four hundred miles off, with a piece of paper in his hand and a person in it, and it had cost that person a day and a hedge and it had cost him nothing at all**; and a hammer in a bag belonging to a man of seventy-one, and a rule on a board that says the count is made at the turn by whoever is here, and nobody on four hundred miles of this coast has ever been asked whether they would like to be whoever is here. **He had not been asked once. In twenty-nine days on this coast not one person had asked him for anything** — a bed, a meal, a leg's worth of bad light, a bar out of a fire, four turns at a post, a door of a shed, and a sheet of paper read out loud to him one piece at a time by a woman with a ledger who did not help him. Every one of those was given and not one was asked for, and he understood why, and it took him four weeks to be able to say it to himself in a sentence. **The toll on this coast is a penny a tide. A boy's thirty-nine tides a week at a penny is three shillings and threepence, and there are about nine hundred people on this water, and a man with a bad leg who comes off a cart on a wet road and stays twenty-nine days costs the coast exactly nothing, because nobody has a column for it, and because nobody on this coast has ever written down what a carrier is, and because the one place four hundred miles off where it has been written down is a city where a man was produced three times in four months by people who had a tray and a seal and a duty, and every single time it cost a district a day and a half of a cart and it cost him a day of a leg, and nobody ever asked him either.** The board is on the post. The number is wrong in at least one place and the number in Seth Garrick's head had been wrong by about nine hundred and both are on the same board in two different hands, and the difference between the post and the returns is every person on that coast who has ever put a foot on a shingle flat, and one boy of eleven is half of the four thousand and sixty-eight and was still on it nine times that day at a penny a time. **And the arch at Thornglass is cold and will be cold for a year, and the charter on the wall of the tide office cannot be touched for a year, and the two panes that did not agree cannot be put side by side by anybody because the only instrument on this coast is the guild's and the guild's has not left that room in nineteen years. And the year had begun on the fourth week of a season, on a coast of four hundred miles, where a rule that cannot be changed for a year is not a rule, and where a man with a cart and a load of lime had said that out loud in the open to about nine people on a shingle and had not been answered by one of them, and where a woman of seventy-one had put a hammer into the hand of a stranger and called it the ward of a road, and neither of those two sentences had been answered, and both of them were true.** And the question that was left standing on that coast at the end of the twenty-ninth day was not about a boy, and not about a woman of eighty-one, and not about a man off a road with a leg he has been carrying since a city four hundred miles behind him. It was the one that had been sitting in a trough and a yard and a shed and a bar and a post and a board for four weeks, and it was the plainest anybody on that shingle had ever heard, and it was this, in four short paragraphs and a last line of its own: **that on a coast of four hundred miles there are about nine people holding about nine things in their heads because there is no column for any of them and no office and no clause and no way of writing one down, and every one of those nine is on its way out, and the whole apparatus of that coast — a charter, a tariff, a card, a song, a post, a pane, a book in a school, a slate in a basket, a bench in a room with a painted staff on a wall — is a machine for making nine things into one written thing, and the machine has just been shown, on a wet shingle, by a man of seventy-one, not to work.**

## Chapter 0121 — The One Who Was Not There

**The first of the sixth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and it is on the ground, in the open, in a drying yard at the north end, and it is not in a room and it is not at Wether Cross.** Cleve Gannock, thirty-one, the only one of the three brothers who does not go on the water, a dealer in what the flat boat brings in, and a house with a good roof that the coast knows about and nobody says in front of their mother, has a letter of four lines off a man who hires carts up the coast road: he wants the flat boat and the towrope for the turn of the year and he wants to know whether the Gannock shallow is worked by a family or by one person, and he asks because he has to write something on his paper and would rather write the true thing. Cleve cannot answer it in one line, because the answer is three lines long and the three lines do not agree. **What he wants is said out loud, once, in front of both his brothers, and it is this: that father crossed on an ordinary day with an ordinary cart.** Dell Gannock, forty-two, with the wheel, says no and gives his account: the fourth of the second tide two years back, the channel out of the low about three weeks before, the whole flat running with water from end to end, the cart in it to the axle for nine hundred yards, a man in the water up to his middle on the off rein for four hours, and the old man walking at the far end with his boots off, not frightened, sixty-one and not a hand at a wet cart. **It was in the water to the axle. Then a man on a bank would have called it ordinary, because from where I stood you could not tell.** Sorrel Gannock, thirty-six, the flat boat and the towrope, and the only one of the three who will take a boat over the shallow at a bar day, and has said so and neither brother believes her, gives hers: under six inches at the lowest the whole two hundred yards at the north end, the channel across it and not along it, four hours watching the gravel go, and **I never saw your cart, Dell. I never saw it. Not once.** The three accounts do not agree and none of the three of them is lying, because the shallow where the boat is and the flat where the cart was are four hundred yards apart and are not the same water, and on the morning in question the channel had made a new one across the flat and had not yet made one at the north end. Ilyan Vester is standing four feet away on a bad leg, having come up the coast road because the arch is cold, and he has not been asked one question in an hour, and he says the only true thing anybody has said all morning: **it is the same channel, in two places, and a piece of a channel is a channel, and it cannot have been in the low at one end and out of it at the other on the same morning, and that is not a thing anybody gets wrong.** Then he says the part that matters, and says it to Cleve's face: **your father crossed with a cart, and the cart is what a man hiring a boat has to be told about, and if you are told the cart was in the water to the axle he will not hire the shallow, and if you are told it was an ordinary day he may, and you would be telling him a thing about your brother's water so that he will hire your sister's.** He refuses to say the true part twice. At the end, alone with the letter, Cleve asks whether three people can be made to say one thing, and he answers honestly: **you would not be making them say it. You would write down what all three of them said, and put one sentence at the top of it, and the sentence would be true, and it would be the only part anybody would read.** It is a bad thing when the sentence is the one you wanted before you asked the question. And then he stops, and there are four seconds in which he works out that he has just described a thing he has done three times in four hundred miles, in the future tense, to a stranger, as a way of helping. **Then I will come and find you tomorrow,** says Cleve, and takes his letter, and goes up to the bait.

## Chapter 0122 — A Bar Day Is the Only Day the Shallow Is a Road

**The second of the sixth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and the chapter is mostly outside and it is not in a room: a hard above the tide line with the flat boat up on her bilge, the garboard up, a canvas off.** The boat is the whole of the chapter's physics and the reader is given it in the first two hundred words: no keel, a flat bottom of larch, a plate of hard wood let into the middle line and riveted through, and the wood of it is what has been in the gravel of a bar for eleven years. **Pell Cawley, fifty-two, a boat's carpenter with a slip at the north end and two boats under him, thirty-one years building and mending flat-bottomed boats, comes up the hard and puts his thumb under the collar and says it in three sentences: the wood has gone round it, it is not cracked and not rotten, it will hold this season and it will not hold the season after, and the day it lets go it lets go in the middle over the deepest part where there is nothing to pull on out there.** He wants the fourth — his own boat comes up on the sixth and it has to be on the ways by the turn or it is a year on his slip. **I do not want your money. If I put a collar on that boat and she comes back in the turn she will be right for another eleven years and I will be sixty-three and I will remember putting it on. That is a better trade than money.** Then a carter, Vanner, with a load of lime, and the ordinary arithmetic of a bar day from a working mouth, **and the toll on that crossing is a penny a tide and a tide is a crossing and the crossing counts what crosses it and not what floats over the top of it**: the load goes in the boat, everything that is not nailed to the shore goes in the boat, forty minutes over and about half an hour waiting for the water to come back to him, and if the wind gets up there is eleven yards and no wind in the right quarter. **You cannot get him a cart. Because the middle of that bar is the soft place, and it is soft because the ebb out of the shallow runs through it, and the soft place is the only thing on the bar that has ever been deep, and a loaded cart goes straight to it and sits there.** Pell Cawley then gives the whole of the geography of that water in the negative, in the trade's mouth, and it is the chapter's map: **There is no other water. There is the Reach, which has four and a half miles of water in it from end to end and a bar of gravel four hundred yards across at the mouth of it, and there is the flat, which is nine hundred yards of shingle and a chain, and there is the road up the coast, which is for carts and not for boats and is eleven miles longer. Three pieces of water and one of them is only a road when the sea is standing on the other one.** On a good day the ebb runs long and soft through the middle and the middle is the soft place; on the fourth the sea is standing up, the ebb is short and hard, and it brings the sand out of the channel and packs the middle flat for about an hour and a quarter, and then it comes back, and it is the wind and the sand, and a man who tells you it is a road every week of your life is a man who has been on it forty times and has never had it go out under him. **You could not put it in a letter if you had a clerk. And you could not put it in a pane and I would not put it in a pane.** And then the want, said plainly, and the reader is asked nothing about it: eleven years and a man has not once asked her name, he knows the name on the boat, and there is nowhere on this coast to put the name of the person who did it — the bar is paint, the post has a board on it with two numbers on it, and the only place on this coast where a person putting their foot over a thing gets written down is a line in a day book at the Reach four hundred yards south of that hard, it has got no name on it on purpose, and it is a different piece of water. **So there is no place. And I am telling you that there is no place as a man who builds the things, and not as a man who is being kind.** The new ash collar goes on in four hours and a quarter. At the end, on the stones, she is asked what would have to be true for it to be in a letter, and she gives the two conditions and then the one that stops it: **it is true about eleven times in twenty, and the eleven is not a fixed eleven, and the only person on this coast who can put a number on it is a person standing in my boat with her hand on the bottom. And a person reading it in two hundred miles could come and find out. And that is the one that stops it.** So it is a true thing and it is worth nothing, and it is worth nothing because it is only true while she is in the boat, and there is no paper in the world that can come and find out. **You may put that in your book, and it will be the first thing anybody has written down about me and it will not be a name.**

## Chapter 0123 — Three Accounts and One Sentence

**The third of the sixth tide, in a net loft over the drying yard, with a fire in the grate, and the tide coming in under the boards about four minutes before anybody in there can see it. Not the guild house, not the tide office, and not a room with a table in it.** Cleve called it, which none of them expected, and the man with the bad leg is on the ladder at the third bell because a man he had met for two days said *come up* and went back down and did not explain. He asks Sorrel, then Dell, then Cleve the same question — say the whole of it in order, first thing to last thing, do not stop for questions — and gets three good accounts that do not agree on one point both of them are certain of; and then he asks all three the second question in the same words, *was the water over the road that morning or was it not*, and the third, *where was the channel that morning*. **Over the flat, said Cleve. I could not tell you. From the bank it looked like an ordinary day and I have not been in that water in my life and I am not going to now. Over it, said Dell. Every yard of the nine hundred. To the axle of the cart and over the boards of it twice. Not at the north end, said Sorrel. Under six inches at the lowest and the lowest was about the eighth bell. And the road is four hundred yards south of where I was.** Twenty minutes of the three of them arguing, and the argument is about different things and is in all three cases right: Dell wants it settled that a cart cannot cross a shingle flat with the channel out of the low and will not say how he knows it; Sorrel wants it settled that a boat is a boat, four times in four different ways; **Cleve wants the thing his father did put in a place where it will be still there, because a man two days up the coast road is going to write something on a piece of paper in a book and everything a man writes is decided before he sits down.** Then the thing that is the best-shaped thing he has found in a month: **three accounts, one thing they were all sure of, and a test.** He writes the three down in full, in his own hand, under their own names, and reads each one back to the person it belongs to, which nobody remarks on. Then he writes one sentence at the top of the page. **ON THE MORNING IN QUESTION THE CHANNEL WAS NOT IN THE LOW. A CART WENT OVER THE WATER AT THE NORTH END AND CAME OFF DRY. THE WATER WAS UNDER SIX INCHES.** Sorrel reads it over his shoulder twice and says no, and gives him exactly what she objects to: **it is all in the room and it is not one of the three things anybody said, and my six inches was over a gravel bar with a boat on it, and Dell's not-in-the-low was about a shingle flat with four feet across the middle of it and nobody in this loft has said the words *four feet*, and Cleve's cart came off a flat under an inch of water four hundred yards south of where either of us was. So put my six inches where I said it, or do not put it in at all.** He says he is putting it where she said it, and she sits down, and the letting go is the worst part of the morning and he does not understand it for about a day and a half. He finishes the page in nine minutes, puts the date on it and the two questions, and there is **no reason on the page, and there had been no reason on the page for three pages now**, and the pad it was written on is in the inside pocket of his coat with a sheet of writing paper from a city four hundred miles behind him. **He wrote it on the last leaf of a pad out of his coat and not in the back of the exercise book, because a book with a torn leaf in the back of it is a different object and there is only one of him.** He tears the sheet out along the perforation and gives it to Cleve, who reads it twice and says **that is what I wanted** and **I do not need it to be wrong. I need it to be short.** And goes down the ladder. And the man with the bad leg sits on the bottom step and works out that he is pleased: nine minutes, a page he paid for, no name anywhere that could be used against anybody, and nobody on this coast sent for him, and he came into a loft because a man said *come up*, and he came out of it with the only thing he knows how to make, and it had not cost a soul a thing. Then the leg goes at the second rung and comes back worse.

## Chapter 0124 — What a Fitter Will Not Say

**The fourth of the sixth tide, a bar day, the strait shut, the flat takes nine, and the chapter is mostly water and weather.** The sea outside is standing up in a westerly and at the north end the water goes up the flat and the gravel bar with it and stands over the whole of it, and then goes out again, hard and short, with about nine feet of it in the sea outside. Sorrel Gannock takes the flat boat off the hard at the fourth bell with Vanner's lime in her and the towrope coiled in the bow and Pell Cawley's new ash collar in the bottom, and the man with the bad leg goes in it because there is nowhere else to be on a bar day, and Dell is on the flat with two crossings and there is nothing on nine hundred yards of shingle to talk to. The reason the three days nobody will work are the three days this boat works is given as a physical fact and nothing else: **on a good day the ebb runs through the hole in the middle of the bar and the hole is the deepest thing on it and it is soft, and on a bar day the ebb is about an hour and a quarter and it is hard, and it comes out of the hole carrying sand, and it puts the sand in the hole, and for the length of that ebb the hole is not a hole.** He puts his hand flat on the bottom and can feel the sand come under it in grains in a wave of them, the way you can feel rain on a roof. The rendering comes up on him the way it does on a moving deck — the boards, the table, the lamp with the wick down, four people at the table and one of them about forty-four with her back to him, at the height and the distance it has been at since the third of the second month in a city four hundred miles off — and it did not move, and the cold place in his hip did the six seconds it does and took the hand out of his left, and by the time it was over they were across and the boat's bottom was grinding on hard stuff. **It was hard all the way. Not hard the whole of it. Hard from the crown to the far bank. The near hundred yards was soft at the north side and there is a place there that will never be hard on the fourth, and I went round it, and I have gone round it eleven years and I will go round it when I am sixty.** Then the cart's wheel comes off at the second bell on the shingle above the flat, and the man who comes out of the yard first is **Ostre Brann, fifty-eight, a wheelwright with his yard two Reaches down this coast, up the road with a load of hoop iron and a stop to get a collar cut.** The physical test is the smallest possible thing and it takes nine minutes once you know it: a line laid across the rim at four places, and a sound wheel touches at four places the same distance apart, and a sprung one touches at one and stands off at another, **and you cannot argue with a thumb.** It is done twice, with the disc on and off, and then the felloe ends are felt and there is a crush about a third of the way along, opened and closed in a wedge, with shingle in it. **Shingle in the wood. That is four or five stones in the felloe ends, and they are not in there from the road, they are from your own flat. Somebody has been over that flat loaded, and going over loaded, and hitting, and hitting on the same place more than once, and every time it hits it closes the wood a little and opens it a little. That wheel is not broken. It is sprung, and it has been sprung for a long time.** Then the trade's answer, true and useless: **A repair is not a date.** There is a piece let into the nave and pegged, the pegs standing proud, old, not his, a good piece of work by a man who is dead or gone out of his trade — **that tells you a wheel once came in with a crack in it and a man mended it properly. It does not tell you when.** And the mark on the hoop: a crossed hammer with a nick out of the left arm of it, Ness Tarn's punch, Ness nine years dead — **A mark says who bought the iron. It does not say when the wheel was built, and it does not say when the wheel went into the water, because the iron does not know anything about water. A smith's name is the only thing on this coast that does not change hands and it is worth nothing to you.** **You cannot get a date off a wheel. You can get a condition off a wheel and a condition is not a season.** The carter is Sarl, thirty-five, on that road since seventeen, not a mystery and not going to be told anything, and he takes the wheel and the lime up the coast road at the fifth bell a day late. Brann goes up the road with the iron and loses the afternoon to rain. And the man with the bad leg walks nine hundred yards of dry shingle to the end of the flat and the leg goes in at the second bell on a moving deck and comes back worse and is not told and is not going to be.

## Chapter 0125 — A Pane Is a Person, a Fire, and a Year

**The fifth of the sixth tide, in the shed at Thornglass, and the arch is cold and will be cold for a year, and behind it is two feet of brick, and the only fire in that shed is the second one, which is a hole in the end wall with an iron plate in front of it, and it is where the small work goes.** Sabra Trew is scraping the floor of it out and she does the second fire on her own about six days in seven, **because the second fire cannot be left once it has been let in. If it is banked for an hour the glass in it goes cloudy and cloudy is not a thing you can mend.** She is twenty-four, in her third year, paid nothing, and her back is the shape of a person who has been leaning over a low fire for three years. She tells him the price, because he was in the yard when the old man said *a person, a fire, and a year* to a man with a good coat, and because she has been the person in that sentence for three years and nobody has ever asked her what it was. **A pane is eleven months.** The glass is turned about forty times over eleven months and every turn is a fire and a half and somebody standing beside it with their hands on iron, and there is one day a year the glass will come out of the arch without being broken by the change in the air, and it is chosen by the tide and not by the man, and if the money is not in the arch on the right day then there is no batch and the next one is a year after it. **So there is a price for it and the price is a person for eleven months, and the person is whoever is here, and there is one apprentice in this shed and I am twenty-four and I have been here three years and I have been paid nothing for three years.** Then the part she has not said to anybody, said to a man off a road because he cannot do anything with it: **the guild has paid it twice already and got a pane twice and I was not the one who paid either of those.** The tide table on that wall is three months old and three months of it is three months of her with her hands on that bar; the charter is nineteen years and eight months old and the woman who stood at that fire was called Mare and is dead, and her name is inside the lid of the batch book because that is how you keep a name. **And if it came in the winter with the money in the same post, then the year would start in the winter with me in the arch on the first of it, and it would be eleven months of my back before anybody in that guild house knew whether there was going to be a pane at the end of it.
