And that is how it works, and it is not anybody being cruel, and it has been done to me twice already.** Hollis Thrale had been standing on the other side of the second fire, on his deaf side, and had not turned round for any of it: **he cannot hear a voice on his right and has not been able to since he was a boy, and it is not a thing that happened to him in one place: his father could not, and his father's father could not, and the kiln was built in the year of the bad seventh by a man who worked one-handed because of it, and nobody in that family has ever been asked about it, and the whole of the family knows.** And she raised her voice by about a third on the last three sentences without appearing to notice, which is what you do after eleven years. Then the bench, and what is on it: his own pane's notes in his own unruled hand, the batch book, the yard book, and Bettany Rook's sheet, which he read three times, has not given back, and has said out loud he does not intend to return. **There is nothing of theirs in this room and there is nothing of mine either that a body in the guild house will ever act on. On that wall, a mile up the hill, there is a thing that came out of this arch and nobody may go and look at it. On this bench there is a thing that comes out of this arch and nobody can take it away. Both of those are the same record and only one of them is a record.** Then the second half of the chapter, which is a man of sixty-one working out what he would have to ask for and the cost of asking being a year. **To settle the third and eighth columns, a man would want to know what the water did in those two places, and not what somebody wrote down about it, and not what a photograph of one morning says: a lead line, a boat, a man who can sound, and a day when the sea is doing whatever it is going to do that week, and not a day somebody remembers. Four things. That is a measurement.** And to ask for it he writes to that guild, and they will want to know what he wants in return, and there is one thing they can give anybody, and it is a year, and the year is not his to spend, and it would be the girl. **I cannot ask. Not this season and not the next one unless the fifth pane comes back with a figure against it, and it came back with its date struck out and no figure at all, and a man who writes to a guild in the middle of a season where the passage money is short is a man who has asked for a year and has been given a season.** **I have not refused to measure the water. I have refused to buy the measurement with a girl's back, and there is a difference and I have not found the wording for it in forty-one years.** And then the letter, said once, plainly, and not made into anything: a works two hundred miles up this coast wants her in the spring, she has not answered it, she has told her master there is a letter and he has said nothing, and **I stand at this fire when you are not, and if I am two hundred miles off in the turn then this fire is not let in for a year, or it is let in by a man of sixty-one on his own, and both of those are worse than what happens now. That is the reason. It is not a deep one. I have made it sound like one and it is not.** Nobody wrote it down, and the cost of the next pane would be paid in advance by a woman of twenty-four who had not been asked.

## Chapter 0126 — The Tide Was Held

**The sixth of the sixth tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, and the guild's own works are out on it, because the sixth is one of the three days of the week when nine hundred yards of shingle is the only road there is.** The chain is down, the two shorter posts are out of their sockets and lying on the shingle in the dry, and eight men are capping nine yards of the sea wall at the far end, two days into a job and wanting one more day and a half. **Hal Bevan, fifty-four, the guild's own man on that flat for nineteen years, and it is his whole trade, and he is not a person anybody on this coast would call important. He puts the chain up and takes it down. He knows where the ground is good enough for a cart to stand and where it is not, and he knows it to about a yard, and he has never written any of it down.** A cart can go over at any of the nine as long as the stone is not on it and the boys are off the line, and that is the whole of the rule and there is nothing in it about a tide. There is a cart at the near end and it has been there since the first bell and it will go on the third, same as the last eight times this year. **You are not going to stand about and watch water, are you.** Then the instrument. In the inside breast pocket of the man on the chain is a sheet folded in four, in a hand that is not his and not any hand in the guild, and it has been there since the second bell of the third of a tide, when Cleve Gannock — a dealer who cannot leave a thing alone — read it out loud twice to about nine men in a works' lodging-house kitchen and then wrote it out again in his own hand and gave a copy to the man who puts the chain up. The sheet says the water at the north end was under six inches and a cart went over and came off dry. **The guild has an instrument. It is on the north wall of the tide office, in an oak frame, and it is about three months old, and it is the only thing on four hundred miles of this coast that anybody will act on when there is a question about water in it, and for the morning in question it says four feet at the lowest at the north end.** Six inches and four feet is three feet six inches, and they cannot both be right, and nobody on this coast can put them side by side in a room, because the only thing that would do it is a pane and the only pane in a room is the guild's and it does not come out of that room and the next one is a year off and the money is not in the arch. **What Bevan had worked out over two days, and had said out loud to nobody, was that he did not have to know which of the two was right. That was the whole of it.** At the third bell he comes back down and holds the near end. **Not today. That load is not going over the flat this tide and that load is not going over this week, and I am not going to argue about it with you, and I will tell you why and then you will do what you like about it.** A table on a wall a mile up the hill says four feet and a piece of paper in a dealer's hand says six inches, and he cannot get one of them in front of the other, and **a man who does not know which one is right does not put a loaded cart over a flat on the third tide, and I have been doing this nineteen years and I have never once not known, and I am not going to start on a sheet off a dealer.** The carter is **Wend Garrow, thirty-three, on that road since he was nine**, who is not angry and not loud and tired in the ordinary way, and who says plainly what it has cost: a day and a day's hire and two men paid for the day whether they are there or not and horses that have eaten and a stone that is paid for and does not care. **I am not saying you are wrong. I want that written, because I am going to say it up the road to four people before the turn and I would like it said that a man put a chain up this morning and would not put a cart over, and gave a reason, and the reason was a piece of paper and a wall, and did not tell me to go to hell.** And then he asks the one question that matters: **Will it be the same on the seventh.** And Bevan, who knows the seventh is the day the harbourmaster does not know and has never pretended otherwise in nineteen years, takes about four seconds and says yes, and says it will be the same the week after, and **not until somebody puts those two things side by side in a room, and there is not going to be a room.** Then the batch's own shape turns over on the shingle, in the open, about thirty feet off: the man with the bad leg says **I know where that sheet came from** and gets as far as *the three of them are all right about the water they were in and that is the whole difficulty* before Wend Garrow answers him. **It does not help. I want to be fair with you about that, because I can see you are going to go and sit on that stone tonight over it. It does not help me one hour. But I will say the other thing, because I have got a day to fill and there is nothing else in it: nobody has ever told anybody anything they did not know was the reason.** He goes up the shingle and the stone stays on the near side for the rest of the day and the whole of the seventh. Jory Vance makes the day-book entry standing up at the chain, because that is where he was. And the System's first of two panels in the batch, the fourth thing on this coast that was a record of a thing nobody had entered anywhere in particular, says the tide was held, the load was not carried, the reason entered is that an account and the guild's own table do not agree and cannot be put side by side, that the account was written by a man off a road, that it is true of three separate pieces of water and is in one place, and that nobody has checked it and nobody can; and it ends on the fact that both were made by persons who were on the water, that the man who held the tide was right either way, and that the cost of being right is a carter of thirty-three with two men on it.

## Chapter 0127 — The Seventh

**The seventh of the sixth tide, a bar day, and the chapter is about the day: what a coast does on the one day of the week when the water owns the bar.** Gell Roke is at the harbour mouth at the eighth bell with a pot of paint and a hammer in a bag and a boy of about ten holding the pot, and he is doing the staff, and the reason he does it then and then is a thing nobody has ever asked him: **the only moment in the week a man can paint a mark at two feet under is the turn on a seventh, because that is the only time the water at the harbour mouth stands up over the two-foot mark. He repaints it when he feels like it and the feeling comes to him on the seventh about twice a season.** **You will hold that pot up and not down. A man that holds a pot down has no arm in it** — and he says it again at the end of the chapter and the boy has not asked him. The mark is short by four hundred yards, and about two hundred people on that coast have worked that out at some time in their lives and not one of them has ever said it to anybody. By the ninth bell there are about seventy people on the flat, which is more than a day that takes nine gets on an ordinary sixth, and there is no reason for it that anybody could put into a sentence except that everybody knows where everybody else will be standing on a seventh. The chain goes up and comes down nine times; Corrie Tallow is at the far stones with her slate for the ninth crossing, unpaid and always there on the ninth; the guild's works are on the far side, a day and a half into a job and two days and a half by the turn; a boy of eleven goes over for the ninth time at a penny a tide and nobody thinks anything about it, including him. **At about the turn Seth Garrick sang at the stone. It is four lines and it is a tariff, and it is sung every seventh at the turn by one man, and it is not written down and it is not on any board, and about nine people on that coast have worked out in the last year that the song and the custom of the stone are two customs and not one, and nobody has said so to anybody else.** The tally post is there with the board on the front of it and two numbers and a difference and a line under them, the fourpence of nails hold, and nobody put a hand on it. Then **the person it hit.** Wend Garrow comes over the flat at the ninth with an empty cart, which is not a thing a carter does on a seventh, because his load of stone is four hundred yards on the wrong side of the water and the man on the chain told him in plain words on the sixth, with nobody else standing there, that the flat would not take a cart on the seventh either. Seth Garrick tells him the whole of it in four sentences without being asked — the sixth held, the reason a page against a wall, the man who held it was right either way — and then gives him the two things he has instead of a way, and the price of each. The first is that the water will be back the way it was on the eighth and probably the ninth, and there is a woman up that hill who has been wrong about the seventh for six years and is right about everything else on this coast, and he has never told a living soul and is not going to start with him. **So there is no getting around the third, the fourth or the sixth for your stone.** The second is the thing that plants Batch 0004, given honestly and with its limits stated twice: **there is one place on this coast where a person can stand and ask a question and be answered out loud, in front of everybody, and I have never told anybody that either because it has never once been any use to anybody I have known. The tide stone, at the head of the flat, on the fourth and the sixth, at the turn, one question, one answer, in the open, and nobody may ask a second, about a hundred years, written down nowhere, and the guild has never interfered with it and never will.** She will not lie and will not hedge and will not ask him for anything, and there is not one person on four hundred miles with a better right to an answer. **And she answers about the water. That is all the stone is. It is for the water.** And, said flat, to his face: **you already know what the answer is going to be. It is going to be about the water.** Then the cost, said in the ordinary way and without heat: **I am not going to stand on a stone about a load of stone. I have been about twenty-four years putting things up coast roads and I have never once asked anybody a question out loud and I am not starting at thirty-three because a man in a lamp oil shed told me the truth about a stone. I have lost a day. A day is a day. I will take the long way and it will be a day and a half, and I will tell the man whose wall it is that it is a day and a half because a sheet of paper and a wall did not agree, and he will say *that is your business* and he will be right, and I will have said it, and my two men will have been paid for two days and I will have the stone up on the fourth of next tide instead of the seventh.** He wants it written down somewhere in the same four lines as a rule that cannot be changed for a year. Nobody wrote anything down. And the man with the bad leg walks nine hundred yards of dry shingle to the end of the flat and the leg goes in on the last of it and all the way, and about nine people are on the flat and not one of them says a word to him then or afterwards.

## Chapter 0128 — What a Pane Is For

**The first of the seventh tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and the chapter is in two halves: a letter read out in a yard at the head of a lane, and a shed at Thornglass where a man who is right about glass is told what glass is for by the woman who stands at the fire.** The letter is Seth Garrick reading it twice with the sheet in both hands: the book is in the back of a register in a school a hundred and forty miles up the coast and not in the press, because a press is a thing twenty-six children go into; there is a sheet laid in front of it in another woman's hand saying in Corrie Tallow's name that it is a record of nothing and that it may be shown to anybody; the fourth pigeonhole has been shut and there is nothing in it but a hat and a slate with the frame off; **she is sorry about the letter and she is not sorry about the book, and she says that whoever told her to send it a hundred and forty miles has cost her four days and eleven shillings and the money for a desk, and she has got what she came for and it is not that.** Then the disposal, in a working man's mouth and not as a scene: **they are four hundred years of weeks that were kept as marks on a kiln wall two miles along this coast, and the woman who kept that wall copied them out twice. She sent one of them in letters to a school a hundred and forty miles up this coast, because a school has the only dry locked room on it, and that is the copy the woman at that school has had in a pigeonhole for eleven years, and a copyist reads a gap between two weeks as nothing, and that is what a gap is, and the woman who copied it did not know it was a hole and did not ask, and she was right not to ask. She kept the other one herself, and it is the thing that came down to this yard on a slate about a fortnight ago, and nobody has asked for it back. Then a woman of twenty-four cut the letters one into marks on a slate in this yard in an hour and a half, and a woman of fifty-one checked every line of it against the letters one herself, standing up, with a pencil, and then said out loud what she had found to about eleven people, and carried it a hundred and forty miles home in the bundle under her coat, and nobody in that building has had it open since the day it arrived. So the whole of four hundred years is a wall two miles along this coast and the back of a register a hundred and forty miles up it, and the four days are in the wall and not in the register, and the only person alive who has read all of it line by line is a woman of fifty-one with a pencil in a yard, and the only person alive who can read a mark is a woman of twenty-four at a fire two miles along this coast who is paid nothing. That is not a mystery and there is nothing to chase. It got lost because the people who had it did not know it was worth keeping, and he knows why they did not know, because it is the same reason as everything else. He asks whether the rest of it is wanted now or at the glassworks, and it is at the glassworks, which is how the chapter gets its second half.** Seth Garrick also says, and it is the only place in the batch that the women on that book are placed at all, that there are three of them and that he could not have told you that a fortnight ago: **one of them keeps a school a hundred and forty miles up this coast and has a column in the back of her own register that she has written in herself, every day, for eleven years, and she is going to stand up in a room in the turn and swear to that column and to nothing else; one of them has a stall at Wether Cross and has counted what crosses that flat in persons into a penny exercise book she pays for out of the stall, and read the whole of the four hundred years out under an awning for an hour and a half and would not copy it in her own hand, and gave the reason for that out loud as well; and one of them is two miles along this coast at a fire, is twenty-four, is paid nothing, cut the marks in an hour and a half, and washed the slate out in the trough in that yard when she had done and put the stick of chalk on the wall and went up the road.** The washed slate is **Sabra Trew's, once, in the yard where the copying was done, and she is the apprentice at Thornglass and Mare's granddaughter**; the stall is Corrie Tallow's, forty yards from the head of the lane, and the twenty-six children are the ones that school has. At the shed there is an order on the bench, the guild's season's own paper, a pane of the guild's water and passage table to be fired and delivered in the turn, with a date against it and no figure against it, and it has been under the batch book since the second bell and is going to be under the batch book at the turn. **Because a pane is a year, and the year is whoever stands at that fire, and I am sixty-one and I would not be let out of my own arch on my own, and so the year is her. And I have got an order with a date on it and no money on it, and a man does not open a fire for a year on a date.** It is not the same order as the charter's burden — it is the tide table again — and **it is the same fire, and the fire does not know what is going into it.** Then the real business: a third pane. Hollis Thrale wants one made, for himself, on his own wall, of the same water, fired this year, by his own hand, with his apprentice at the fire, **and it is to go beside the one he made three years ago and it is to be the third**, and the argument is that the only instrument anybody will act on is theirs and it does not come out of that room, so if there are three and two of them are his and made four years apart by the same man with the same hand on the ruling and the same brass, then the channel has moved and that is not a thing a person can argue with. And he has had that argument for two years and it has cost him nothing yet because he has not been able to pay for it, and if the money were there tomorrow he would put it in the arch and it would be the best glass he has ever made. **Then the person who stands at the fire puts the bar down in the middle of it, which she has done once in three years, and says it, and is not wrong.** **You cannot settle a disagreement between two photographs by making a third one. A photograph is not a witness. It does not answer anything. It says what a channel was doing once, on a day, to a man who was standing beside an arch, and a channel moves, and if you fire a third one this year you will have three mornings and no more truth than you have now. And you do not need a photograph, you need a number, and a number on this coast is not made in an arch. A number is made with a lead line and a boat and a man who can sound, and a day when the sea is doing whatever it is going to do, and it does not last and it does not go on a wall and in four months it is a thing in a man's book that he will swear to in a room if anybody ever wants him to. That is what you have wanted for two years and it has cost you nothing and you have not had it because you have been going about asking for the wrong thing, and I have watched you ask for the wrong thing for two years and I have never once said so, because when I came here you told me the second fire cannot be left and I have been at it since about the fourth hour of my first week.** She is right. **Say the last of it again. No. You have said it once and I have asked you for it twice in my life and I am not going to be a man who is asked a third time and expects a different answer.** And he is wrong in the way people from roads are wrong, and says so: **You have given me four things you cannot do in a building. And I have asked for four things I can do, and I can pay for, and a woman of twenty-four is not part of them.** And she says: that is fine. Then Ilyan almost says she is right, and does not, and says instead that he has had it in his mouth — and is corrected: **I have watched you ask for the wrong thing for two years and I have never once said so.** **It is a difficulty about a pane and about a fire, and you have had it backwards for a month. If the guild will not bring the charter out of that room then no third pane on this wall will fetch it out of that room, and you are standing in my shed telling me the trouble is a building. The trouble is that a woman of twenty-four is the only person on this coast who could stand at that fire for eleven months and the guild has not noticed that and neither have you.** He is right about the photograph, and she is right about the photograph, and he has been wrong about what there is instead for two years. Ilyan says he had it in a building twice before and asks that it be noticed that he has noticed; **noticed by me, and not by anybody else on this coast, and it makes no difference whatever.** And then the order goes back under the batch book, and the reason is not the money: **I am not going to ask her, because I know what she will say and I would be asking a woman of twenty-four to agree to a year in a bad season for a thing that has no money against it, and that is not a question. That is an instruction with a question on the end of it.**

## Chapter 0129 — The Fourth Time

**The second of the seventh tide, in two halves, and the second half is four miles up the coast road and back on a leg and a day.** The first half is the guild's quarter going up the coast road in a cart, read by the guild's own office two hundred miles off and by nobody else. Bettany Rook starts it at the first bell, would be four hours at it, will not be helped, and gets to the fourth sheet and stops; **and she has the rest of it out on the boards inside an hour, which it has not been done in nineteen years, because a quarter is four weeks of sums and a sum does not stop for a line with a wage on it and no person on it.** The System's panel reaches him on the lane below the office at about the second bell, so the quarter is out before the second half of the chapter is found. The sheet is ready by the third bell, the day's post goes out on the end of the counter at about the third bell, and he takes the sheet off the counter then and carries it himself instead of sending it up in the afternoon cart. **The clock is fixed and the next writer may not move any of it: out of the counter at the third bell, over the top of the rise at the sixth, back at the head of the lane at the ninth and in the dark — four miles each way, about a day, nothing to sit on.** **There is a line in this quarter for a person, and it has been in the quarter since the fourth of the fourth tide and been empty for nineteen days, and I am not going to write a name in it, and I am not going to ask anybody to write a name in it.** The council voted five to four for two pounds a year out of what the crossing takes; the money is not there; four of the nine were right; she has not put him back on the crossing and nobody has asked her to and she is not going to start asking people for things in the fourth week of a season with a short one coming. **He is thirty and he has been counting that post in his head for four years and got it wrong by about nine hundred and said so out loud in front of thirty people, and he has not asked for two pounds.** Jory Vance has had him in the shop twice in a fortnight buying lamp oil with the book open in front of him. And the reason she tells a clerk of twenty-two is the shape of the whole coast: **It is a very heavy thing to ask a person to come and stand at a counter and say yes to something out loud. I do not want it and I have never wanted it and I was not going to get it.** And then what the clerk will do with it two hundred miles off: he will write a name in, and then a man on this coast is holding a wage he never agreed to and there is no way back out of it, **because the way out of a wage is a decision and a decision needs the man in the room.** Jory Vance writes the line out on the edge of the returns board in his own hand because he wanted it, and there is nothing at the end of it. The System's second and last panel in the batch says the quarter carries a line for a person at two pounds a year, that the line has no name and nobody has asked, and that the crossing takes ninety pounds eighteen, **which is what it took this quarter, and that it took a hundred and six four years ago, and that the last man on that line was paid for eleven months** — and ends on whether a line with no name on it is a wage that has been refused by everybody on this coast or a post nobody has applied for. Then the other half of the quarter, and the mechanism, and it is right and it is four hundred years old and nobody on that coast could have told him why and two of them could have told him exactly. **No person on this coast answers for another person about a crossing.** Not the guild, not the assembly, not the harbourmaster, not a clerk; if a thing on a road is a person, that person says what it is, in their own words, standing at a counter, and not one word of it is anybody else's. The bar road has gone into that quarter once a quarter for eight years as a slate of carts out of a basket, and the office writes the number down and does not check it, **because a number is not a person and nobody had to be a person about it.** A blank is no good either, because a blank is a road with nobody on it and a clerk two hundred miles off will write *abandoned* on a road that four carts go over every week of the year. **It has to go to her. There is no second door and there is no one to send.** The sheet is tied round with a loop for a hand, **because a sheet that carries a bar road is a person and a person does not go in a cart with a load of oil, and nobody gives it to anybody**: a man with a bad leg had been in that room since the second bell with the door open and had the whole of it in his ears, the blank and the road and the woman on it and the day and the bell, said out loud twice, **and he read nothing and understood all of it, and when the clerk set the sheet down on the end of the counter for a carter who was going up for his own reasons he picked it up off the counter and put it inside his coat. He has been on this coast thirty-eight days and not one person has asked him for one thing, and there was nobody in that building to ask him whether he would like to be the one standing there. Nobody sent for him. Nobody had standing to send for him, and the fourth time he was produced on this coast it was because he picked a piece of paper up off a counter.** Then the hut, four miles above the north end. Alma Gannock is eighty-one, she knits, and there is a boy of eight in the hut because his mother is dead and his father is on the water, and nothing whatever happens to him. She sees him coming and says **you have not brought anybody**, and puts one thing on the table before he says his: whatever it is, it is not about her and it is not about that card and it is not about her husband, and if it is she would rather he had not come up the road. He says it in about four minutes and it is the shortest he has ever said anything to anybody: the guild is making its quarter, a return goes up it for that bar road, nobody is permitted to say out loud what her road is but her, and so she is asked to come to the counter at the Reach on the third of next tide at the first bell and say, in her own words, what that road is and who keeps it, and it will be in the return in her own words, and there is no form and no seal and nothing to sign. It will cost her the best part of a day, four miles each way and eight in the day, and she is eighty-one and there is a boy of eight. Then the exchange that is the chapter: she says there is always a rest of it, and he is going to want something while he is here because he will be gone by dark, **and it is about the card.** He does not say it. **That is true, and it is not the reason, and you know it is not the reason, and I know you know, and there is no point in either of us standing here saying the other one.** And then the true thing, which is the thing he did not know and could not have got out of her and did not try: **I am not going to the Reach, and I am not going to say why, and I have had about eleven minutes to think of a reason that would sound well to a man off a road and I have not found one, and I have decided that I am not going to invent one for you. You may write down *she would not say why* and that is true, and it is the only true thing about it.** And then the kind thing, and it is the hardest line in the batch: **I am not going to say it is not a sulk and it is not about the guild and it is not about the money and there is no money in it. And you are going to go back down that road in the dark and you are going to write it down, and I would like it written down that I did not want to be told anything and that you did not tell me anything, because in about a year somebody is going to come up this road wanting to know, and I am not going to have told them, and I want there to be a page somewhere that says I had the chance and said no.** **It is not fair. It is only written down. Come up again in the turn and bring nothing.** He is back at the head of the lane at about the ninth bell, in the dark, on a leg that went at the top of the rise and came back worse, and he writes four lines on a torn-out page — the date, that it was his, the ask, the no, the not saying why, and that he did not tell her about the card and had had one question with her on the second of the third tide and had spent it. **And there was no reason on the page, and there had been no reason on the page for four pages now, and he had never once been able to work out whether that was a discipline or a habit, and it was a habit.** And then he says the other half out loud to a shed, to nobody, and it is not a thing he would have written down: **I was right. The thing I was doing this morning was right, and it is four hundred years old, and it cost her a day and it cost me a day, and she has not got a return and the quarter is going up the road with a blank on it that says a road is not kept. So it is right, and it produced me, and it did not produce her, and I have done that four times now in four hundred miles and I have never once asked anybody whether they would like to be the one who is standing there.**

## Chapter 0130 — The One Standing There

**The third of the seventh tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and this is the batch close: a partial, real, paid-for change, a burden named and visible, and an ending that is a decision and not a verdict.** The guild's works are still on the far side with nine yards of wall opened to the second course, a course of stone and lime in the first six yards of it and nothing in the last three, because the stone for the last three yards came round the coast the long way and is stacked on the near side of that water at the head of the chain, and a wall does not wait for a cart. What came off the water is the gear and the string and the two shorter posts, back into their sockets, and the flat is a crossing again by the second bell instead of a working flat, **which is what a bar road costs a coast in days, and nobody on the coast adds it up.** And the small physical test, and not a speech: there is a cart standing at a bar of shingle at the near end that came up the road in the night with a load of stone; **there are four carts waiting on that flat and the man with the bar will take three of them over on this tide and not four, and that is the number that is in his head and in nobody's book; Hal Bevan keeps a slate for it, a stroke for the first, a stroke for the second, and a mark on the fourth for the tide, and it is not in anybody's book and it is not a rule and it is the only thing on that coast that decides who gets the tide when there are more carts waiting than one tide will take.** The third of the seventh tide is a good day and **the flat takes six crossings that day, so nothing in this scene may be counted against six** — the constraint is carts on one tide, which is a man's number and not the custom's. The cart has no mark on it. **You are not on the list for this tide.** I have been on this road nineteen years. Then you have. **Then put me on it. I cannot put you on it, that is not mine to do, and there are four carts on it and he will take three. You have no standing on this road this morning, that is all that is. There is a man on the chain and he has the slate and he will come round in about an hour and the tide will be going off in about two, and if he says you go then you go and the road is not shut and you have lost an hour and a bit and not a day.** The road is not shut. And the ninth-bridge term is on that shingle in an ordinary mouth, used as though it obviously applied, and four feet off stands a man who has spent a month learning what standing is, and every crossing on that water comes to a person, and the person has no standing either, and **the word goes round this coast about nine times a year in the ordinary way and every single one of the nine is worth a day to somebody.** He says nothing — not to the carter, not to Jory Vance, not to the man with the bar — **because he has no standing on this coast and has never had one, and the only two things he has ever been able to give a person on this coast are an argument and a piece of paper**, and the argument was refused in a yard and the page he wrote in a net loft stopped a cart three days later and cost that carter a day. The cart goes on the second tide and the load is up the coast road a day later. Then the account, told once, in four sentences, and not taken back. **I am not going to take it back. The three of them are all right about the water they were in. That is the whole of it and it is why nobody can find the join.** Then: **Then I would not have held the tide. You would have. And you were right to, and if you had gone over the flat on the sixth with a loaded cart on that paper then something bad would have happened to a carter and it would have been on that paper, and I would have made it, and there is nobody on this coast I could have taken it off.** And Bevan, who has been on that chain nineteen years and whose only reason for ever holding a cart is that he is the man on the chain: **the only thing that has ever held a cart off that flat is me, and before me it was a man called Tern, and there is no rule anywhere that says the man on the chain holds the tide. There is a slate in my pocket. And the next one of these that comes up this coast, I am going to bring it to you before I use it, and you will tell me whether it is one water or three, and if you tell me it is three I will not use it, and that is the whole of what I have got and I am aware it is about nine words and a man off a road.** **That is more than the guild has.** And the change that is partial, real, and paid for by somebody else: one cart and one tide and about nine words. Then the man who lost a day's hire, ordinary, not unhappy, nobody on the coast has ever worked out why: **I am going to do a thing on the fourth, and I am telling you because you were straight with me on the sixth and I do not care whose road you are on. I am going to go up and stand on that stone at the turn and ask her the one question.** She gives a straight answer, and the straight answer is about the water, and that is all the stone is, and he says so before he is asked. **You already know what the answer is going to be. It is going to be about the water.** And then the want, which is the cheapest and the most ordinary want on that coast: **I have got a cart coming up on the fourth and I want to know what the water is going to do, and that is the only question I have ever wanted answered by anybody on this coast, and I am thirty-three years old and I have never once asked anybody anything out loud and I am going to do it on the fourth because a man in a lamp oil shed told me the truth about a stone when he had no reason to.** And what it buys: **if she tells me the road is not passable then I will turn round at the top and come back, and that is a day and a half and a day and a half's hire, and I will have spent a day and a half of my own to be told by the one person on this coast who is allowed to say it, in the open, in front of everybody, instead of finding out from a wheel that has gone in up to the nave on a shingle flat. And if she tells me it will go then I will go, and if it will not then I will not, and either way I will have asked. There is not one other door on this coast and I have looked for two days.** Then Thornglass, and the shape of the thing Volume 02 was about, in a shed, at a bench, said in the ordinary way to nobody in particular, and it is the record and not the instrument: **A record is a thing a person has to stand behind. That pane on the wall of the tide office is not the charter because the guild paid for it. It is the charter because a woman called Mare stood beside an arch on one morning nineteen years and eight months ago and stood behind it, and the guild has been borrowing her standing for nineteen years and does not know that it is borrowing it. And that post is not a count because a man of seventy-one cut it. It is a count for one day a year while whoever is here is the one who is here, and the moment that man puts the hammer down, it is wood. And that stone is not a court. It is a woman with the answer, standing on a rock, in the open. The whole of it is her. Take her off it and it is four feet of flat stone and about a hundred years of people respecting a habit. And a pane is the man at the fire. Not the guild and not the customer and not the frame and not the arch. The man at the fire, that morning, and the next four hundred people to stand at that fire. And nobody on this coast has ever once asked the man at the fire whether he would like to be the one standing there, and that is not a small thing, and I have been the man at the fire for forty-one years and nobody has ever asked me either.** And when Ilyan says that a man who has been doing it for forty-one years is not a person anybody thinks to ask: **That is not an excuse. It is a description.** Then the fire, and the last thing in the batch. **I want whoever stands at that fire to say out loud what went into it.** Not on a day off and not when somebody comes for oil — say it once, before the batch goes in, with the two of us standing here and the fire open, so that the next person who stands here knows it in their own ears and not off a bench, because there is nobody on this coast who knows it except them and a man in an office two hundred miles up this coast who learned it out of a book about a year before it was his problem, and she has been standing at this fire for three years and is twenty-four and is paid nothing and has not said out loud one single time what it is that she is doing here at two in the morning. You are asking me to say it in this shed to you, and I am sixty-one and I have said that sentence about four hundred times to men who came for oil, and I am not going to do it for the two of us on a day like this, and there is no version of me that stands at a bench and says it like a man reading out a list, and if I did it that way it would be worth nothing to the person who heard it, and the person who heard it would be you, and you are the only person on this coast who already knows.** **That is not a no, and I am not going to turn it into one at this fire with you standing in it. Bring me a day and a person and I will say it. Until then it is on a bench and not in anybody's mouth, and you are twenty-four and it is not going to be you for another three years if you are here at all, and I have not asked you about that either and you have not asked me.** **All right,** says Sabra Trew, and turns round and picks the bar up and goes back to the second fire, and she is still there, and she will be there at the second bell on the fourth and the fifth and the sixth, and the arch behind two feet of brick is cold and the order is under the batch book with a date on it and no figure against it, and there is a pane on a wall a mile up the hill that cannot be touched for a year, and a post at a crossing with a board on it and nobody's name at the end of the rule, and a man of thirty-one on a crate by a warm wall with a leg that had gone to the hip three times since the fourth of the sixth tide, once in a boat on a bar day, once all the way from the hip down on nine hundred yards of shingle, and once at the top of a rise four miles up a coast road, and had been his own for four hours once, on the fourth of the third tide, and had not been his own since, and he had been on that coast thirty-nine days and not one person on it had asked him for one thing. And on the fourth of that tide, at the turn, a carter of thirty-three who has lost a day's hire and a day's and a half's hire over a piece of paper he has never read is going to stand on four feet of flat stone at the head of a shingle flat and ask the one question that stone is for, and the woman standing on it has the answer, and she will give it to him in the open in front of everybody, and it will be about the water.

## Chapter 0131 — The One Question

**The fourth of the seventh tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, and the appeal is used and it works.** The sea has been standing up behind a westerly since the middle of the night; by the fourth bell there is a channel in the near end with a cart's width of dry ground either side and about a foot of running water in it. **There are more carts on a bar day, not fewer: every cart on this coast that has to come round comes over nine hundred yards of shingle instead of going up above the harbour by a road a loaded cart does not care for.** Seven carts on the flat and a cart at the head of it with stone that has been on the wrong side of the water since the sixth of a tide five days ago. Two men on the line, because that is what a fourth is for; the guild's own works are finishing the last of nine yards of sea wall at the far end; the tally post is there with its chain and its board and the fourpence of nails and nobody put a hand on it. **At the turn Wend Garrow, thirty-three, carter since he was nine, goes up to the stone, which is four feet of flat stone at the head of the flat and has nothing on it and no name near it, and asks one question: what is the water going to do.** She is already on it. **Bettany Rook answers out loud, in front of about seventy people, without hedging and without asking him for anything, and the answer is specific, useful and tradeable: take the cart at the near stones and not in the channel; the ebb that will carry you comes off the far side; you will know it when the water at the far stones goes brown at about the quarter past the turn; you want the middle of it and not the start of it, because at the start of it the sand is coming up and the channel is still full of the last tide, and a cart that gets on the start of an ebb on this flat is a cart that stops; you will have the best part of two hours of it; you will be over in about three quarters of an hour if your beasts are used to a flat and about an hour if they are not; it is true tomorrow and the day after; on the sixth the water is back over the crown of the bar; the fourth of next tide is a longer one because the wind will have had three days.** Nobody asked her a second question. There was not time and there was not anybody who would have been allowed to, and the custom is about a hundred years old and is written down nowhere, and the guild has never interfered with it and never will, and **about nine people on this coast have worked out in the last year that the song and this are two customs and not one.** Corrie Tallow, forty-eight, at the far stones with her slate down, says one sentence at half her usual volume: *he is going to go, I would give a year of the stall to have heard him ask that.* Then **the man says thank you out loud, on the shingle, in front of seventy people, in the ordinary voice, and it takes about a second and a half** — and there is nowhere on this coast to put what has just happened, not as a decision but as a fact: no form, no second door, nobody to take it to, and a man who was done a wrong by a piece of paper cannot put it anywhere at all. **The answer is not written down by anybody afterwards as though it were a record.** Ilyan, forty days on the coast, has four pages in a penny exercise book with a date on them and that they are his and no reason on any of the four, and **there is no fifth page that would hold a thing that is not his, that is not a record, and that is about the water, and the water is the one thing on four hundred miles of this coast that has never once been written down because it does not stay put long enough.** Then **the leg goes at the turn, at the moment the man says thank you, from the hip down, all of it, in about four seconds, and he stays where he is on nine hundred yards of wet shingle because moving is worse, and about seventy people see it and not one of them says a word — the fourth time since the fourth of the sixth tide.** At the fifth bell the water at the far stones goes brown, the cart goes out on the ebb with a man on each side of the boards, and the chain comes up behind it and the man on the chain says nothing to it at all. The load is at the wall at the north end by the ninth bell, a day and a half later than the man who hired it had reckoned, and nobody on this coast adds that up either.

## Chapter 0132 — What a Man Has Left

**The fifth of the seventh tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and a man decides what to spend the only appeal he has on.** The sea came down off the bar in the night and the ebb runs long and soft. **Hal Bevan, fifty-four, the guild's own man on that flat for nineteen years, has one question still unspent and it expires at the turn on the sixth, and he has never told anybody he has one and there is nobody on four hundred miles he could have told.** In his breast pocket, folded in four, is the dealer's copy of the merged account, **in that pocket for nine days** — since the fourth bell of the third of the sixth tide, when Cleve Gannock read it out twice in a lodging-house kitchen and wrote it out again. **Six days ago it stopped a cart:** the sixth of the sixth tide, at the third bell, a load of dressed stone stood on the near side for a day and a day's hire and two men on it and went up the coast road the long way instead, and the reason given was that paper against a table on a wall a mile up the hill. He has not taken the paper out since the second day; he can read it and reading it a third time does not do anything. He prices the three possible questions while working, and nobody asks him what he is doing: **ask her about the water and he learns nothing, because he knows that channel to about a yard and has since he was nineteen — a yard is not a figure and cannot be put down; ask her whether the guild's table is right and it is not a question about the water, it is a question about a thing somebody made in a shed and paid for, and he would get one true sentence in about four seconds and there is no way on this coast to get it back; ask nothing and the only reason a cart has ever been held off that flat remains a sheet a stranger wrote in a net loft and he will be the man who held it and will not know.** **The thing he would spend it on is not a question about the water, and he knows it, and there is no other door.** Dell Gannock, forty-two, comes off the water about the fourth bell, says the day is good and you can go over it loaded today, and is right, and cannot tell anybody what a man cannot get by looking. Cleve comes down the lane to ask whether his cart will be off by the seventh, which is a dealer being right, and goes back up. A carter with a load of deals says the true thing unasked: *I would not spend it on that… there is only ever one of them in a life, and I am forty and I have not spent mine.* At the far stones, to the man with the bad leg and not to Bevan, **Corrie Tallow says she has asked on that stone once, and that she has wanted to ask where the ninth crossing of a bad sixth goes for about eleven years and is not going to, because a ninth crossing is a number and the stone does not do numbers — and the day there is a paper with a number on it, a man with a cart says *the paper says* and goes over it, and the nine that is wrong will be somebody in nine feet of water and it will be her fault because of the paper. And she has watched four men spend a thing like that and get a straight answer and not be able to use it, and every one of them did it for exactly his reason.** Then **Bevan keeps his nine-word promise**: he comes down the shingle at the sixth bell, stops four feet off, and tells him exactly what he is going to ask tomorrow, *because if you hear it from me first then at least one man on this coast knows exactly what I spent it on.* The question is **is there anything in that paper that is wrong about the water** — *it is a question about a piece of paper about the water* — and Ilyan cannot tell him it is the wrong thing, cannot tell him it is the right thing, and says so, and Bevan says *I know you are not, you have not got the standing and neither have I*, and picks the bar up. At the seventh bell he takes out the slate he has carried for nineteen years, a stroke for the first cart, a stroke for the second, a mark on the fourth for the tide, looks at it, and puts it back. **It is a slate, and it is the only thing he has that anybody on this coast will act on, and it has nothing whatever to say to him about a piece of paper in a dealer's hand.**

## Chapter 0133 — Spent

**The sixth of the seventh tide, a bar day, the turn, and the question is spent and answered truly and about the water, and the answer is useless to the man who spent it.** Seven carts on the flat; the sea over the crown since before the light; about seventy people, because everybody knows where everybody stands on a sixth. Bevan does his nine crossings first, because a man does his work, and at the second crossing the driver stops dead in the middle of nine hundred yards of wet shingle and looks back at the head of the flat for about four seconds, and nobody notices and it costs him a yard and a half of ground and four minutes. At the turn he holds the paper up so the people near the stone can see there is a thing in it, and asks: **is there anything in that paper that is wrong about the water.** She looks at him for about two seconds, says *that is a question about the water*, and tells him that he gets one and has got one and nobody may ask a second and she is not going to make him walk back down nine hundred yards of wet shingle with nothing because he asked it in the wrong words. **And then she answers it, out loud, in front of about seventy people, and it takes about a minute and a half: there is nothing in that paper that is wrong about the water. The water at the north end on a good day goes out to about four inches at the lowest of the ebb and about six on the bar, and the bar is a bar and not the channel, and the channel on a good day lies across that shallow and not along it; on the fourth, the sixth and the seventh the sea stands over the crown of that bar and there is no lowest at the north end at all, and the crossing four hundred yards south of him is the road on those three days and this flat is not.** And then the one thing she will not do, said plainly and not unkindly: **she will not stand on a stone and say whether the table on a wall a mile up the hill is right about that morning or whether the paper is, because she has never had those two things in front of her at once, and if she tried it she would be guessing about the water, and she does not guess about the water.** Somebody at the back says the word *four* and somebody else says the word *feet*, and about nine people on that shingle have a number in their heads that has just been contradicted out loud by the only person on this coast who is allowed to say a number about the water, and about sixty understood the shape of it and not the size. **Nobody asks her a second question. Nobody refused anybody. The paper was right about the water, and that was the whole of what was wrong with his week.** Then, in the ordinary way and not unkindly: **you did not spend it on the water… that has cost you the only thing you have ever had on this coast and it has bought you a thing you could have got by standing on your own flat with your own eyes on it for a day… that is the whole of what this stone is. It is for the water. There is no second door. There is one stone and two days a week and me on it, and you had it.** He goes down to the chain with the slate in his hand for the rest of the day and says nothing to anybody, and about nine people on that flat would have given a great deal to have heard it. The boy of eleven goes over for the ninth time at a penny; Corrie Tallow is at the far stones with her slate for the ninth crossing, unpaid and always there on the ninth; the post and the board are there and the nails hold and nobody puts a hand on it. **Seth Garrick comes out of the oil shed when the ninth crossing is done, looks at the flat, says one word out loud to nobody in particular — "Well." — and shuts the door, and that is all he ever says about it to anybody for the rest of his life.** Ilyan heard all of it, and could have said that this stone is not the way to put two records side by side and never was going to be, **and did not, because he has no standing on this coast and the only two things he can give a person here are an argument and a piece of paper, and the argument was refused in a yard in the fifth of a tide and the piece of paper was in the man on the chain's breast pocket and had cost a carter a day. He does not put it down anywhere; there is no place on this coast for a fifth page, and he has known that since about the third of a tide and has not found one.** At the ninth bell the water goes off the flat, the chain goes into its sockets, and **the man on the chain took the slate out and put a stroke in it for the last of the nine and went home. He kept the paper and never took it out again.**

## Chapter 0134 — The Two Days a Week

**The seventh of the seventh tide, a bar day, and the day there is no appeal at all, and the chapter is the day: water, weather, the ordinary work of a seventh.** The strait is shut, the water has been on the crown of the bar since before the light and is not coming off it, and every cart that has to come round this coast is on nine hundred yards of shingle. **Gell Roke, seventy-one, is on the staff at the harbour mouth with a pot of paint and his hammer in a bag and a boy of about ten holding the pot for him**, because the turn on a seventh is the only moment in the week a man can put a mark on the bar staff at two feet under; he has a second coat on the mark from about four years ago and it has gone thin at the two-foot line. *Hold it up. Not down. There is no arm in a man that holds a pot down.* **The mark under the water is the same width as the mark above it and has been for nineteen years and it is a long way from where the thing it marks now is, and about two hundred people on this coast have worked that out and not one of them ever said it, and this morning nobody said it either because it is a bar day and there were seventy people on the flat and every one of them had a load.** The chain goes up and comes down nine times and rests an hour between the fifth and the seventh, and the seventh is the day it rests and the eighth is the day the man with the bar has to hurry, and that is how a seventh is made. Corrie Tallow counts forty-one crossings, about half of them people on foot; Nim Hask, eleven, goes over for the ninth time at a penny with a hand-lead in his fist and nobody thinks anything about it, including him. **The tally post is there with its chain and its board with two numbers and a difference and a line under them, and it is inspected at about the turn on a seventh once a year by a man with a hammer, and it had been inspected in the turn of the year and will not be inspected again until the turn of the year after, and nobody had any business at that post on a seventh morning and nobody had.** **Danno Ray, forty-five, with a load of lime and two men on, crosses on the seventh and stands at the chain: there is a stone you can ask one question on, and a song at the stone this afternoon, and a number cut on a post that a man counts with a hammer once a year, and there is a board on the front of it. And I have worked out that a rule that cannot be changed for a year is not a rule, it is a season. Nobody answered him** — the boy with the board, two carters, a man in his fourth crossing — **because about nine people heard the same sentence from the same man standing in the same place in the fifth of a tide and nothing has happened on this coast since. It is not the rule I am complaining about. You have had that crossing a hundred years. I am complaining about the season.** He goes to the hard. **At about the turn Seth Garrick sang at the stone — four lines, a tariff, sung every seventh at the turn by one man, not written down, not on any board, four or five people in on the chorus, which they do. The stone was bare behind him. Nobody stood on it that day and nobody could have, and there is nobody on this coast who has ever explained that to the guild or to anybody else, and the guild has never asked.** The person it lands on is **the man on the chain**, who spent his question on the sixth, got a true answer out loud in the open in front of seventy people, and **had known for six days that it would be about the water and spent it anyway** — and what he has instead is the fact of it, that a man who has held a chain for nineteen years without once not knowing where the going was has been told by the only person on four hundred miles allowed to say it that he was right about the water, **which is not nothing, and which he thinks about about twice a day for the rest of that year and never says out loud, and about four people work out that he spent the stone's question and never said so.** Then, to a carter who asks when the next one is, in the ordinary way: **the first of the eighth, and the third, and the fifth, and the seventh, and then the first of the ninth, and it goes like that and there is no more to it. The sixth is there and the fourth is there and the first, the second, the third and the fifth are not. There are two days a week when the stone is there and a woman on it, and about four hundred thousand days a week when it is not, and I have never once heard anybody on this coast work that out in a sentence until just now.** Nobody is going to be able to use that. *It has got a stone in it and no more.* Ilyan's finding, on a head of flat in the dark: **the whole working life of every person within four hundred miles of that shingle is arranged around two days a week and a question about the water, and not one of them has ever been asked for a single thing in return for it, and that is the only arrangement on this coast with no form in it and no counterparty and nobody to complain to and nobody who can be got at.** Gell Roke comes past with the hammer in the bag and the boy with the empty pot and neither of them says a word, and neither of them ever did.

## Chapter 0135 — Two Hundred and Sixty Yards of Stone

**The first of the eighth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and the defence the guild built is on the page, and the guild is right about it.** At the north end, four hundred yards up the lane from the crossing, the ebb out of that deep runs long and soft and two hundred yards of gravel stands out of it in the low like a floor. **Halse Tarrant, fifty-eight, has been on that wall for thirty-one years and was one of the hands that laid the first of it.** The wall: two hundred and sixty yards, laid in courses, nine courses at the inner end falling to about two at the outer, and the outer end standing out into the deep on purpose. **What it is for is one thing and it has been one thing since the year it went down: the deep is nine feet on a good day and it is a hole the ebb has cut in a gravel bar, the wall stands along the seaward side of that hole, and where the ebb and the outside are close the water makes a hole in the gravel. In the year before they laid it, it had gone back eleven yards in two winters and taken a hut and a boat slip with it, and the slip had belonged to a man called Teel and there is nobody on this coast who was ever taught by him.** Eight men, one season, about seven weeks, **the last course nineteen years and eight months ago, in the same year the guild had a thing fired in a shed at Thornglass for the room upstairs of the office at the Reach — two things paid for out of the same season, about two miles apart, and there is not one person on this coast who has ever put them in the same sentence.** **Four things he says, in the open, on a good day.** First: **the guild was right.** He has watched that ebb every day since he was twenty-seven; take the wall out and there is no north end inside three winters; nobody was wrong and it has not been looked at since. Second, the thing he has not got: **a wall of stone does not stop water, it stops gravel; the wall is worth exactly what is behind it, and what is behind it is about two feet of gravel, and those two feet are the whole of that point, and if they go the wall is a wall standing in the sea about eleven yards from where anything is. And the toe of the seaward face is the part that goes, and he has not been at it since about the fourth year — on a good day it is under nine feet of green water, and on a fourth and a sixth and a seventh the ebb brings the sand out of the channel and packs the middle of the bar flat and the deep fills and a flat boat can be stood over the top of it, and on those three days the sea outside is standing on the crown and the wind is on the quarter and a man out there has about an hour and a quarter and then is sitting in nine feet on the flat, because it holds about eleven times in twenty and the nine is somebody's cart in nine feet of water and it would be his fault for being there.** **He has the four things short of one: a lead line, a boat, a man who can sound, and a day.** The lead line is the guild's, in a box in a shed since the wall was laid; the boat is his own and has not been out in about a year; he can sound to a foot inside of forty yards. **He has wanted the day since about the sixth year and has not asked anybody, because a wall does not ask for a boat.** Third, the thing nobody is going to like: **the water has moved.** Not a fright, not a discovery, not anybody's fault. The line where the ebb works along the outside of that wall is outboard of the outer end now, and it has been going outboard for a good many years, and he can see where it is working and has no number for where it worked then — the number anybody has is on a wall a mile up the hill, a morning about three months old, a photograph of a morning taken by a man standing beside a fire, the only instrument anybody will act on, and it does not come out of that building; the other one is on a wall two miles along this coast and is three years old and is a different morning, and the man who made it cannot get at the first one and cannot get the first one to him and has not been sure his own is right since he made it. Fourth: **he has been in charge of it since the fourth year and nobody has ever asked him whether he would like to be, and he is not complaining, and he would not have said yes the first twenty times, and he has not thought about it in six years, which is the same as not caring except that the wall and the man are two different things and he has only ever been sure about one of them.** And then, to a stranger who asks *who do you ask when you want a day*: **nobody… the last man who asked me for something was a man in a good coat who asked for a year, and the answer to that was a woman's back for eleven months, and he did not ask her, and he was right not to… a person who wants a thing off a person who has not got it to spare does not ask the person. The person finds out afterwards or does not, and either way the person is standing there with it and nobody has said yes to anything. And I have watched that happen four or five times on this coast since you came and I have not liked any of it and I have not been able to do one single thing about any of it, and I am fifty-eight and I am the man who keeps two hundred and sixty yards of stone and there is not one part of that I am allowed to spend on anybody.** The ordinary business goes on round it the whole time — six crossings on the flat, a boat standing along beyond the point, a man not sorting his bait, a carpenter planing a hull on his ways — **and nothing on this coast is alarmed and nothing is threatened and the ebb goes out long and soft and does exactly what it has done every good day for thirty-one years.** Ilyan is forty-four days on the coast, puts the lead down about four seconds after picking it up, hears the whole of it, and says one stupid thing and is told, at length and in the trade's mouth, why nobody on this coast asks anybody for anything. He has the four things in his head already — a glassmaker named them at a bench ten days ago and would not buy them with a woman's back — **and there is no way on four hundred miles of this coast to make a man hold them, and he has known that for forty-four days.**

## Chapter 0136 — The Third Column and the Eighth

**The second of the eighth tide, a good day, and the two columns that are places and not depths are read aloud in a shed.** The second fire is let in at the second bell and Sabra Trew stands at it because it cannot be left once it is let in; the arch is cold behind two feet of brick; **the guild's order is on the bench under the batch book with a date against it and no figure against it, and it was under the batch book since a cart came up the coast road with a load of oil and it is going to be under the batch book at the turn.** **Hollis Thrale, sixty-one, forty-one years at the fire, deaf in the right ear as his father was and his father's father was**, has his own pane on the wall of that shed, three years old, eleven places for every tide. **The two columns are the third and the eighth, the two that record a place and not a depth. Sabra Trew is the only other person who can read them and she reads them out.** Third: the outer end of the north wall, a place and not a figure — **two hundred and sixty-one yards from the post at the inner end, along the line of the wall**, and then the height at the top of the inner end, which is the ninth course, which is the six-foot one. Eighth: the toe of the seaward face — **the gravel stands against the seaward face to the second course for the whole length of the wall, and at the inner end it stands to the fifth.** The exception is at the inner end and not the outer, because the wall is nine courses there and about two at the outer, and it is at the inner end in the prose because a repair pass had put it at the outer end, where nothing can stand to a fifth course on a two-course wall. **Nine of the eleven are depths, taken from a boat at the lowest of the ebb with a line, and they are for a morning; the two that are not depths are the two that are about a wall, and a wall is not a depth, and whoever made these two columns knew it.** Hollis has the guild's two figures **in his head in a clerk's hand and has had them for two years**, and says how he got them: **the harbourmaster read him two columns off her own wall in a yard on the fourth of a tide and would do nothing else, and she was within her rights, and she was right to refuse him the other nine, and he did not go back.** The guild's third says two hundred and sixty-four. **The guild's eighth says the toe stands to the fourth course for the whole length and does not give the inner end at all.** So: **the third column is three yards in nineteen years and eight months on a wall laid in seven weeks by eight men, and that is not three yards of nothing — that is the water moving along that wall, and the outer end is three yards further in than it was on the guild's morning, so something has taken the gravel off the outer end of it, and he cannot say how much or when, because a number about a wall is not a number about water and you have to be in a boat to get one of those.** And the eighth is the whole of the trouble: **mine says two courses for the whole of it, theirs says four; a course is about the thickness of a hand, so four is two feet eight and two is a foot and four; if theirs is right the wall has had two feet eight of gravel standing against its face for nineteen years and is doing its job; if mine is right the toe has come down by a foot and four along two hundred and sixty yards and there is no bottom under that bottom course in places and the ebb is working round the foot of a wall instead of past the end of it. And he cannot tell you which is true, and the only way there has ever been to try is to put the two panes on one bench, and the only instrument anybody will act on is theirs, and it is on a wall a mile up the hill in a frame and does not come out of that building and the next one is a year off and there is no money in the arch.** Then **a third pane**, and Sabra Trew puts the bar down in the middle of the fire, which she has done once in three years, and refuses him again: **a third one is a third morning. It settles nothing. It is harder to argue with and no more true, and the water will have moved three times instead of twice, and a year of somebody's back is the price of it, and the price is me, and the price was me the first two times as well, and one of those two is on a wall a mile up that hill and one of them is on this one and they do not agree in the eighth column, and there are two photographs on this coast and no room to put them side by side in.** She refuses to say it a second time in this chapter. And she gives him the other half, in her own hand's knowledge: **the eighth column is worth everything and worth nothing at the same time, because it is a record of a morning… and the only thing on this coast that will tell you how much gravel is standing against that wall this morning is a man in a boat with a line on the day the sea is doing whatever it is going to do** — and then the thing nobody has said out loud: **he has said that to me and to a man off a road who came up this road twice in three days and stood at that bench, and he has never once said it to the man who keeps the wall, because he is two miles along this coast and has never been there, and neither has the man been here, and the two of them have been four feet from the same argument for two years and neither of them knew it.** Ilyan has the true answer and does not say it, because he has been wrong about the same shape of thing three times on this coast and every one of the three times he put it in a building. He says the stupid thing instead — that there is a man up the coast with a lead line in a box and a boat and the ability to sound who needs a day — and is told that Hollis Thrale has known that since about the fifth of a tide, and did not believe a word of it and then did, and has not been up there and has not sent anybody and is not going to: **because the last time anybody on this coast got a person to stand on a thing for a year they did it without asking her, and I am not going to be the man who starts a second one in a season where the passage money is short; and because he will not say yes.** She goes back to the fire. **The order stays under the batch book with a date and no figure. The arch stays cold. And nobody on this coast is going to be asked which of the two columns is right.**

## Chapter 0137 — Would You Like to Be the One Standing There

**The third of the eighth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and the volume's relationship milestone is delivered: the question Chapter 130 set up for the man at the fire is asked of the woman who stands on the stone, and she says no, and the truth is not the answer he wanted, and he is wrong out loud and she is right out loud and the coast does not treat it as an event.** **The stone is bare, because the stone is bare five days in seven and this was one of the five** — and he asks her anyway, in daylight, with people about, because doing it in an office would be a conversation and doing it on a bare-stone day would be a complaint, and there was one in a week of them when she came down a hill in daylight past about forty people. **Bettany Rook comes down at about the fourth bell with a board under her arm and a hood up to see the near end of a flat that takes six, and she knows what he is before he says it, and says *no* before he says it, and then lets him ask it properly, because he has told her twice in a month she is not going to invent a reason for him.** He asks: **would you like to be the one standing on it.** And she says **no**, and answers it in the open at four in the afternoon, in three parts. **The first half is the one he expected and it does not decide anything:** a stone is worth what the person on it is worth, take her off it and it is four feet of flat stone and about a hundred years of people respecting a habit, the guild has never interfered with it in a hundred years and never will, and that is the whole of what it is worth — and *you are a man who has spent a month on this coast learning that the shape of a thing is not the thing, and you have learned it four times and got it wrong three.* **The second half is the one he did not expect: she would not give it up, and that is not a smallness.** In nineteen years in that office nobody in that building has asked her for one thing; she is the harbourmaster and she has never once been asked by anybody to stand in a particular place; **the stone is the only thing on this coast that has ever happened to me, and what happened was that nobody made me do it… the whole of what that gives you is that nobody can ever say you agreed to it.** And if she stands down, in about a month a man on a bar road four miles along this coast finds out what happens to a road with nobody on it, and he will find it out the way a carter finds it out, and it will be a person and not a rule, and she is not going to be the reason. **The third part is the one she wants in his own words: I do not want to be a person doing it. I want it to keep being the coast.** As long as it is the coast it is a hundred years of everybody respecting a habit and nobody has to be brave and nobody has to be asked and no day comes when a woman of fifty-one with one eye has to say to a room that she is going to stop. **The day somebody asks is the day it stops being the coast and starts being a woman on a stone. And then it is one woman. And one woman goes blind in the eye she has got and then she is tired, and then she is a person in a chair in a building a mile and a flight of stairs above a flat, and the stone is bare for about a year while somebody works out what to do, and in that year a man on a bar road finds out. I would rather be a coast than a woman. That is not a noble thing to say. It is the true thing.** And the last thing: **do not write it that I stood on that stone for six years out of duty. Standing in somebody's habit is not duty. Duty is a thing you agree to. I never agreed to it, and if I had agreed to it I would have had to be asked, and nobody asked me.** And then **he is wrong out loud, in front of nine people who can hear it, which is the fourth time in a fortnight he has been wrong in front of strangers and the first time he has said so before he was shown:** he had wanted her to say yes because he had it that the hundred years of habit was the fragile part, **and the hundred years of habit is the strong part; it has outlasted every record anybody on this coast has ever made, including three glass ones, and it went on while a man of sixty-one sat on a bench two miles along this coast being right about photographs, and it is the only durable thing out here and it has never once been in a room.** And he is wrong about the other half too: **you are not standing in a habit. A habit is what people do without anybody asking. You are the person in it, and the habit is the hundred years of everybody else, and I have had those the wrong way round in my mouth since the sixth of a tide, and that is the difference between a woman doing a job and a coast doing a thing with a woman standing in it.** She answers: **that is the first thing anybody has said to me about it… you may have noticed that we have not agreed about a single thing in the last six minutes and we are still both going to be here tomorrow.** *Yes. I did notice.* **They walk round the far stones and say nothing else about it.
