The tide office gets its woman back at the sixth bell and she does four hours' work and gets it out inside an hour and nobody in that room asks her one question. There are two panes on the north wall of that room in oak frames and they have not been out of that building in nineteen years and were not out of it that afternoon, and nobody asked her to bring one down and he did not ask her to bring one down, because he has been wrong about that three times and was not going to try it a fourth time with a woman who had just told him the truth about herself in the open at the near end of a flat.** He is **forty-six days** on the coast, and the leg had been his own for about four hours once, on the fourth of the third tide, at a fire two miles along this coast, and had not been his own since.

## Chapter 0138 — The Driven Mark

**The fourth of the eighth tide, a bar day, mostly water and weather, and the record that is in the world and is useless.** **This chapter's opening and its whole wheel sequence were lifted from Chapter 124 in the first draft — the boat and the ebb and a hand flat on the boards under him, the sound of the wheel going, the man out of the shed, the one word, the instruction to the driver, and the line at four places — and the first repair pass claimed to have rewritten them and did not. All of it is rewritten in the third pass and what is left is three short echoes, all marked in Brann's own mouth. Read `state/batch-summary.md` under *Third pass* before quoting any of it.** The ebb out of that deep is about an hour and a quarter and hard and it is coming out carrying sand and putting the sand into the hole, and for the length of it the hole is not a hole. **Sorrel Gannock, thirty-six, the only one of the three brothers who will take a boat over that shallow on a bar day, eleven years in her father's place and for nothing, takes the flat boat off the hard at the fourth bell with the towrope in the bow and nothing else in her at all, and a man with a bad leg goes in it because there was nowhere else to be**, and the leg goes — from the hip down, all of it, in about four seconds — **and nobody in the boat says anything about it then or afterwards, and she does not look round, and she had not looked round the last two times either.** They cross, and then they go round the point, because that is the way the boat lies when the ebb is still running, and neither of them says that was the plan. **It held: about the eleventh time in twenty that the deep packed and stayed packed for the length of a crossing.** The training wall is on the seaward side of that deep — two hundred and sixty yards, nineteen years and eight months old, nine courses falling to two at the outer end — and it stands up out of that deep on a fourth, and a flat boat can be stood over the top of the whole of it and the courses walked and the seaward face looked at from the ninth course down to a foot and a half of brown water and sand. **Halse Tarrant is on the inner end with a bar, doing the only thing there is to do to a wall that is out of the water, and there is an iron stake in the seaward face about the thickness of a wrist, with a plate on it, gone the colour of a coin.** Ilyan does not lean over it: **say the number out loud. You can see it. I cannot, and I would rather you said it than that I looked at it and guessed.** **Hundred and thirty-nine.** And then the whole of what a driven mark is, in the trade's mouth, in about four minutes: **a chainage — a hundred and thirty-nine yards along something from something, a place and not a depth, and there is no depth anywhere on it. The guild drove those along the whole wall on the last two days of the job, one every five yards, off a chain, with a boy and a book, and the book went up the coast road and the book is in that building a mile up the hill with a lock on it and he has never seen it and was not in the gang that did it because he was on the inner courses putting the big stone in. So I can tell you what kind of number that is and I cannot tell you what it is a number of.** And: **it is a man saying the water was working here this morning and I have put iron in the ground so that the next man knows where here was. And that is worth having, and it is worth having for about three years, because the next man does come and look… and after that it is a piece of iron with a number on it. The water does not come back to the same place because a man was in a boat and put iron in the ground, and if it did then none of us would have to sound anything.** And the whole of it, on the ground: **it is a place and not a depth, and nineteen years old, and about two feet under the ninth course where it stands out of the water on a fourth and a sixth and a seventh, and nine foot under on a good day, and there is not one boat on this coast that will come out here on a good day and there is not one cart in the world that can reach the foot of that face on a bad one. So it cannot be produced — at the moment anybody needs it, which is every single time anybody needs it. And there is a second thing worse than that: a thing that is written down can be produced; a thing cut on a post can be counted, and it took a man of seventy-one two hours and eleven minutes the last time anybody did it; a thing fired in a fire can be brought out of a building in an oak frame if anybody in that building ever says yes, and nobody ever says yes. This is the only kind of record on this coast that cannot be produced at all, because it is not in a room and it is not on a bench and it is not in a cart. It is in the wall.** **Nobody put the two together**, and the reason is the plainest thing on the coast: a thing in a wall cannot be put side by side with a thing in a room, the man in the boat did not know a third of what was in that shed, the man in the shed had not been told there was a man in a boat, **and the number was not even a number that matched one — a hundred and thirty-nine of something is not the same kind of thing as a column on a pane, and there was nobody on the flat to say it ought to be, and a man who cannot say that ought to be cannot go and ask. It settled nothing, it was not evidence of anything, it was not a discovery.** Then **a cart's wheel comes off on the shingle above the flat and seventy people do not look up.** Ostre Brann, fifty-eight, is up the road with iron and has stopped to get a collar cut; he says *sprung* once, and gives the driver the instruction short — *up the hard, and take the disc off, and get that iron off the shingle, and I am not going to say the rest of it, because I said the rest of it on this flat a fortnight ago to a man who is not standing here* — and does the line at four places and puts his thumb in the gap and leaves it there until the carter has put his own thumb in it, **because you cannot argue with a thumb**, and finds the crush in a pair of felloe ends with shingle in it. And he calls the instruction back at the end of it — *twice now, in about a fortnight, both the same way, on that flat, in the worst of the week* — and says the three he always says in the same words, with *I said that to the last man who came in here* in front of them, and then the thing that is his and is **new**: **you can get a number off a wheel. Nobody has ever told anybody that and it is not any use to anybody. Everything in that nave above the axle line took the load and everything below it did not, so the wood above the axle line is a record of how deep the going was and the wood below it is a record of how deep it was not, and if you take a wheel to pieces and put a rule on it you can tell me within about four inches what the going was like on the last crossing, and I have done it, and I have never once told anybody, because it is a fact about the last crossing and about nothing else in the world.** And the rest of his trade's speech: a repair is not a date, a punch is Ness Tarn's and Ness has been dead nine years, a mark says who bought the iron and the iron does not know anything about water — **but the number I can give you is four inches, and it is about the ground, and it is about one crossing, and it is about the axle height of one cart. It is not about the water. Your water is a different thing entirely and it does not touch a wheel.** A woman of thirty-six stands at the mouth of the yard with the towrope on her shoulder the whole time and says nothing about the wall or the mark or the number, and **is not asked to solve anything and does not solve anything.** The water comes back into the deep at the ninth bell, and by the next morning there will be about nine feet of green water over a hundred and thirty-nine of something, in a wall, in the dark, for another six days.

## Chapter 0139 — A Day Given Away

**The fifth of the eighth tide, a good day, the flat takes six, and the batch's cost is priced and paid for by somebody who is not thanked.** **A lead line, a boat, a man who can sound, and a day.** Slee has three of them: the guild's own line in a box in a shed since the wall went down, a boat of his own that has not been out in about a year, and thirty-one years of sounding that bar to a foot inside of forty yards. He does not launch until the sixth bell because a boat in the water for two hours is a boat doing nothing. **Nobody has the money for it and the only thing missing is the day.** And the day is given away in about nine words in a lane four hundred yards from the crossing: **Cleve Gannock, thirty-one, who does not go on the water and deals in what the flat boat brings in, comes down with a pair of beasts yoked and a cart behind them on his way to a landing, and says he has got two hours before the turn, the beasts and the cart are by the day, he has paid for both whatever happens to him, the only thing his day buys is him standing at a landing being useful to a man at the Quay, and the man does not need him until about the ninth bell. And: you are not going to ask me. Right. Then I will tell you what I have got… take the day. And you are not to say anything about it, and if anybody in this yard starts on about it I will tell them it was the beasts that were late.** **Halse Tarrant did not ask him for it, and that is the whole of what happened and it is worth being exact about, because it is the shape of every single thing on this coast: the man who had the day and the man who needed the day were standing in a lane and neither of them asked the other for anything.** Cleve takes the cart to the landing and is two hours late and tells the man at the Quay it was the beasts, **and nobody said thank you — not the man with the bad leg, not the man in the boat, not the three people at the yard — and nobody thanks him then or afterwards.** And **Ilyan's part is what he cannot do:** he cannot refuse it, because he has no standing on this coast and a man who is not from this coast does not get to decide what a Gannock's day is worth to a Gannock, and a thing he is not owed is not a thing he is entitled to hand out; he cannot say the man in the boat should not spend a day somebody else lost on a question nobody asked him about; he cannot say the answer will be a number and that a number is the right instrument. He says none of it, and **being the person standing nearest is not a qualification and has never been a qualification and cost a woman of eighty-one nothing and a clerk of twenty-two nothing and a man of thirty-one the best part of a day four times.** What he does is take out the penny exercise book and write four lines: the date, that Cleve Gannock gave a day to Halse Tarrant, that it was his cart, his pair of beasts and his own day and he was two hours late at a landing because of it and told the man there it was the beasts, and **nobody asked him for it and nobody thanked him for it — it is mine to write down and it is not mine to decide.** The date on it, and that it was his, and no reason on the page, for the fifth page running, and he noticed himself not putting one and did not stop. The sounding goes over the seaward face from the inner end, every nine feet, for an hour and a half, and the result is: **the toe of the seaward face is gone along about eleven yards of it, in the middle, not at the ends, and gone to about two feet of depth. There is no bottom holding gravel under the bottom course of that wall for eleven yards. There is stone in courses and then there is the sea.** And then, in the trade's mouth, and it is worth more than a stake with a number on it and worth nothing at all tonight: **I have got that in a book in a shed with the date on it and the number in it, and I will swear to that book in a room in front of anybody who wants me to, for as long as I am alive, and that is the whole of what it is worth… and nobody on this coast will do anything about a number in a book. There is a table on a wall a mile up that hill that is the only instrument anybody will act on and it does not come out of that building, and there is a pane on a wall in a shed two miles along this coast that is about the same wall and is three years old and disagrees with the first one in two columns out of eleven, and the two columns are the ones that would settle this, and the man who made the second one cannot get at the first one and the first one does not come out of its room. So I have got a number and there are two photographs that do not agree, and one of them is nineteen years and eight months old and the other is three years old, and my number is from today, and today is the best day anybody has had on that wall in about fifteen years, and none of the three of them is going to travel to meet either of the others.** The post comes up the coast road in the afternoon and **Seth Garrick reads it out twice in the lane.** The guild's own four lines are correct and are a refusal: **that a record of a moving thing is a working instrument; that instruments do not travel, and a man who has one is the only man who can use it, and a body two hundred miles from a wall cannot act on a number taken from a boat; that the guild will sound that wall at its own cost and in its own season, and the season's money is not in the arch, and it will not be this season, and the guild does not send a copy of a working instrument to a person who has one; and that the guild is not in dispute that the wall is nineteen years and eight months old and that it is to be walked.** And Slee: **that is four sentences of the truest thing anybody has said to me in a year, and it is a refusal, and every part of it is right, and I want it written down that I have not got one single thing to say against any part of that, and I am not going to argue with a guild about the one thing they know best.** And: **the fourth line — they want to be walked. That is the first sentence anybody in that building has written to this coast in about two years that was not about a sum.** The second letter is short, sealed, from a city four hundred miles behind this one, from **a woman who is a magistrate and who was out-voted in a room and has not been out-voted since and is not going to be.** It asks two questions — what body stood behind the crossing at Wether Cross, and what instrument governed the counting of it — **notes that both have been answered in the return for the last quarter and that both answers are the same answer, and that the same answer has been given for eight years** — and it asks for one thing: that a person ought to be able to be told no by the harbourmaster's office in a place where the refusal is written down and can be shown to somebody else, **and that she does not care in the least what the answer was and only wanted it to be somewhere.** Corrie Tallow reads it once and gives it back: **we have not got that and we are not going to have it… she has asked for it in the only way anybody ever asks for anything that is not money, and she is right, and a body two hundred miles off asking this coast to have a box for refusals in is a body that has not noticed what this coast does with a person who says no to a question she was never asked. It has got a stone. Four feet of it, a hundred years old, and she answers about the water, and nobody may ask a second, and that is the only door anybody on this coast has ever had and it is not a door anybody can write a refusal on. And we are not building one, and I would say so to her office as well as to her and I would not put a name at the end of it.** And the letter goes back up the road and **nobody on this coast was any nicer or kinder about a magistrate than she had been about them, and nothing about it softened.** Then, in the same post, **the guild's quarter, and the line in it for a person to be paid two pounds a year out of what the crossing takes, to count the crossings in persons and not carts, has had no name on it since the fourth of the fourth tide, and the office read it and chose the one of its two options that writes nothing.** And then the finding, in four hundred words and no speech: **the vote of a council of nine sits in a book two hundred miles up this coast for a year and does nothing at all. It will not be reopened. Nobody in that building has asked the office here to fill it in and nobody here has written and asked. In about a month the next quarter will go up in a cart on the coast road and a clerk will read a line with a wage on it and no person on it, and he will leave it, and the next one after that he will leave it too, and by the turn of the year the paper with the vote in it will be in a drawer with last year's… and a man of thirty, who is the only person on this coast that line is for, has a lamp-oil shed at the head of the lane and counted that post in his head for four years and got it wrong by about nine hundred and said so out loud in front of thirty people, and he has not asked for it, and nobody is going to ask him, and nobody in that building two hundred miles off is ever going to know that there was a man on this coast the wage was voted for, because the line is blank and the quarter does not say.** And the last sentence of the chapter: **That is what a blank does. It does not stop a thing and it does not refuse one… and in about a year somebody in that office will go looking for the minute that voted it, and will find that the minute says a person is to be paid, and will not be able to find the person, and will put the minute back. Seth Garrick shut the door of the oil shed behind him and did not say anything, and nobody asked him, and nobody is going to.**

## Chapter 0140 — The Sixth

**The sixth of the eighth tide, a bar day, the flat takes nine, and the tidal breach opens in the last third of the chapter and the batch closes on a decision.** The westerly has been getting up two days; by the first bell the sea is over the crown of the bar at the north end and the whole two hundred yards of gravel is gone under, **and there is nobody on this coast who is surprised by that and nobody who is frightened of it.** Then the volume's spine, untouched: **Alma Gannock, eighty-one, four miles above the north end, on the stool with her back to the water and the chain round her knees, the boy of eight on a board with a slate and a stick of chalk putting something on it and getting it wrong, and she is not correcting him, and the one thing he had put in front of her in six weeks she had said no to in about four minutes and had not asked him for a reason, and she did not know what was under any of it, and nobody on that road was going to tell her — and he had been up the lane before the light and had not gone into the hut, and he was not going to do it on a sixth, and nobody on this coast is owed a page by him for that and nobody has asked for one.** Nine crossings; the chain rests an hour between the fifth and the seventh; about seventy people; a good sixth said twice. **The training wall, and the guild is right about it, said out loud by the man who keeps it.** At the fifth bell Slee walks the wall, and about a hundred and forty yards of it has four or five people on it. At the sixth bell he is about nine yards along it from the inner end, at a driven mark about two foot under the top of the ninth course, with his hand on it, **looking at the water in the deep, which is going down** — the ordinary end of the bar day: the ebb has done its hour and a quarter, the sand has packed, the bar has been a road, and the flood has come back in behind it and the sand is going out of the deep again. **Pell Cawley sees it first** — the boat's carpenter of fifty, thirty-one years at that hard, with a slip of his own a long way inside the gravel and a plane in his hand, standing on a hull — and says *Slee, come here and look at that*, and puts the plane down. **He is named, because he is somebody the reader has met and a person who does work in a chapter may not arrive from nowhere. The first draft gave him a lost slip in a year when a hole went the other way; the slip was Teel's, and Teel is dead, and that is gone.** **Nobody was in it. That is the first thing about it and it is the whole of why it was possible for six people to stand on a wall and look at it.** In the middle of the seaward face, for about eleven yards, the water was not going down the face; it was going out through it, and the surface of the deep was doing what water does when it is pulled through a hole about the size of a doorway, **and a man who works the water says what it means in the trade's mouth: that is the water going out through the middle of it.** And Slee: **it is not the wall failing. Do not anybody say that. The wall is doing what it was built to do and it has been doing it for nineteen years. That is the bed going out from under the bottom course in the middle of eleven yards of it, and I found that yesterday with a lead line in a boat, and I have got it in a book in a shed with the date on it, and there are three people on this coast who knew I went out yesterday and not one of them knew what I went out for.** **Those three are not the three who could act, and this line was rewritten by the first repair pass into four people who had all been told by him, which was a story-level change made as a wording fix, contradicted the batch record, and named four people no chapter identifies. It is reverted, and `state/batch-summary.md` under *Third pass* has the account.** And then the two things he wants said, in the open: **the harm is not the hole. The harm is the gravel. That wall is worth the two feet of gravel behind it and the two feet is the whole of that point, and the wall is not holding the gravel up, it is holding it sideways against the ebb, and where the bed has gone out the ebb comes through the wall now instead of past it, and the first thing it does on the far side is take gravel, and the second is go round the end, and the third is have a wider hole to work on next time, and none of that is a disaster. That is a shore going. It took two winters the last time and it will take less than that this time, because this time the guild is putting stone in a hole instead of taking a wall out, and there is a difference and it is the only good thing anybody can say about it. And the guild was right. I want that said too, and by me, and in the open, and not once in the next year by anybody else.** Then, at about the eighth bell, about four people come off the wall and across the flat, and **by the time they are on the shingle the story has four versions in it and none of them is about the gravel and all four of them are about the hole.** Jory Vance, twenty-two, four years on the counter, writes down what happened while it is happening, and makes the entry standing up at the chain:

> THE SEA STOOD UP OVER THE BAR AT THE NORTH END ON THE SIXTH DAY OF THE EIGHTH TIDE. AT ABOUT THE SEVENTH BELL THE WATER WENT OUT THROUGH THE SEAWARD FACE OF THE NORTH WALL FOR ABOUT ELEVEN YARDS. A MAN OF FIFTY-EIGHT WHO KEEPS THE WALL SAW IT FROM THE HARD. NO BOAT WENT OUT. NOTHING WAS DONE.

**And the batch's one System panel, the sixth thing on this coast that was a record of a thing somebody had entered nowhere in particular, reaching him on the wet shingle at about the ninth bell, rendering the entry and nothing else** — no mechanism, no join, no cause, and it ends on the two things nobody has decided. Then **the forward pull, and it is a link and not a monster. There are three people on this coast who could do something about the eleven yards of it and not one of them can see the other two, and nobody has asked any of them for anything.** Sorrel Gannock at the north end with her boat, who had been in that deep twice that day in a flat-bottomed boat with a man in it and had stood on that wall at the fifth bell and had not been there at the seventh, and who is **not asked and is not going to be told anything about the ninth bell because nobody in that yard is going to think of it.** Bettany Rook a mile and a flight of stairs up the hill, told in about a minute and a half, and asking the only question: *how many feet* — and being told that nobody had measured it in front of anybody — and then: **that is a number somebody is going to want, and there is not one instrument on this coast that will give it to him except a man in a boat… I have wanted to see that number for about a week and I am not going to send a boy up that lane for it, because that is what this coast does to people, and in about a week it would come back to him from a room and it would be the worst thing that has ever happened to him.** And Hollis Thrale two miles along the coast, at a bench, with an order under a batch book with a date and no figure, a pane three years old on his own wall, **and the eighth column of it saying that the gravel stood against the seaward face to the second course, which at that moment was the only figure anybody on this coast had in their head about that wall** — three years old against nineteen years and eight months, and the two of them cannot be put in one room, and the arch behind two feet of brick is cold. Then **the rendering, on nine hundred yards of wet shingle at the ninth bell, at the height and the distance it has been at since the third of the second month in a city four hundred miles behind him** — the boards, the table, the lamp with the wick down, the window with the wrong weather, four people at the table, one of them about forty-four with her back to him — **and the leg goes at the same moment, from the hip down, all of it, and does not come back inside two hundred yards and comes back the rest of the way worse, and he tells nobody then or afterwards, and about nine people on this coast have now seen something happen to him on a shingle or in a boat and not one of them has said a word.** And the decision, in the dark at the near end, four minutes of it, and it is the ending: **he is going to go and ask three people things. Not to tell them. Not to fetch them, and not to carry a sheet to any of them, and not to write one. He has no standing on this coast and has never had one and there is no way on four hundred miles of it to get one, and he has been produced four times on this coast, every one of them by a thing that was simply nearest to him, and he is not going to be produced a fifth.** The three are **a woman of thirty-six in a yard with a boat and a towrope and eleven years of a thing nobody has ever asked her the name of; a man of sixty-one who has been at a fire for forty-one years and has never once been asked; and a woman of fifty-one who said on a shingle four days ago that she would rather be a coast than a woman**, and he does not know what any of them will say, and **his one guess is that at least one of them will say no, and that the no will be right, and that it will be right in the way a thing is right when it is about the person saying it and not about the wall.** And he has no page for it and is not going to write one, because a page with a date on it and that it is his is a thing he has done five times in forty-nine days and every one of the five was a thing he wrote instead of going and standing in a yard. And **there is a fourth road he is not going to go up at all — four miles above the north end, a hut, a woman of eighty-one knitting, a boy of eight with a slate — and he is not going to knock, and that is because of the second of the seventh tide and a page in his coat, and it is the hardest thing in that paragraph and it is not one of the three, and nobody on this coast knows that he worked out where he was going and left that road off.** Close: the ninth crossing goes over, the boy of eleven goes over for the ninth time at a penny, about four people are carrying a story about a boil in a wall and about nine are carrying the same story without the boil in it, Corrie Tallow writes the ninth down and nothing else, and **a man of thirty-one stood at the near end in the dark with his weight on his good side and had not asked anybody for one thing in forty-nine days and was going to ask three of them.**
